# The Making of the Kyrgyz: Name, Language, Genes and History

**How a name first recorded on the upper Yenisei came to belong to a people formed in the Tian Shan, traced through chronicles, archaeology, language, belief, and genomes**

**Edition:** First edition
**Author:** Emil Madraimov · salam@qylym.com
**Date:** November 2026
**License:** This book is licensed CC BY 4.0. See `LICENSING.md` in the repository.
**Cite as:** Emil Madraimov (2026). *The Making of the Kyrgyz: Name, Language, Genes and History*, first edition. Archived with Jany-Latyn: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22844476

---

## Abstract

The ethnonym *Kyrgyz* is recorded from about 200 BCE and is fixed on the upper Yenisei in southern Siberia from the 6th century CE, yet the people who bear it today live some 1,650 kilometres to the south-west, in the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay. This book asks how the name crossed that distance and how many people travelled with it. Acts I and II follow both landscapes from the Bronze Age to the Russian annexation of 1855–76; Act III tests the resulting reconstruction against three independent lines of evidence: language, material culture and belief, and whole genomes. The record supports the staged movement of a political name and its institutions, carried from the Yenisei through the Altai and the Oirat world of western Mongolia between the fall of the Uyghur khaganate in 840 and the 15th century, rather than a mass migration. Persian sources call the Tian Shan people Kyrgyz by 1503–04, two centuries before the Dzungar deportation of 1703 ended the Yenisei polity, whose remaining population passed largely into the Khakas. The Kyrgyz language pairs a phonology and grammar closest to Southern Altai with Kipchak vocabulary; eight genomic estimates from five studies place the formation of the modern Kyrgyz between about 1250 and 1600 CE; and models of Kyrgyz genomes do not require the Cis-Baikal ancestry shared by the Khakas and Southern Altaians, while Kyrgyz men carry almost none of the northern male lineages Q and N. The modern people took shape between the 15th and 18th centuries, as small northern and Altai groups married into resident mountain Turkic tribes, Moghuls, Kipchaks, and Oirats through clans built to adopt outsiders. The synthesis lands nearest to the name-transfer school, modified: the name was not borrowed from afar but carried south by real, repeated small movements. It is older than the people who carry it, and the book argues that this is not a paradox. Five appendices give a regional chronology, a gazetteer, the scholarly reconstructions and historical exonyms, an evidence scorecard, and the source checks that remain open.

---

*Numbers in square brackets refer to the numbered References at the end. Primary sources are cited directly wherever they could be read.*

## Act I: The Mystery and the Northern World

### Chapter 1: The 1,650-Kilometre Puzzle

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 1**
>
> - **A 1,650-kilometre historical puzzle:** The ethnonym *Kyrgyz* is attested from ~200 BCE and firmly anchored on the upper Yenisei in southern Siberia, yet more than five million modern Kyrgyz live 1,656 kilometres south-west in the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay—dividing scholars across staged-migration, political name-transfer, and Tian Shan autochthony schools.
> - **No single archive resolves ethnogenesis alone:** Chronicles track political names, languages shift through contact, archaeology dates material culture rather than speech, runic stones reflect literate elites, and genes can mix without changing a name—requiring all five lines of evidence (chronicles, archaeology, language, material culture and belief, and genomes) to be weighed together.
> - **Three modern heirs show sharp asymmetry:** Tian Shan Kyrgyz stand closest to Southern Altaians by language, whereas Khakas and Southern Altaians share a Yenisei Cis-Baikal genetic component that models of Kyrgyz genomes do not require—and Oirat-descended Sart-Kalmaks on Issyk-Kul show how readily outsiders still fold into Kyrgyz tribal identity.
> - **Russians did not pin the Yenisei name onto Tian Shan nomads:** Persian chronicles call the Tian Shan people Kyrgyz by 1503–04 (written 1547), six decades before Russia met the Yenisei Kyrgyz (1606); Russian scribes instead misapplied *Kirghiz* to the Kazakhs and coined *Kara-Kirghiz* to distinguish the real Kyrgyz.
> - **Political myth-making requires an evidentiary filter:** Soviet autochthonous frameworks and post-Soviet jubilees (such as "Osh-3000" or "2,200 years of Kyrgyz statehood") must be tested against primary sources; the historical narrative closes with Russian annexation in 1855–76, long after Kyrgyz identity, speech, and ancestry were already established.

#### 1.1 Barskoon to Kyzyl

Measure a straight line on the map between two clusters of medieval Turkic runic stones. At the southern end stands Barskoon, on the alpine shore of Lake Issyk-Kul in Kyrgyzstan. At the northern end lies Kyzyl, on the upper Yenisei River in southern Siberia. Roughly 1,650 kilometres of mountain range, desert basin, and steppe separate the two—1,656 kilometres along the great circle.

That distance frames the central mystery of Kyrgyz origins. The ethnonym *Kyrgyz* carries a written history stretching back more than two millennia. Han-dynasty Chinese chronicles already record a northern people under an early form of the name around 200 BCE, whom I. L. Kyzlasov places in southern Siberia [1], and the upper Yenisei is the earliest homeland where the Kyrgyz can be located beyond doubt [2]. Yet more than five million Kyrgyz—about 5.2 million in Kyrgyzstan alone, 73.4% of its roughly 7.04 million people at the start of 2023 by official figures [3] [205]—live far to the south-west, in the valleys and high pastures of the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay mountains.

How the medieval Kyrgyz of the upper Yenisei connect to the modern Kyrgyz of the Tian Shan is the problem historians and anthropologists call *ethnogenesis*. Surveying the debate on the basis of Peter Golden's history of the Turkic peoples, János Sipos finds "no proof of masses of Yenisey Kyrgyz migrating to the Tien-Shan" and concludes that "the problem remains unsettled" [4]. Three broad schools have competed to explain those 1,650 kilometres:

- **Staged migration:** Groups bearing the Kyrgyz name moved south-west from the Yenisei and the Altai into the Tian Shan in waves, absorbing the tribes they encountered along the way. Valikhanov, Radlov, and Karaev placed those moves in the 9th to 13th centuries, whereas K. I. Petrov located the formative core on the upper Yenisei and in the southern Altai among Kipchak-speaking populations [5].
- **Political name transfer:** The label carried dynastic and political weight rather than shared biological descent, allowing populations far from Siberia to adopt it. S. M. Abramzon placed the formation of the Tian Shan Kyrgyz in the eastern Tian Shan during the 14th to 17th centuries on a foundation of older local Turkic tribes, rejecting any mass relocation from the Yenisei [4] [6]. L. R. Kyzlasov likewise treated the name as a political designation borne by an "Inner Asian" group [4], while R. A. Abdumanapov suggests that "Kyrgyz" began as the name of a ruling stratum that spread across the wider population it governed [5].
- **Tian Shan autochthony:** A third position denies that the gap needs bridging at all. Seizing on an eastern-Tian-Shan notice of Jiankun in the *Xin Tangshu* and on the Wusun, Bichurin, Aristov, and Soltonoev placed Kyrgyz in the Tian Shan from antiquity [5]—a reading Chapters 2 and 3 test against the Han bearings.

A separate debate concerns what the name meant in the north itself. Working from Khakas folklore and place names in the Minusinsk Basin, V. Ya. Butanaev argues that on the Yenisei the word *Kyrgyz* operated "not only in a political but also in an ethnic sense" [7], naming a genuine people rather than an aristocratic house alone—an argument about the Yenisei rather than a bridge to the Tian Shan.

*(A compact side-by-side summary of ten major scholarly reconstructions sits in Appendix C.)*

In practice, these positions blur into one another. A political name can travel south with a real group of migrants who remain numerically small beside the mountain population that eventually adopts their identity. Resolving the puzzle therefore turns on timing and proportion, and every discipline brings a distinct blind spot to the table. Written chronicles track *names*, and names jump across populations with startling ease. Languages can spread through trade, alliance, or marriage with minimal gene flow, just as genes can mix thoroughly without altering anyone's language or ethnonym. Archaeology dates graves, ironwork, and pottery, never the tongue spoken by the dead. Stone inscriptions are narrower still: in societies where literacy belonged to a thin political or scribal stratum, a monument records the prestige language of the elite who commissioned it, leaving open what ordinary herders spoke in the valleys around it. That is why the dense runic corpora of the Yenisei, the Altai, and the Tian Shan cannot by themselves settle what language lived on the mountain pastures. Oral family trees (*sanjyra*), household rituals, and the monumental *Manas* epic illuminate something different again: how the Kyrgyz themselves stitched their past into collective memory. Grouped for this book, the evidence falls into five archives: written chronicles, archaeology (inscribed stones included), language, material culture and belief (oral memory included), and genomes.

Tracking those five archives requires keeping three geographical corridors in view from north-east to south-west. Furthest north lie the **Yenisei and Sayan** basins—the fertile steppes of the Minusinsk Basin, the headwaters around Kyzyl in Tuva, and the forested Sayan passes sealing them off from Mongolia. In the middle stretches the **Altai, western Mongolia, and Oirat corridor**, linking the alpine valleys of the Russian and Mongolian Altai to the upper Irtysh steppe, the salt-lake basin of Uvs, Lake Khövsgöl, and the old imperial heartland on the Orkhon River. At the south-western end rises the **Tian Shan, Semirechye, and Fergana** world: the lowland river valleys of the Chüy and Talas, the high alpine basin of Issyk-Kul, the Bedel Pass dropping down toward the Tarim oasis rim, and the snow-fed pastures overlooking Fergana and the Pamir-Alay. *(Readers who want an at-a-glance reference while moving across these landscapes will find a master ten-period regional chronology in Appendix A and a fifteen-place gazetteer in Appendix B.)*

Read together across all five lines of evidence, the record points to a layered answer that fits none of the neatest national myths. The name *Kyrgyz*—accompanied by political titles and small, repeated human movements—travelled south-west by stages from the upper Yenisei through the Altai and the Oirat world of western Mongolia between the fall of the Uyghur khaganate in 840 and the 15th century. Most of the ancestry of the modern Kyrgyz, however, never made that journey from Siberia. Instead, the modern people coalesced in the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay between the 15th and 18th centuries as incoming northern and Altai groups merged with resident mountain Turkic tribes, Moghuls, Kipchaks, and Oirats—leaving behind a striking divergence between name, language, ritual, and DNA. Acts I and II follow that chronological trail across both homelands down to the Russian annexation of 1855–76; Act III then cross-examines the historical reconstruction through three independent forensic lenses—language, material culture and belief, and whole genomes.

#### 1.2 Three Heirs of One Medieval World

Modern political borders split the Tian Shan Kyrgyz across several states, yet the Kyrgyz of Kyrgyzstan, Xinjiang's Kizilsu prefecture, and the Pamir Kyrgyz of Tajikistan, Afghanistan, and Turkey are a single people speaking one language, divided only by 19th- and 20th-century frontier lines. Elsewhere across Inner Asia, however, the name, the language, and the ancestry pull apart in ways that expose the medieval fault lines. The **Khakas** of southern Siberia descend largely from the population of the Yenisei Kyrgyz polity yet do not call themselves Kyrgyz. The **Fuyu Kyrgyz** of north-east China still carry the ethnonym *Kyrgyz*, yet their speech is a dialect of Khakas on a separate branch of the Turkic family [8] [9]. Meanwhile, on the eastern shore of Issyk-Kul, communities of Oirat Mongol descent have largely become Kyrgyz within the past century and a half [10] [11].

To untangle this history, it helps to keep the medieval label distinct from the modern one. Throughout this book, **Yenisei Kyrgyz** refers to the medieval people of the Minusinsk Basin and upper Yenisei—first echoed in Han records under forms such as Gekun and Jiankun, securely fixed on the Yenisei from the 6th-century Türk period onward, victorious over the Uyghur khaganate in 840, and politically active on the river until 1703. Used alone, **Kyrgyz** denotes the modern people of the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay.

Set side by side, three modern peoples—the **Kyrgyz**, the **Khakas**, and the **Southern Altaians** (Altai-kizhi, Telengits, and Teleuts)—represent three diverging outcomes of a single medieval world. The Kyrgyz name moved south, carried by comparatively small groups. The Khakas remained in the Minusinsk Basin and inherited what survived of the Yenisei polity. The Southern Altaians stayed in the mountain corridor between the two. Their three-way relationship is sharply asymmetrical, and that asymmetry is one of the strongest clues in the entire dossier:

- **By language,** Kyrgyz stands closest to Southern Altai—close enough that the Russian linguistic tradition places the two alone in a dedicated Kyrgyz–Kipchak subgroup [136] [137]—while Khakas belongs to a separate South Siberian branch of Turkic altogether [8].
- **By genome,** Khakas and Southern Altaians both carry the distinctive Cis-Baikal ancestry of the ancient Yenisei basin, which every passing model for them includes and models for the Kyrgyz do not require (traced to the Bronze Age in Chapter 2 and examined in Chapter 10)—even though, on the paternal line, Southern Altaians look more like the Kyrgyz than like the Khakas.
- **By ethnonym,** only the Tian Shan Kyrgyz still bear the name as their primary self-designation, though Khakas oral tradition preserves *khyrgys* (Kyrgyz) in folklore and toponymy [7], and the deported Fuyu community in Manchuria has held onto it for nearly three hundred years [9].

If that divergence feels abstract, a living snapshot on the eastern edge of Lake Issyk-Kul shows the human mechanics of identity shift in real time. The **Sart-Kalmaks** are Sunni Muslim Oirats who migrated out of Xinjiang into Kyrgyzstan in four waves—in 1863, 1876, 1884, and 1894 [11]. Little more than a century and a half later, their Western Mongolic Oirat dialect is close to vanishing: in a 2021 field survey, only 20 of 100 respondents still spoke it natively [11]. Official figures reflect the quiet shift of allegiance even more starkly: Kyrgyzstan's 2009 census recorded just 2,805 Sart-Kalmaks, whereas unofficial estimates put the community between 10,000 and 25,000 because so many now register as Kyrgyz [11]. Most Kyrgyz neighbours already regard the Kalmaks as one of the large Kyrgyz lineages [10]. Watching an Oirat-speaking migrant community fold itself into Kyrgyz tribal identity within a century and a half shows, in miniature, how porous steppe ethnonyms have always been.

#### 1.3 Clearing the Naming Fog and Modern Stakes

Before following the story back to the Bronze Age, two modern distortions have to be cleared from the lens: an old suspicion about Russian colonial labels, and a more recent tide of state-sponsored origin myths.

A stubborn rumour holds that Russian officials, having met the Yenisei Kyrgyz in Siberia first, mistakenly pinned their name onto unrelated nomads in the Tian Shan. The archival chronology rules that out completely. Persian sources already call the mountain people of the Tian Shan *Kyrgyz* roughly sixty years before any Russian set foot near the Yenisei. Writing in 1547, the Moghul prince Mirza Haidar Dughlat records events of 1503–04 in which a Moghul prince was installed as ruler of the Kyrgyz [14] [15], and an independent 16th-century Sufi chronicle from the Fergana Valley, the *Majmūʿ al-tawārīkh* (also transliterated *Majmūʿat ul-tawārīkh*), names the Kyrgyz in the same era [16]. Russian colonists did not encounter the Yenisei Kyrgyz until 1606 near Tomsk and 1628 near Krasnoyarsk [17].

Even early Russian bureaucracy never confused the two populations. A Tobolsk record of 1624 distinguishes the southern "Chon Kyrgyz" ("Great Kyrgyz") from the Yenisei Kyrgyz by name, and a 1643 document refers to the "Alat Kyrgyz" around Tokmok and the Ala-Too [17]. Outsiders to the east did use a different name: Oirat rulers and Qing clerks called the Tian Shan Kyrgyz *Burut*—an exonym echoed in Russian reports of 1707, translated explicitly as Kyrgyz by the envoy Ugryumov in 1731–33 [17], and still paired with Kyrgyz in the Qing *Xiyu zhi* of 1763–70 [14]. Surveying the 17th- to 19th-century Russian archives, D. Saparaliyev concludes that *Kyrgyz* was the Tian Shan people's own self-name and *Burut* a label imposed from outside [17]. The 1911 *Encyclopaedia Britannica* agreed, stating that "the distinctive national name Kirghiz" properly belonged to the mountain Kyrgyz alone while Chinese and Mongols knew them as *Burut* [18]. *(The full ten-source timeline of historical ethnonyms and exonyms is compiled in Appendix C.)*

The real Russian naming blunder ran in the opposite direction—onto the Kazakhs. The Kazakhs never called themselves Kyrgyz. Russian scribes initially termed them the "Kazak Horde" in 1618, then drifted during the 18th century into calling them "Kirghiz," "Kirgiz-Kazaks," and "Kirgiz-Kaisaks," the form Rychkov used in 1771 [17] [18]. Saparaliyev finds no archival explanation for the swap and classifies it as an outsiders' invention [17]; the 1911 *Britannica* attributed the compound to an 18th-century bureaucratic desire "mainly to distinguish" the steppe nomads from Russia's own Cossacks (*kazak*) [18]. Having reassigned *Kirghiz* to the Kazakhs, tsarist clerks then had to coin a new label to identify the actual Kyrgyz again—calling them "Kara-Kirghiz" (as in Andreev's 1795 "Kara" or "beyond-the-mountain" Kirghiz [17]) or "wild-rock" (*dikokamennye*) Kirghiz, a misnomer that Denis Sinor observes existed purely "to distinguish them from the Kazakhs" [2] [18]. Whatever else remains contested about Kyrgyz origins, Russians did not invent the Tian Shan name; the puzzle is how that name reached the Tian Shan by the 15th century, and how many people travelled alongside it.

Modern politics adds a second layer of fog. Origin stories are high-value assets for states, and political utility rarely aligns with evidentiary strength. Rather than speculating about the personal motives of individual historians, a cleaner discipline is to apply a single stress test wherever a sweeping claim enters the debate: *if true, would it conveniently prop up a state founding narrative, and is the primary evidence solid enough to hold it?*

Post-Soviet Kyrgyzstan supplies vivid examples. Political scientist Erica Marat highlights three state-driven identity campaigns: President Akayev's 2003 celebration of "2,200 years of Kyrgyz statehood," supported by his own book tying ancient statehood to the *Manas* epic; the 1995 *Manas* millennium—staged four months before a presidential election—from which a state committee distilled seven official national maxims; and the 2000 "Osh-3000" jubilee, sharply criticized by Kyrgyz scholars including Salijan Jigitov [19]. The Osh jubilee illustrates why the claim matters more than the ceremony. Settlement on the Osh site is genuinely three millennia old, and celebrating an ancient city is entirely sound. Yet the Fergana Valley spoke Iranian languages, then Karluk Turkic, then Chagatai; Kyrgyz groups arrived along its rim only from the 16th century, and in strength during the 17th and 18th. Treating ancient Osh as a 3,000-year-old *Kyrgyz* city asks archaeology to ratify a timeline the sources contradict.

Earlier empires exerted parallel pulls. Soviet historical writing rewarded *autochthonous* theories—arguing that each republic's titular nationality had inhabited its modern territory since deep antiquity—and ingrained a habit of treating ethnic groups as timeless, fixed biological units that outlived the USSR itself [19]. Even rigorous scholarship occasionally intersects with those currents: V. M. Ploskikh, cited later in this book among advocates of an Altai formative stage, is noted by Marat among historians who cast the nation in ancient, homegrown terms [19]. Recognizing that political backdrop does not discredit the Altai hypothesis—archaeology and linguistics support an Altai stage on independent grounds—but it reminds a reader where caution belongs.

That same boundary explains why the historical narrative in Acts I and II closes in 1876. Russian annexation arrived in two main steps: the northern Bugu tribe submitted in 1855, and Russia absorbed the Khanate of Kokand—and the southern Kyrgyz with it—in 1875–76 [2] [20]. By then, the name, the language, and the gene pool were already firmly anchored in the Tian Shan, centuries after Mirza Haidar wrote in 1547 and long after Tobolsk clerks distinguished the "Chon Kyrgyz" in 1624 [14] [15] [17]. The 1924–36 national-territorial delimitation did transform fluid tribal and confederal loyalties across Central Asia into bounded modern republics with fixed frontiers, standardized literary languages, census boxes, and official genealogical registers [2] [21]. The tsarist naming mix-up even survived into early Soviet usage: from 1920 to 1925 the Kazakh autonomous republic was the "Kirghiz ASSR," a name the Kyrgyz republic itself then bore from 1926 to 1936 [2] [21]. Yet drawing administrative borders did not conjure the peoples beneath them out of thin air. As the linguistic, ethnographic, and genomic tests in Act III demonstrate, the Kyrgyz, Kazakhs, and Karakalpaks already stood apart in speech, paternal ancestry, and political history long before 1924. Mapping modern nation-states is a 20th-century administrative story; tracing how an ancient name from the upper Yenisei came to live in the Tian Shan requires stepping back five thousand years, to two landscapes that existed long before either bore the name Kyrgyz.

### Chapter 2: Two Landscapes Before the Name

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 2**
>
> - **Western steppe lineages took root early in southern Siberia:** Starting with Yamnaya-identical Afanasievo herders (~3000 BCE) and the Sintashta–Andronovo wave (~2000–1400 BCE, 68% R1a-Z93), steppe paternal ancestry embedded itself across the Minusinsk Basin and Altai, persisting through the Iron Age Tagar and Tashtyk (Oglakhty) cultures even as East Asian admixture climbed from the Late Bronze Age onward.
> - **Cis-Baikal ancestry provides a sharp northern genetic fingerprint:** A distinct Bronze Age Cis-Baikal ancestry tracks early Yeniseian speakers in southern Siberia and survives today only among Kets, Samoyeds, and South Siberian Turkic peoples—matching linguistic evidence of Turkic–Yeniseian intermarriage in kinship vocabulary.
> - **The Tian Shan was densely populated long before any Kyrgyz appear there:** Iranian-speaking Saka (600–200 BCE, carrying rising southern oasis ancestry) were followed by the pastoral Wusun confederacy, recorded in Chinese annals until 436–437 CE—whose capital *Chigu* ("Red Valley") bears only a coincidental phonetic resemblance to the later *Chigils*.
> - **Turkic governance and religious pluralism predated the Kyrgyz by centuries:** By the 5th–8th centuries, Sogdian trade hubs such as Suyab (Ak-Beshim—hosting Buddhist, Christian, and Zoroastrian shrines), Western Türk confederations, Issyk-Kul **Chigils**, and Chüy-valley **Karluks** (from 766) already structured the Tian Shan.
> - **East–West Eurasian admixture began well before the Mongol era:** Xiongnu and Amur-basin eastern lineages moved into the steppe and Semirechye from the late first millennium BCE onward, matching the earliest East–West admixture wave (~2,225 years ago) still visible in modern Kyrgyz genomes.

Neither end of the 1,650-kilometre corridor stood empty when Turkic speakers entered it. Southern Siberia held Yeniseian- and Samoyedic-speaking communities alongside a long succession of Bronze and Iron Age herding cultures, several of them carrying western steppe ancestry derived from a mixture of Eastern Hunter-Gatherers (EHG) and Caucasus hunter-gatherers (CHG) [22] [23] [24]. Far to the south-west, the Tian Shan was already home to Iranian-speaking Saka nomads, the Wusun confederacy, and Sogdian merchant towns—and it came under Turkic rule centuries before any chronicle mentions Kyrgyz in those valleys. Because each landscape accumulated its own distinctive genetic, linguistic, and material signatures, mapping both baselines before the Kyrgyz name takes centre stage provides the forensic yardstick for every chapter that follows.

#### 2.1 Southern Siberia

Pastoralism arrived in the Minusinsk Basin and the Altai around 3000 BCE with the **Afanasievo** culture. Genetically, these first herders cannot be separated from the **Yamnaya** pastoralists of the Pontic-Caspian steppe north of the Black and Caspian seas: in statistical tests of shared genetic variants (*D-statistics*), the two behave as a single population [25]. Later genomic work describes them as "the same genetically homogenous population," whose Afanasievo founders trekked roughly 3,000 kilometres eastward across the central steppe [23]. Their shared ancestry was split roughly evenly between western-steppe Eastern Hunter-Gatherers—themselves partly linked to Upper Palaeolithic Siberians—and populations related to Caucasus hunter-gatherers [22] [23] [24].

The **Okunevo** culture that replaced Afanasievo in the Minusinsk Basin drew primarily on a local population related to Ancient North Eurasians [25], retaining only 10–20% Yamnaya–Afanasievo admixture [23]. A second major eastward surge followed between roughly 2000 and 1400 BCE with **Sintashta-** and **Andronovo-related** herders. In *D-statistics*, Andronovo behaves as an eastward extension of Sintashta, and both carry more shared genetic variants (*alleles*) with early European farmers than Yamnaya or Afanasievo populations do [25], adding a European farmer stratum to their EHG and CHG steppe ancestry [23] [26]. On the paternal line, 68% of their men carried the **Z93** branch of haplogroup **R1a** [26]. From the close of the Bronze Age onward, the **Karasuk** and subsequent cultures of the region grew increasingly admixed, taking on a rising share of East Asian ancestry [25]. Yet male lineages from the steppe endured: a study by Keyser and colleagues of 26 individuals from the Krasnoyarsk area, spanning the mid-2nd millennium BCE to the 4th century CE, found **R1a1** in nearly every man sampled [27].

Sharing this southern Siberian world with the steppe herders were speakers of **Yeniseian** languages. A 2025 paleogenomic study by Zeng and colleagues identifies a specific Bronze Age ancestry from the **Cis-Baikal** region as "a genetic tracer-dye for the spread of early Yeniseian language speakers," matching river-name reconstructions that place the Yeniseian homeland between Cis-Baikal and the upper Yenisei [28]. That homeland remains contested: working from hydronyms and a proposed link with Burushaski, Václav Blažek places pre-dispersal Yeniseian far to the south, toward the northern slopes of the Tian Shan and the Pamirs [29]. Where the two river-name hypotheses point in opposite directions, the one that ancient genomes independently corroborate carries the firmer weight—though genes alone cannot fix where a language was spoken. Two Late Bronze Age individuals from the Minusinsk Basin, dated roughly 3,000–2,900 years ago and originally published as Karasuk [25], carry 85–95% of this Cis-Baikal ancestry; the 2025 study reassigns them to the **Lugavskaya** culture [28].

Today, appreciable levels of Cis-Baikal ancestry survive only among Kets, Samoyeds, and South Siberian Turkic peoples—a distribution Zeng and colleagues tie directly to historical and ethnolinguistic accounts of Turkic speakers absorbing Yeniseian populations, "beginning with the arrival of the Yenisei Kyrgyz in the 6th century CE and lasting to early modern times" [28]. *(That 6th-century threshold tracks the Turkic-speaking Kyrgyz of Tang records; how they relate to the much earlier Han-era Gekun and Jiankun is a separate puzzle taken up in Chapter 3.)* Independent linguistic evidence records the same long cohabitation. Examining Yeniseian kinship vocabulary, Bayarma Khabtagaeva finds that "only Siberian Turkic had direct linguistic contacts with Yeniseian," unfolding in two distinct strata—Yenisey Turkic and Altay Turkic. Crucially, while "the terminology of kinship by blood is of Yeniseian origin," words for relatives by marriage in Kott, Arin, and Pumpokol were borrowed from Turkic during a later phase, "indicative of the practice of intermarriage between Yeniseian and Turkic people" [30].

During the Iron Age of the first millennium BCE, the Minusinsk Basin, Tuva, and the Altai hosted the eastern Scythian cultural complex: **Tagar** in the Minusinsk Basin, **Aldy-Bel** in Tuva, and **Pazyryk** in the Altai. Ancient genomes across eastern and western Scythians resolve as "a mixture of Yamnaya-related ancestry and an East Asian component," with demographic modelling favouring separate regional origins connected by ongoing gene flow [31]. A time transect of 111 ancient individuals from the Kazakh steppe pins the formation of the eastern Scythian gene pool to roughly 1500–1000 BCE, blending the Bronze Age steppe layer with an eastern source related to herders around Lake Khövsgöl in northern Mongolia [32]. The Aldy-Bel burials at **Arzhan 2** in Tuva—among the earliest Scythian graves anywhere—belong to this same cluster, whose origin the authors place in the Altai [32]. In the Minusinsk Basin, the **Tashtyk** culture succeeded the Tagar around the turn of the era. The earliest nuclear genomes sequenced from the Tashtyk cemetery at **Oglakhty** (early centuries CE, still available as a Research Square preprint) cluster tightly with the preceding Tagar population, and the male sampled there carried haplogroup **R1a1a** [33]. What tongue any of these Bronze and Iron Age communities spoke remains beyond recovery.

#### 2.2 The Tian Shan

At the southern end of the corridor, the Iron Age belonged to the Iranian-speaking **Saka**. Their most celebrated monument is the **Issyk kurgan**—the burial of the "Golden Man," lying roughly 50 kilometres east of Almaty in Kazakhstan rather than in Kyrgyzstan's Issyk-Kul basin despite the shared name—which yielded a silver cup bearing what its excavator called the oldest written monument in Central Asia [34]. Ancient genomes from Saka burials across the Tian Shan, dated between roughly 600 and 200 BCE, fall along a genetic gradient between the eastern Scythian pool and an Iranian-related ancestry best matched by Bronze Age oasis farmers of southern Central Asia. That southern oasis share rises from about 13% around 600 BCE to roughly 20% by 400 BCE, and at **Alai Nura** in the Tian Shan the same genomic profile persisted into the 3rd century CE [32].

After the Saka came the **Wusun**, who moved into Semirechye around 160 BCE [35]. The *Hanshu* (*Book of Han*) sketches a formidable pastoral state governed from the walled centre of **Chigu**, 8,900 *li* (around 3,700 kilometres) west of the Han capital at Chang'an, encompassing 120,000 households, 630,000 people, and 188,800 fighting men [36]. The chroniclers note that they "do not farm or plant trees; they follow their herds to water and grass, and share the customs of the Xiongnu" [36]. Their spoken language is unknown, though the 19th- and early-20th-century historians Aristov and Soltonoev cast them as early ancestors of the Kyrgyz [5].

Chinese dynastic histories follow the Wusun well past the Han era. According to the *Weishu* (*Book of Wei*, juan 102, echoed in the *Beishi*, juan 97), Wusun statehood endured into the 5th century CE before pressure from the **Rouran** (*Juan-juan*) pushed them deeper into the **Congling** (Pamir and Tian Shan) mountains [37]. Their final recorded diplomatic exchange with a Chinese court took place in **436–437 CE**, when Northern Wei envoys reached the Wusun and Wusun delegates answered at the Wei court—with Yu Taishan noting a minor chronological discrepancy between chapters 4 and 102 of the *Weishu* over the exact mission dates [37]. After 437 CE, the Wusun drop from the Chinese annals altogether. Their capital's name, **Chigu** 赤谷, means "Red Valley" in Chinese and is placed by Kazakhstan's Institute of History and Ethnology on the shore of Lake Issyk-Kul [35]—the same basin held from the 7th century by the Turkic **Chigils** [38]. Because *Chigu* is a Chinese descriptive translation rather than a transcription of a local name, its phonetic echo of *Chigil* is coincidence rather than evidence of ethnic descent.

Within decades of the Wusun vanishing from the annals, Sogdian commerce and early Turkic power reshaped the northern Tian Shan valleys. Gerard Clauson identified the ruins of **Ak-Beshim**, near modern Tokmok in the Chüy valley, as medieval **Suyab** [39]. Founded as a trading post—probably Sogdian—in the 5th or 6th century, Suyab stood by the mid-7th century along the border between the two confederations of the **Western Türks**, and a Tang Chinese protector-general subsequently enclosed the town with walls [39]. Among the Turkic tribes living across Semirechye and the eastern Tian Shan in those centuries were the **Chuyue** 處月, whom Peter Golden and Yu. A. Zuev identify with the later **Chigils** of Issyk-Kul [38] [40]. Around **766**, the **Karluks** seized Suyab and governed the Chüy valley until the Karakhanid dynasty emerged in the mid-10th century [39]. What archaeologists pulled from the ground at Ak-Beshim captures the cosmopolitan texture of this pre-Kyrgyz Tian Shan: a Buddhist temple burned in the late 8th century, an 8th-century church yielding bronze pectoral crosses, and a Zoroastrian platform built for exposing the dead [39]. By the 8th century, the Chüy corridor was already Turkic-ruled, commercially Sogdian, and religiously plural.

Genetic shifts across the steppe ran parallel to these political turnovers. Scythian-era populations mixed with eastern nomads of the **Xiongnu** confederation and were "further admixed with East Asian groups during several short-term khanates in the Medieval period" [41]—a trajectory echoed in the chronicles, which trace the Chuyue of the Tian Shan to late Xiongnu (**Yueban**) and Western Türk groups [38] [40]. Across the Kazakh steppe, a distinct eastern ancestry related to Neolithic populations of the **Amur basin** in the Russian Far East first enters in the second half of the first millennium BCE and spreads widely across the north-east during the **Xianbei–Hun** period around the turn of the era; medieval burials at **Karakaba** in the Kazakh Altai (c. 830 CE) and **Kayalyk** in Semirechye (c. 950 CE) still carry it [32]. Modern Kyrgyz genomes keep a faint imprint of the very start of that east–west convergence: Chen and colleagues model an initial East–West Eurasian admixture roughly **2,225 years ago**, "likely reflecting west–east contacts during the period of the Warring States and the Qin Dynasty" [42].

Both baselines are now in view: a southern Siberian world where R1a-rich steppe herders met and intermarried with Cis-Baikal Yeniseian speakers, and a Tian Shan world where Iranian Saka and Wusun pastures gave way to Sogdian towns, Chigils, and Karluks. With those two landscapes set, the name *Kyrgyz* itself enters the historical record—first as a distant line in Han chronicles, and then as a formidable power on the upper Yenisei.

### Chapter 3: The Northern Power — The Yenisei Kyrgyz to 840

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 3**
>
> - **Early Han records place the name far to the north:** *Shiji* and *Hanshu* record Gekun and Jiankun around 200–50 BCE roughly 5,000 *li* north of Turfan, followed by six centuries of sparse notices bridged by Tashtyk cremation burials and Tang memories of a Jiankun–Dingling fusion.
> - **Türk and Uyghur vassalage forged a northern state:** By the 550s, the Kyrgyz were anchored on the upper Yenisei under Türk, Xueyantuo, and then Uyghur (758–840) overlordship—marrying into Türk, Türgesh, and Karluk ruling houses, trading iron (*jiasha*) and Arab brocade, and leaving the contested Sudzhi inscription in Mongolia.
> - **Medieval Yenisei Kyrgyz differed in appearance and speech from modern Kyrgyz:** Tang annals describe tall, fair, red-haired, green-eyed agro-pastoralists (matching ancient Krasnoyarsk genetics before East Asian ancestry rose across the Altai–Yenisei), who carved literary *d*-type Old Turkic runes while speaking a *z*-type vernacular closer to Khakas than to Kipchak Kyrgyz.
> - **Cremation forms the sharpest northern archaeological tracer:** Inherited from Tashtyk crypts and attested in Tang annals and Gardizi (c. 1050), cremation marked Yenisei Kyrgyz burials across more than six centuries.
> - **Victory in 840 did not move the capital to the Orkhon:** Guided by the Uyghur defector Julu Mohe after civil strife and the probable 839–40 *dzud*, Kyrgyz forces burned the Uyghur golden tent—yet shifted their court south of Mount Lao (generally Tannu-Ola), fifteen days' ride short of the Orkhon heartland.

The Yenisei Kyrgyz are the earliest population securely known by the Kyrgyz name. Chinese dynastic histories carry forms of the ethnonym back to the Han era and anchor its bearers firmly on the upper Yenisei from the 6th-century Türk period onward. Across three centuries under Türk, Xueyantuo, and Uyghur overlords, they combined herding with grain farming, forged prized iron weaponry, cut Old Turkic runic inscriptions on stone, and finally, in 840, broke the Uyghur khaganate on the Mongolian steppe.

#### 3.1 Han Echoes and Six Centuries of Silence

The name enters history in the shadows of the Xiongnu empire. Sima Qian's *Shiji* (c. 90 BCE) records that around 200 BCE the Xiongnu ruler Modu "subjugated in the north the states of the Hunyu, Qushe, Dingling, Gekun 鬲昆 and Xinli" [43]. The *Hanshu* repeats the list—writing the name 隔昆—and supplies the first geographical bearings [36]. In the mid-1st century BCE, the Xiongnu pretender Zhizhi marched west, "defeated Jiankun 堅昆 in the west and subdued the Dingling in the north," and established his seat at Jiankun, which the chronicle puts 7,000 *li* west of the Xiongnu court and 5,000 *li* north of Jushi in the Turfan basin [36]. Even as round estimates, those distances place Jiankun deep in the north, well beyond the Tian Shan. I. L. Kyzlasov equates these Han-era peoples directly with the population of the Yenisei [1]. D. G. Savinov—who refers to them as the Gyangun—is more guarded: while a placement in north-western Mongolia (first floated by Bartold and developed by Kiselev and L. R. Kyzlasov) cannot be ruled out, the only firm fix places the Gyangun south of the Dinlin (Dingling) somewhere in the Sayan-Altai highlands [44].

A millennium later, the 11th-century *New Book of Tang* (*Xin Tangshu*) stitched the old Han names directly onto the medieval Yenisei state: "The Xiajiasi are the ancient state of Jiankun 堅昆" [45]. It adds that they had also been known as Juwu 居勿 and Jiegu 結骨—later Hegu 紇骨 or Hegusi 紇扢斯—that "their stock was mixed with the Dingling," that they had formed "the western frontier of the Xiongnu," and that Jiankun "was originally a powerful state, with territory equal to the Türks'" [45].

Yet the Tang compilers introduced a geographic contradiction that still echoes through modern historiography. In one passage, the *Xin Tangshu* places ancient Jiankun "west of Yiwu, north of Yanqi, beside the White Mountains"—that is, west of Hami and north of Karashahr along the eastern Tian Shan. A few lines away, however, it locates the Tang-era Kyrgyz 3,000 *li* northwest of the Uyghur court with the Tanman mountains to their south, and describes their main river, the Jian, running northeast across the country before emptying "north into the sea"—an unmistakable match for the Yenisei [45]. Nineteenth- and early-twentieth-century advocates of an ancient Tian Shan Kyrgyz homeland, notably Bichurin and Aristov, seized on that eastern-Tian-Shan notice [5], even though it directly contradicts the Han-period *Hanshu* placing Jiankun 5,000 *li* north of Turfan [36].

What the ethnonym itself meant remains unresolved, split between two rival linguistic families. One links the opening syllable to Turkic *kyrk* ("forty"). S. M. Abramzon, following Ligeti, read it as *kyrk* joined to a Mongolic plural ending—naming a federation of forty tribes of diverse descent [46]. Popular memory reshaped the same root into *kyrk kyz* ("forty maidens"), a folk etymology with a 14th-century cousin in the *Yuan shi*, which derived the name from forty women of Han territory who married local men [47]. The rival school looks elsewhere in the lexicon: Peter Golden derives the name from Old Turkic *qïr* (a horse colour, "grey" or "roan") plus a suffix, reconstructing an early form *\*Qïrqïŕ* ending in the *r*-like consonant (*-ŕ*) retained in the Oghuric branch of Turkic that later shifted regularly to *-z* in Common Turkic [38].

Early Chinese transcriptions place a strict phonetic hurdle in front of every proposal. The Han forms Gekun 鬲昆 and Jiankun 堅昆 end in *-n* [36] [43]; trisyllabic transcriptions matching a final Turkic *-z* (Hegusi 紇扢斯 and Xiajiasi 黠戛斯) appear only in the Tang histories [45]. On the face of the characters, the *-z* that makes *Kyrgyz* look unmistakably Turkic is a late arrival. That phonetic shift tracks a deeper chronological gap. The ethnonym is already attested around 200 BCE—and Savinov treats it as established by the mid-2nd century CE on the evidence of the *Wei shu* [44]—whereas secure evidence for Turkic speech on the Yenisei emerges centuries later, and Bayesian phylolinguistics dates the break-up of Common Turkic to roughly the 5th century CE [13]. Savinov accordingly treats the assumption that the early Gyangun already spoke Turkic as a hypothesis of Kiselev and L. R. Kyzlasov rather than a settled fact, arguing instead that the Yenisei Kyrgyz formed through a merger of the Gyangun and the Dinlin [44]. If the earliest bearers of the name did not yet speak Turkic, Han scribes were rendering a word from another family—Yeniseian being an obvious regional candidate, since Yeniseian river names extend south into the Abakan basin [28]—and the 8th-century *qırqız* would represent a later Turkic adaptation. No non-Turkic original has ever been convincingly reconstructed, yet every Turkic etymology rests on the unproven assumption that the name was already Turkic in 200 BCE.

Two further clues complicate the picture, pulling back toward a Turkic—or at least southern—origin. Working independently from the same Han transcriptions, Edwin Pulleyblank reconstructed *\*Qïrqur* in 1962 and revised it to *\*Qïrqïr* in 2002; Václav Blažek observes that this answers directly to Proto-Turkic *\*Qïrqïŕ* [29], matching Golden's independent reconstruction [38] while still placing the *-z* shift late. Meanwhile, a Yeniseian oral tradition recorded among the Kets by Anučin in 1914 recalls ancestors retreating northward before two incoming southern peoples: the *Týstad* ("mountain or stony people," tentatively linked by Vajda to Indo-European or Iranian speakers) and the *Kiliki*, whom Vajda identifies with the early Siberian Kyrgyz [29]. Blažek reads *Kiliki* as reflecting Turkic *qïrq* ("forty") without a plural suffix [29]. The Ket memory yields no Yeniseian etymology, yet it preserves an outsider's witness from the northern forest: to Yeniseian speakers, the Kyrgyz were newcomers pushing in from the south.

Between Zhizhi's campaign in the mid-1st century BCE and the rise of the Türks in the 550s CE stretch six centuries of near-total textual silence. Aside from the *Zhou shu*'s later placement of the Qigu between the Afu and Jian rivers and Mukan Qaghan's conquest in the 550s [48], only two brief notices fall in the gap. The *Wei shu*, cited by Savinov, lists the Hegu among peoples submitting to the Xianbei ruler Tanshihuai in the mid-2nd century CE [44]. A century later, the *Weilüe* (composed between 239 and 265 CE largely from Later Han material) devotes a short entry to Jiankun: a pastoral people northwest of Kangju mustering 30,000 fighting men, rich in sables and fine horses [49]. Repeating the *Hanshu* bearings to the Xiongnu court and Jushi, its translator J. E. Hill tentatively locates Jiankun near Omsk [49]. Neither notice puts the name on the Yenisei.

Two inferential bridges span those six silent centuries. The first is the company the Gekun and Jiankun kept in Chinese lists. The Dingling appear beside them in Modu's northern conquests and fell alongside them to Zhizhi [36] [43], and the Tang historians state plainly that the Kyrgyz "stock was mixed with the Dingling" [45]. Building on that notice, Kiselev traced the medieval Yenisei Kyrgyz to a fusion of the Dinlin with their southern neighbours, the Gyangun [44]—suggesting that the people whom the Türks met in the 550s were forged out of Jiankun and neighbouring populations during the silent centuries, a merger that could easily have carried Turkic speech to the upper Yenisei without any chronicled conquest.

The second bridge lies in the ground. Across the Minusinsk Basin, the **Tashtyk** archaeological culture fills much of the interval. Teploukhov dated it from roughly the turn of the era to the 3rd or 4th century CE and Kiselev from the 1st century BCE to the 4th CE, while Savinov pushes its upper boundary "no earlier than the mid-6th century"—bringing it straight up to the Türk annexation [44]. Savinov observes that Tashtyk, "especially in its later — Tepsey? — stage, could be called early Kyrgyz," and cites L. R. Kyzlasov's finding that the early stone-pillar tombs (*chaatas*) of the Kyrgyz era "are always located at the sites of old Tashtyk burial grounds" [44]. What binds Tashtyk most tightly to the medieval Yenisei Kyrgyz is how they treated their dead. Savinov identifies **cremation** as "the main ethno-differentiating feature of Yenisei Kyrgyz burials," inherited directly from Tashtyk crypts [44]. From the early centuries CE through the *Xin Tangshu*'s account of Kyrgyz burning their dead and interring the bones a year later [45] to the Persian chronicler Gardizi still recording the custom around 1050 [50], cremation marked Yenisei Kyrgyz burials across more than six centuries—forming a physical trail whose eventual disappearance further south-west becomes one of the decisive clues in Chapters 4 and 5.

One widely cited linguistic shortcut through this period fails closer scrutiny. In 1962 Pulleyblank proposed that certain Xiongnu words preserved in Chinese texts were Yeniseian, and Vovin later expanded the argument by reading a short Chinese-transcribed Jié-language couplet as Yeniseian (closer to Kott than to Ket), leading Blažek to place Yeniseian speakers inside the Xiongnu confederacy on the open steppe [29]. Savelyev and Jeong have dismantled that reading [51]. Of the four words in the Jié couplet, only two can even tentatively be linked to the Yeniseian Pumpokol language, and both rely on grammatical endings (*morphemes*) "not actually attested in Pumpokol," while the remaining two force Vovin and his co-authors to posit loans from an unknown third tongue—"This makes the whole reading quite doubtful" [51]. Finding Pulleyblank's Xiongnu–Yeniseian etymologies largely unpersuasive or explicable as shared steppe vocabulary, Savelyev and Jeong note that ancient Xiongnu genomes align with the linguistic verdict: Kets lack the Iranian-related ancestry of Iron Age steppe populations and cluster instead with Samoyedic neighbours, leaving "evidence for a Yeniseian affiliation of the Xiongnu, or a part of them" unconvincing [51]. Notably, that genetic rebuttal relies on modern Kets as a proxy for historical Yeniseian speakers—the same logic used in Chapter 2.1 to treat Cis-Baikal ancestry as a Yeniseian tracer [28]—and applied consistently, it keeps early Yeniseian speakers north in the forest-steppe zone rather than deep inside the Xiongnu steppe.

Strip away speculation, and what remains of the early centuries is spare but sturdy: a northern ethnonym attested from roughly 200 BCE, unanchored to the Yenisei until the 6th century CE, by which time its rulers likely spoke Turkic and practised a cremation rite that endured for another six hundred years—most plausibly emerging out of the Jiankun–Dingling fusion remembered by Tang chroniclers.

#### 3.2 Vassals of the Steppe Empires (550s–840)

With the rise of the First Türk Khaganate in the mid-6th century, the Yenisei Kyrgyz step fully into dated history. The *Book of Zhou* (*Zhoushu*, completed in 636) records that Mukan Qaghan "in the west defeated the Hephthalites, in the east drove off the Khitan, in the north annexed the Qigu [契骨], and overawed the states beyond the frontier" [48]. Remarkably, the same chronicle preserves a Türk origin myth in which one of the four sons of the founding ancestor Nishidu "founded a state between the Afu and Jian rivers, called Qigu" [48]—grafting the Kyrgyz ruling house directly into the Türks' own royal genealogy.

Over the next century, the Kyrgyz navigated shifting steppe overlords. The *Xin Tangshu* notes that they were "at first subject to the Xueyantuo," who stationed an overseer in their state during the brief Xueyantuo ascendancy from 629 until the Tang broke them in the mid-640s [45]. Türk rulers married their daughters to Kyrgyz chiefs, and Kyrgyz iron weapons were "often sent to the Türks" [45]. In **648**, as the Tiele tribes submitted to Chang'an, Kyrgyz envoys arrived at the Tang court, and the emperor designated their country the **Jiankun prefecture**; two further embassies followed under Emperor Gaozong (649–683) [45].

The uneasy alliance snapped under the Second Türk Khaganate. In **710 or 711**—dated to 710 by Erhan Aydın—the inscriptions of Kül Tegin, Bilge Qaghan, and Tonyukuk recount a daring winter strike across the snowbound Kögmen (Sayan) mountains [52]. The Türks "attacked the Kyrgyz people while they slept" and met the Kyrgyz ruler in battle at Songa mountain [52]. That ruler, **Bars Beg**, had married a daughter of the Türk qaghan Ilteriş and held the title qaghan himself; in the battle, as A. Mokeev summarizes the disaster, "Bars Beg was killed, and the Kyrgyz lost their qaghan and their independence" [53]. On the Tonyukuk stone, the ethnonym appears in Old Turkic runes as *kırkız* [54].

Even after that defeat, the Kyrgyz maintained diplomatic reach toward China, sending an embassy in 707–710 on the eve of the war and four more under Emperor Xuanzong (712–756) [45]. That corridor slammed shut when the **Uyghur Khaganate** rose on the Orkhon River in **744** [55]. During the Qianyuan era (**758–760**), Uyghur forces defeated the Kyrgyz and sealed off their access to the Tang court [45]. The Kyrgyz ruler—who carried the indigenous title **A-re 阿熱** (*ajo*)—was forced to accept a subordinate Uyghur title as well [45].

Cut off from China, the Kyrgyz pivoted west and south. The *Xin Tangshu* reports that they "often relied on the Arabs, the Tibetans and the Karluks": Tibetan caravans wary of Uyghur raiders waited inside Karluk territory for a Kyrgyz armed escort, and every three years Arab merchants dispatched a heavy brocade to the Kyrgyz court, sliced into twenty separate bolts because a single uncut cloth would have required twenty camels to carry [45].

Those western ties became dynastic when Uyghur authority began to fray. Seizing the opening around **820**, the A-re proclaimed himself qaghan. His mother was a **Türgesh** and his wife a daughter of the **Karluk** ruler (*yabghu*) [45]—marriages linking the Yenisei royal house directly to the ruling confederations of Semirechye, whose names later resurface among modern Kyrgyz tribal lineages (*uruu*) [6]. A punitive expedition led by a Uyghur minister failed, locking the two powers into roughly twenty years of inconclusive warfare, during which the A-re sent taunts warning the Uyghur khan that his fortune was spent and the Kyrgyz would soon take his golden tent [45].

One enigmatic stone monument in Mongolia sits directly across this threshold: the **Sudzhi (Suji) inscription**, whose author places himself "in the Uyghur land" under a khan of the **Yaglakar** (the Uyghur ruling clan) and introduces himself as the "son of a Kyrgyz" [56] [57]. Scholars have bounced the stone back and forth between Kyrgyz and Uyghur ownership—and its dating splits cleanly into two rival camps tied to that attribution:

- **Uyghur-era dating (late 8th to early 9th century):** G. J. Ramstedt originally assigned the stone to the end of the 8th century, and Hatice Şirin (2022)—alongside a proposal identifying the author with **Işbaras Seŋün Yaglakar**, a commander named in the Taryat inscription of about 753—places it squarely inside the Uyghur Khaganate (744–840) [57].
- **Post-840 dating (mid-9th century):** S. G. Klyashtorny, reading *yaglaqar xan ata* as a Kyrgyz commander who drove out or defeated the Uyghurs, dated the monument immediately after 840 [56].

On the Uyghur-era reading, a Yaglakar sovereign and an officer attested in a c. 753 Uyghur text point to a Kyrgyz aristocrat building a career inside the Uyghur realm before its fall, rather than evidence of a post-840 Kyrgyz occupation of Mongolia; Klyashtorny's post-840 reading keeps the stone's precise chronology contested.

#### 3.3 What the Tang Saw (and Heard)

Through envoys and frontier intelligence, the compilers of the *Xin Tangshu* assembled a striking portrait of the Yenisei Kyrgyz at the height of their northern power. They counted "several hundred thousand" people and 80,000 fighting men [45]. Physically, the Chinese observers found them unforgettable: "all tall and big, with red hair, fair faces and green eyes; black hair is considered unlucky," while dark-eyed Kyrgyz claimed descent from Li Ling, a Han general who had gone over to the Xiongnu centuries earlier [45]. Their economy blended pastoralism with agriculture—cultivating millet, wheat, barley, and highland barley—and their mountains yielded gold, tin, and a prized iron called *jiasha*, hammered into razor-sharp blades [45]. Religiously, they venerated spirits of water and grass, called their shamans *gan*, and cremated their dead, burying the remaining bones a year later [45].

That physical portrait aligns closely with the Bronze and Iron Age paleogenetics of the Minusinsk Basin: Keyser and colleagues genetically predicted light hair and blue or green eyes among their Krasnoyarsk kurgan samples [27], and the early Tashtyk genomes at Oglakhty cluster with the same steppe-descended population [33]. It contrasts sharply with the modern Tian Shan Kyrgyz, who carry substantially higher East Asian ancestry [42]. Even in southern Siberia, the demographic tide was shifting. Soviet physical anthropologists led by Abdushelishvili placed the earliest East Asian skeletal traits on the Yenisei in the Xiongnu era and their predominance in the Chinggisid period [4]. In the neighbouring Altai, a 2026 bioRxiv preprint by Özdemir and colleagues examining 91 ancient genomes documents a "large-scale spread of East Asian genetic ancestry" between the 4th and 12th centuries, unfolding in tandem with "the rise and spread of the Turkic cultural customs" [58].

Language tells an equally layered story. By the 8th century, the Kyrgyz elite wrote in **Old Turkic**. The *Xin Tangshu* asserts that "their script and language are exactly the same as the Uyghurs'" [45], and runic stelae scattered across the Minusinsk Basin and Tuva are attributed to them [59]. Reading the Sudzhi stone's boast—"son of a Kyrgyz"—as a badge of aristocratic status, I. L. Kyzlasov argues that the Kyrgyz were "the ruling clan" over a wider Yenisei polity whose general subjects were styled *qırqız bodun* [1]. Whatever its social boundary, the script of those stones belonged to the literary Old Turkic standard of the steppe khaganates, not to a Kipchak language like modern Kyrgyz [8].

Whether the earlier Jiankun of Han times already spoke Turkic may never be known. With Bayesian phylolinguistics dating Proto-Turkic to roughly the turn of the era (95% probability range: 755 BCE to 483 CE) and the split of Common Turkic to about the 5th century CE [13], Han-era Jiankun could well predate Turkic speech on the upper Yenisei. Tang sources already describe a mixed population [45], and Soucek (following L. R. Kyzlasov) characterizes the Yenisei Kyrgyz as "Turkified Samoyed and Ostyak groups, who were ruled by Turkic Kyrgyz who may have spoken a Kipchak-type language" [4]—a final clause that sits uneasily with Tenishev's *z*-type reconstruction of Yenisei Kyrgyz speech, discussed below. Even the royal title *ajo* (Tang Chinese A-re 阿熱 [45]) has been linked variously to a Samoyed word for "father," to the name of the Az tribe, or to a Turkic root [53].

The idea that Turkic reached the Yenisei only via Mukan Qaghan's conquest in the 550s fits poorly with how the Türks treated them—claiming them as kin in their origin legend [48] and confronting them by 710 as an established qaghanate [52] [53]. More plausibly, a Turkic-speaking elite was already rooted on the Yenisei by the 6th century, governing a Yeniseian-, Samoyedic-, and steppe-descended population that adopted Turkic gradually over the ensuing millennium—the exact timeframe Zeng and colleagues cite for the absorption of Yeniseian speakers from the 6th century CE into early modern times [28].

Even that medieval Yenisei Turkic, however, was not yet modern Kyrgyz. Turkologists divide the family partly by how an ancestral *-d-* between vowels evolved in everyday words like "foot": Old Turkic retained *-d-* (*adaq*), South Siberian languages such as Khakas shifted it to *-z-* (*azax*), and Kipchak languages such as modern Kyrgyz shifted it to *-y-* (*ayak*). Summarizing Tenishev's linguistic analysis, A. Mokeev notes that while the Yenisei Kyrgyz carved a conservative *d*-type literary language onto stone, they spoke a *z*-type vernacular akin to Khakas in daily life [53]. Because modern Kyrgyz belongs to the *y*-type Kipchak branch [8], the linguistic transformation that produced modern Kyrgyz speech happened centuries later and well to the south-west.

#### 3.4 840: Breaking the Uyghurs

By 840, the Uyghur Khaganate on the Orkhon was hollow inside, and tree-ring paleoclimate records help establish the sequence of its collapse. Di Cosmo and his co-authors note that Uyghur decline from roughly **832** "has been attributed to internal rivalries and the rebellion of subject peoples," culminating in the winter of **839–40** in a probable *dzud*—extreme cold locked under deep snow that starves herds by the tens of thousands—"likely the coup de grâce to an already compromised pastoral production" [55]. When Kyrgyz forces struck, the Uyghurs were "too weak and divided to resist" [55]. The *Xin Tangshu* supplies the political timeline that tree rings cannot see: in **839**, a Uyghur chief minister rebelled, summoned Shatuo allies, and drove the qaghan to suicide; famine followed, then pestilence, then heavy snows that killed off sheep and horses across the steppe [45].

The Kyrgyz A-re (*ajo* [53]) did not ride south alone. A defected Uyghur general, **Julu Mohe**, crossed over and guided the assault. In the Tang account of the Uyghurs, the 100,000 horsemen who swept down on the capital were Julu Mohe's troops and the Kyrgyz combined [45] (a host the paleoclimate study treats simply as the Kyrgyz invading army [55]). They killed the reigning Uyghur khan; the A-re burned the khan's court and golden tent, seized the imperial treasury, and captured the Tang princess Taihe [45]. Fifteen Uyghur tribes fled west toward the Karluks, while others scattered south toward Tibet and Anxi [45].

Then came the decision that shaped the next six centuries of Inner Asian history. Every previous conqueror of the eastern steppe had claimed the sacred Ötüken heartland in the Orkhon Valley. The Kyrgyz A-re did not. Instead, he shifted his court (*ya* 牙—the exact term the Tang chroniclers use for the Uyghur royal seat, marking a formal court rather than a temporary camp) "to the south of Mount Lao" (*Laoshan* 牢山), also called **Duman** (賭滿), fifteen days' ride from the ruined Uyghur capital [45].

The Tang record offers three geographic clues to that move. Prior to 840, the Kyrgyz country lay 3,000 *li* northwest of the Uyghur court with the Tanman mountains on its southern flank, and the A-re's seat stood forty days from the Uyghur capital by camel; after 840, the relocated court lay fifteen days' ride away [45]. Differing units and travel speeds between camel caravans and mounted riders prevent precise triangulation, yet taken at face value the move brought the Kyrgyz court closer to Mongolia without placing it within easy striking distance of the Orkhon. Most historians identify Mount Lao (Duman) with the **Tannu-Ola range** along the southern rim of Tuva—straddling the passage between the upper Yenisei and north-western Mongolia—though, as Michael Drompp emphasizes, no archaeological site has yet confirmed the exact mountain peak or court complex [60].

Tree rings suggest why the victors may have turned back toward the mountains: the Orkhon pastures they had just captured had plausibly been battered by the same prolonged drought and killing winter that ruined the Uyghurs [55]. Di Cosmo and his co-authors hedge their climate argument carefully, warning that the "assumption of a direct causal link between drought and the stability of nomadic societies is not always justified," noting that dry years alone "did not trigger migration, pillaging, or conquest," and acknowledging that tree-ring series tracking summer growth "cannot confirm" winter mortality [55]. Still, the historical sequence is unmistakable: a decade of civil strife, a devastating winter, a lightning strike guided by a Uyghur defector—and a victorious qaghan who burned the golden tent and walked away from the imperial valley. That unexpected refusal to occupy the Orkhon opens the most fiercely contested mystery of the medieval record: whether the Kyrgyz ever built an empire stretching to the Tian Shan after 840, or whether the "Kyrgyz Great Power" was largely a phantom.

### Chapter 4: The Phantom Empire and the Tian Shan Gap (840–1207)

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 4**
>
> - **Post-840 Kyrgyz reach did not produce an Orkhon empire:** Although armies struck as far as the Shiwei (847–48) and the Irtysh, Tang archives show the Kyrgyz never settled the Orkhon Valley, the *Liao shi* records no 924 Khitan expulsion, and cemeteries in Tuva and the Usinsk Basin (in use until the Mongol clearance of 1293) confirm the core population remained in southern Siberia.
> - **Altai cremations mark a real but intermittent south-western outpost:** At Chineta II in the north-western Altai, Kyrgyz cremation kurgans span the late 9th through 11th centuries (Yakonur and Ak-Tash phases) before vanishing—the furthest archaeological trace of northern Kyrgyz funerary customs toward the Tian Shan.
> - **Muslim geographers consistently place the Kyrgyz in the north:** The *Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam* (982) and Gardizi (c. 1050) locate them far beyond the Kimaks and Karluks; only al-Marvazi's (~1120) borderland group adopting burial near Muslims and Esin's contested Aksu/Barčūk reading in al-Idrisi hint at any earlier proximity to the Tian Shan rim.
> - **Chigils and Karluks held Issyk-Kul throughout the medieval gap:** Writing in the 1070s, Barskan native Mahmud al-Kashgari places Chigils on Issyk-Kul and consigns the Kyrgyz to the eastern margin of the Turkic world; after the Mongol era, Karakhanid oasis dwellers flowed into Chagatai (Uzbek/Uyghur) towns while mountain pastoralists remained to merge into later Kyrgyz clans.
> - **Kyrgyzstan's runic stones mainly reflect pre-Kyrgyz Semirechye elites:** Paleography and clan *tamgas* tie the Talas and Kochkor inscriptions primarily to 7th–10th-century Western Türk, Türgesh, and Karluk–Chigil rulers, with isolated northern epigraphic traits such as Chiyin-Tash marking limited Yenisei contact.

The year 840 is the hinge on which long-range reconstructions of Kyrgyz history turn. Warriors from the upper Yenisei had toppled the dominant steppe empire of eastern Eurasia; if they stepped into its imperial shoes and pushed their realm westward to Issyk-Kul, then Kyrgyz communities stood in the Tian Shan six centuries before any Persian chronicler noticed them. János Sipos's survey reflects that maximalist picture, giving a mid-9th- to early-10th-century Kyrgyz state spanning southern Siberia and Mongolia all the way to "part of Kashgar, Issyk-kul and Talas," before shrinking back "to Altay and Sayan" in the 11th and 12th centuries [4].

Testing how much of that picture holds up requires prying apart two questions that are routinely run together: how far Kyrgyz armies could ride, and where the Kyrgyz population actually lived.

#### 4.1 Did They Take Mongolia?

Every earlier conqueror who broke the incumbent power on the Mongolian plateau seized the Orkhon Valley. Both the Türks and the Uyghurs anchored their imperial legitimacy in the Ötüken highlands along that river, and installing a royal seat there was the expected next move of any steppe victor. Whether the Kyrgyz broke that precedent is one of the most consequential questions in their history, because the maximalist reading of 840 is what underwrites claims of an early medieval Kyrgyz homeland in the Tian Shan.

Tang diplomatic archives give the decades after 840 a dated skeleton [45]. An embassy dispatched by the A-re in the early 840s took three years to reach Chang'an. Emperor Wuzong intended to invest the Kyrgyz ruler formally as qaghan but died in 846; after court debate under his successor, investiture went forward in 847. During the opening years of the Dazhong era (847–848), when the seven Shiwei clans divided surviving Uyghur groups among themselves, a Kyrgyz minister named A-bo led 70,000 troops against the Shiwei and swept the Uyghurs back north of the Gobi. Three further embassies reached the Tang court between 860 and 874, after which the imperial compilers admit the record of Kyrgyz missions and investitures broke off—closing with the terse verdict that the Kyrgyz never managed to finish off the Uyghurs [45].

What the victors did with Mongolia itself looks strikingly un-imperial. Rather than moving into the burnt Uyghur capital, the A-re established his court fifteen days' ride away, south of Mount Lao. Di Cosmo and his co-authors put it bluntly: the Kyrgyz "did not settle in the Orkhon Valley" and instead "returned north," plausibly because "the economic devastation caused by prolonged droughts and catastrophic frosts may well have made the Orkhon Valley unsuitable for them" [55]. Whatever the cause, the Orkhon Valley did not function as an imperial capital again until the Mongols raised Karakorum there four hundred years later [55].

Archaeology and medieval geography converge on where the Kyrgyz people actually stayed. Surveying the Usinsk Basin in the Western Sayan Mountains, O. A. Mitko catalogues roughly sixteen excavated medieval sites—campsites, cremation cemeteries, and memorial complexes—left by "the medieval population that occupied the Usinsk Basin in the Western Sayan Mountains after 840 AD," whose large burial grounds match "the same features as the monuments of the first variant of the Kyrgyz culture" in Central Tuva and the Yenisei Canyon [61]. Reading the Usinsk Kyrgyz as a distinct local polity inside the Kyrgyz state, Mitko tracks their archaeological continuity unbroken until **1293**, when Mongol forces resettled the population out of the Sayan-Altai [61]. Written geography places the population in the same basin. Soucek situates the victors of 840 "in southern Siberia along the upper Yenisei and in what is today the republic of Tuva" [62]. Reconstructing the Kyrgyz territory depicted in al-Idrisi's 12th-century Arabic atlas—bounded by China to the south, the Kimaks to the north, and the Toghuzghuz (the Muslim geographers' name for the Uyghurs) to the west—Yu. N. Esin concludes that "the depicted domains of the Kyrghyz match to their indigenous lands in the basin of the Yenisei River in South Siberia," preserving "the historical situation of the middle – second half of the 9th century" [63]. Compiled independently in 982, the Persian *Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam* names the same neighbours, differing only on compass bearings (putting China east and the Kimaks west) [64]. Graves, Persian itineraries, and Arabic maps all anchor the Kyrgyz population in the north; apart from two contested clues weighed below—Esin's reading of a second holding in al-Idrisi and al-Marvazi's borderland group—no source puts Kyrgyz in or beside the Tian Shan before the 15th century.

How far Kyrgyz *military reach* extended beyond that northern base is a separate question. Vasily Bartold dubbed the eighty years after 840 the "Kyrgyz Great Power," and in S. A. Ugdyzhekov's rendering of that historiographical tradition, Kyrgyz columns pursuing Uyghur remnants struck "as far as the Irtysh and the Amur rivers" while raiding the oases of Eastern Turkestan [65]. Sipos's summary carries their sphere as far as Talas [4], and Esin treats Kyrgyz authority across "the territory of modern Mongolia" for several decades as established background [63]. A polity able to field 70,000 horsemen against the Shiwei certainly possessed formidable reach, and nothing in the record rules out far-flung raids or tributary leverage.

Yet reach never translated into an imperial centre on the Orkhon. Surveying the Mongolian plateau between the Uyghurs and Chinggis Khan, Isenbike Togan writes that "the Kirgiz, who came to occupy the earlier Türk and later Uyghur territory, did not settle in these lands" and "remained aloof of 'imperial' ideals" [66]. What followed after 844 was an imperial vacuum: "we do not see any new polities ruling over Eurasia until the second half of the 12th century," leaving the plateau to "smaller nomadic groups" that, "in the absence of a unifying and central authority... split further" [66]. From completely different archives, Togan's institutional picture and Di Cosmo's paleoclimate study arrive at the same empty valley.

Two pillars of the conventional "Great Power" narrative collapsed completely once Michael Drompp checked them against the Chinese archives [60] [67]. First, Tang diplomatic correspondence reveals Kyrgyz envoys repeatedly voicing an intention to relocate into former Uyghur lands—a move subsequent Tang letters confirm never happened—while Tang officials complained of the sheer distance to reach them, and attested Kyrgyz military campaigns cease after 848 [60]. A court does not keep petitioning to move into lands it already governs. Second, the textbook story that the Khitans expelled the Kyrgyz from Mongolia in **924** turns out to be a ghost: the *Liao shi*'s record of Emperor Abaoji's 924 western campaign contains "no mention whatsoever of the Kirghiz," and Khitan sources point instead to continuing diplomatic relations [60] [67]. Even Togan, who grants an earlier Kyrgyz presence on the plateau, notes simply that Liao power reached the Orkhon by 924 [66]—an advance requiring no battle against Kyrgyz occupants.

Anyone defending a post-840 Kyrgyz occupation of Mongolia usually reaches first for the **Sudzhi inscription** in Mongolia, whose author called himself "the son of a Kyrgyz." Yet as Chapter 3.2 showed, while S. G. Klyashtorny placed the stone immediately after 840 [56], both Ramstedt and Hatice Şirin (linking its author to a commander named in the c. 753 Taryat monument) situate it inside the pre-840 Uyghur Khaganate [57]. Notably, Drompp's archival dismantling omits the Sudzhi stone altogether [67]—a small blind spot in an otherwise devastating case.

One concrete piece of the maximalist argument survives on the map, however, and cannot simply be brushed aside. Reading al-Idrisi's 12th-century atlas, Yu. N. Esin identifies a *second*, landlocked Kyrgyz possession distinct from the Yenisei homeland, locating it near **Aksu**—possibly the town of **Barčūk**—on the southern fringe of **Karluk** territory east of Kashgar, and linking it to post-840 Kyrgyz expansion [63].

Dismissing Aksu as "the Tarim basin" rather than "the Tian Shan" would draw an artificial border where herders saw none. The Aksu River gathers its headstreams in the Tian Shan [68], and the high mountain road climbing from the Aksu oasis over the **Bedel Pass** down to the southern shore of Lake Issyk-Kul was already well travelled between the 2nd and 4th centuries CE, guarded at its northern mouth by a fortress at Barskoon [69]—right beside the Chigil centre of Chigil-balyk. Pastoralism across these ranges operates vertically: families move mixed herds of sheep, goats, cattle, horses, yaks, and camels between lower, drier winter quarters (*kyshtoo*), spring (*jazdoo*) and autumn (*küzdöö*) foothill camps below 2,500 metres, and alpine summer pastures (*jailoo*) at 2,500–3,500 metres [70] [71] [72]. Vertical *transhumance* and the felt yurt (*boz üy*) are shared across Inner Asia—already echoed in the *Hanshu*'s observation that the Wusun followed "the customs of the Xiongnu" [36]—and therefore prove little about ethnic descent. What they illuminate is the geography of power: to mountain pastoralists shuttling between winter plains and summer meadows, a foothold on the northern Tarim rim and the alpine basin behind the Bedel Pass form a single ecological system.

If Esin's identification holds, it would place Kyrgyz forces or herders at the southern mouth of the Barskoon corridor within decades of 840—five centuries before Persian histories report them in the Tian Shan, along the exact pass overlooking their future pastures. That makes it the sharpest geographical counter-clue cutting against a late arrival. Yet the evidentiary footing is remarkably fragile: a single modern reading of place-names inside one 12th-century compilation, backed by no excavated archaeology. Rather than explaining it away, it belongs squarely on the ledger as an open anomaly.

Taken together, the century after 840 looks neither like a sprawling steppe empire nor like total invisibility. Kyrgyz columns rode far—to the Shiwei, the Irtysh, and perhaps the Tarim rim—while Kyrgyz rulers received envoys and collected tribute. Meanwhile, the Kyrgyz population stayed anchored in the Sayan and Minusinsk basins, cremating its dead in the same ancestral cemeteries. Military projection and demographic migration rarely move in lockstep on the steppe. The upheaval of 840 shattered the Uyghur khaganate and sent Uyghur tribes southward into the Tarim oases to build a settled urban civilization; no comparable evidence shows it carrying the Yenisei Kyrgyz population into the Tian Shan.

#### 4.2 Outposts in the Altai and the Muslim Gaze

Between the final Tang embassies of the 870s and the Mongol arrival in 1207, three independent vantage points catch sight of the northern Kyrgyz: excavated kurgans in the Altai, the Khitan empires pressing from east and west, and Muslim geographers writing from the Islamic world.

The **Altai burials** supply the sole physical trace of Kyrgyz movement toward the south-west during these centuries. At the **Chineta II** cemetery in the north-western Altai, P. K. Dashkovskiy excavated four Kyrgyz cremation kurgans (nos. 10–13) stocked with weapons, belt fittings, and horse harness alongside Srostki-culture features, dating them to the second half of the 9th century—the **Yakonur stage** (second half of the 9th to first half of the 10th century, c. 840–950) [73]. The two radiocarbon determinations reported from kurgans 10 and 12 fit that dating unevenly: 1100 ± 70 BP is compatible with it, but 1300 ± 45 BP, if it is an uncalibrated age as its notation suggests, falls on calibration largely before 840 [73]—a discrepancy that could reflect old wood or a Kyrgyz presence in the Altai before the fall of the Uyghurs, and one that published calibrated ranges would help settle (Appendix E). Dashkovskiy interprets that earlier Yakonur horizon as reflecting peaceful coexistence with local communities during Türk–Kyrgyz cooperation against the Uyghurs [73]. A later **Ak-Tash stage** (second half of the 10th to 11th century, c. 950–1050) tells a sharper story: an intrusive 11th-century Kyrgyz burial was cut directly into kurgan 1, an earlier Srostki-culture mound, pointing either to renewed hostility or to newcomers who no longer knew whose grave stood beneath them [73]. Dashkovskiy offers as one possible explanation that Kyrgyz settlement of the Altai in the 11th century was temporary rather than permanent [73]. The archaeological foothold between the Yenisei and the Tian Shan was real and datable, yet intermittent—and by the 12th to 14th centuries, cremation vanished from the Altai altogether, superseded by Mongol-period inhumation burials (examined in Chapter 5.2) [74].

On the imperial horizon, Khitan powers framed both sides of the Kyrgyz world. In the east, the Khitan Liao reached the Orkhon by 924 [66]. Two centuries later, as the Liao collapsed and the Khitan prince **Yelü Dashi** set out west to found the **Qara Khitai** empire, he wrote to the Uyghur king Bilge recalling that his ancestor, the first Liao emperor, had passed the city of Bügü Khan on his northern campaign and offered the Uyghurs their old homeland [204]. Vásáry's *Iranica* summary renders that reminiscence as Abaoji having "crushed the Kerḡiz (Qerqiz) in Mongolia" [75], but the letter as preserved in the *Liao shi* (juan 30) names no Kyrgyz [204]—so even this late Khitan reminiscence adds no witness to a 924 expulsion [60]. Establishing his new capital at **Balasagun** in the Chüy valley during the 1130s, Yelü Dashi projected authority northward until Qara Khitai jurisdiction "stretched as far as the forest zone where the Kerḡiz (Qerqiz) lived" [75]. Clashes erupted between the Qara Khitai and the Kyrgyz around **1130**: the Kyrgyz were not conquered, though they may afterwards have slipped within the Qara Khitai diplomatic orbit [67]. For much of the 12th century, the Chüy valley and the northern Kyrgyz forests stood at opposite ends of a single imperial frontier.

Muslim geographers looking north-east across that same world between 982 and c. 1120 had no stake in modern national origins—and not one of them locates the Kyrgyz in the Tian Shan.

Compiled in **982**, the anonymous Persian geography ***Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam*** boxes the *Khirkhiz* country cleanly on all four sides: to the east, "the country of China and the Eastern Ocean"; to the south, the Toghuzghuz and part of the Khallukh (Karluks); to the west, part of the Kimak realm; and to the north, empty frozen wastelands [64].
