Governed by the *Khirkhiz-khaqan*, they exported musk, furs, *khadang* and *khalanj* timber, and knife-handles carved from *khutū* [64]. On settlement, the text is stark: aside from the khaqan's own residence, "no class of the Khirkhiz has any villages or towns at all" [64].

That blanket claim sits uneasily beside the *Xin Tangshu*'s catalogue of Kyrgyz millet, wheat, barley, and highland barley harvests and iron weapons [45]. Perhaps Chinese court annals recording tributary commodities and a Persian gazetteer tracking trade routes simply looked at different layers of society, or perhaps farming clustered near the khaqan's districts—the sole exception the *Ḥudūd* permits. An ethnographic analogy from Tian Shan mountain agriculture in Chapter 9.2 suggests a further possibility: transhumant herders could harvest frost-hardy grain without maintaining permanent villages at all. Whatever the exact agricultural balance, an informed Persian writer in 982 knew the Kyrgyz as a northern people far beyond the Kimaks, with the Karluks still lying to their south.

Writing at Ghazna around **1050**, **Gardizi** (*Zayn al-Akhbar*) offers the richest Muslim account—opening with an origin myth as fantastical as any in the Chinese annals. A chieftain of Slav descent kills a Byzantine envoy, flees through Khazar territory, gathers refugees fleeing factional strife among the Toghuzghuz, and "the tribe that gathered around him was given the name Kyrgyz" [50]. Set alongside the *Yuan shi*'s legend of forty women from Han territory marrying local men [47], the parallel is arresting: independently, eastern and western chroniclers explained the ethnonym as an assembly of strangers around a founding episode rather than descent from a single bloodline. Neither fable is literal history, yet both capture how medieval observers understood the name.

Gardizi's ethnography stands on firmer footing. He describes shamans called *faghinun* falling into trance at festive assemblies to prophesy the coming year, trade in musk, furs, and horn, and mixed mortuary customs—corpses placed in trees to decay alongside cremation [50]—matching the *Xin Tangshu*'s water-and-grass spirits and *gan* shamans [45]. His caravan route to the Kyrgyz camp runs north from Chinanjiket (the Turfan oasis) straight through Toghuzghuz country [50], tracing the exact northward corridor implied by the *Hanshu*'s 5,000 *li* north of Jushi a millennium earlier [36].

Around **1120**, **al-Marvazi** recorded the explicit belief behind centuries of northern cremation—stating—in the Kyrgyz notice of V. Minorsky's translation (p. 30), quoted here from summaries pending a print check—that the Khirkhiz "burn their dead," holding that "Fire purifies [the dead] of every stain" [76]. Modern summaries of al-Marvazi's notice add a striking exception: once a group of Kyrgyz became neighbours of the Muslims, they gave up cremation and began burying their dead [20] [76] *(a clause absent from ʿAwfi's 13th-century abridgement; see Appendix E)*. No version names where this borderland group lived. In 1120, the Muslims nearest to any Kyrgyz were subjects of the Karakhanid state spanning Kashgar and Semirechye, leading scholars to associate this passage with Kyrgyz drifting toward the Tian Shan frontier [20]. Paired with Esin's contested Barčūk reading in al-Idrisi [63], al-Marvazi's notice is one of just two clues between 840 and the 15th century placing any Kyrgyz near those mountains—and it depicts neighbours along the Karakhanid border rather than settlers inside Issyk-Kul.

A fourth Muslim author knew the shores of Issyk-Kul from childhood, writing in the 1070s—and he leaves no doubt about who actually occupied the lake.

#### 4.3 Meanwhile at Issyk-Kul: Chigils, Karluks, and the Runic Stones

While northern Kyrgyz bands camped intermittently in the Altai, the Tian Shan belonged to other Turkic peoples. The **Karluks** controlled the Chüy valley until the rise of the **Karakhanid** dynasty in the mid-10th century [39]; in **960**, the Karakhanids adopted Islam as their state religion from their capital at Balasagun [20].

Around Lake Issyk-Kul itself, the defining people from the 7th to the 10th century were the **Chigils** (*Čigil*), whom Peter Golden and Yu. A. Zuev identify with the Tang-era Chuyue (some scholars have also floated a link with the *Čik* of the upper Yenisei runic texts) [38] [40]. Chigil groups held the northern, eastern, and southern shores of Issyk-Kul and lived alongside the Karluks in the Chüy and Talas valleys as well as between the Ili and the Irtysh [6] [38] [77]. Their main settlement, **Chigil-balyk**, stood beside modern Barskoon (medieval Barskan) [78] [77]. Combining pastoralism with town life, they hosted Manichaean, Nestorian Christian, and eventually Muslim communities [78] [38]. Together with the **Yaghma**, the Chigils formed the ruling pillar of the Karakhanid khaganate—the Chigils associated with the khan styled Arslan ("lion") Qara-Khaqan and the Yaghma with the Bughra ("male camel") Qara-Khaqan [38]. Although part of the Chigil military aristocracy marched west with Karakhanid and Seljuk armies toward Transoxiana and Anatolia in the 11th century, S. M. Abramzon argued that the pastoral majority remained anchored around Issyk-Kul [6] [38].

No witness captures 11th-century Issyk-Kul more decisively than **Mahmud al-Kashgari**, born between 1029 and 1038 into the family of the local lord (*bek*) of Barskan on the lake's southern shore [79]. Compiling his monumental dictionary of Turkic dialects, the *Dīwān Lughāt al-Turk*, in the 1070s [77], al-Kashgari states that he learned the speech of the *Qırqız* alongside other Turkic peoples and groups them with the *Qıfčaq, Oğuz, Tuxsı, Yaγma, Čigil, Oγrāq,* and *Čaruq* as speakers of "a pure Turkic, a single language" [80]. Yet when he orders the Turkic peoples geographically—"from West to East between Rūm (Byzantium) and Sin (China)"—the Tatar and the *Qırqız* sit at the far eastern edge, and the *Qırqız* do not appear on his circular world map at all—though Schönig cautions that many tribes named in the text are missing from that map [80]. The people he places at Barskan are the **Čigil** [80]. He distinguishes three Chigil communities—nomads around Barskan and Issyk-Kul, townspeople near Taraz, and villagers near Kashgar—noting that the Oghuz stretched the label *Čigil* to cover every Turk between the Oxus and Upper China [77]. To a native aristocrat of Issyk-Kul, the Kyrgyz lived at the distant eastern margin of the Turkic world; the pastures outside his own door belonged to Chigils and Karluks.

Their speech shaped the written culture of Central Asia for centuries. Karluk and Chigil dialects formed the foundation of Karakhanid Islamic literary Turkic [38], which al-Kashgari himself ranked among the purest forms of Turkic [80]. That literary tradition did not pass directly to Chagatai: Gerhard Doerfer identifies an intermediate stage, **Khwarezmian Turkic**, written along the lower Amu Darya in the 13th and 14th centuries as "the direct predecessor of Classical Chaghatay" [81]. Classical Chagatai flourished from the 15th to 19th centuries—serving, in Doerfer's view, as an early stage of modern **Uzbek**, while modern **Uyghur** descends from the same Karluk lineage [8] [81]. Along that literary chain, Persian and Arabic vocabulary steadily flooded out older Turkic roots: Doerfer measures inherited Old Turkic words dropping from **67.9%** in the Karakhanid era to **51.8%** in early Chagatai and just **14.3%** in classical Chagatai [81]. Centuries later, it was through this late Fergana Chagatai conduit that most Perso-Arabic vocabulary eventually entered Kyrgyz.

Politically and ecologically, the Karakhanid world split in two after the Mongol conquest. The settled oasis Karluk and Chigil populations of Kashgaria, Transoxiana, and the lower Fergana Valley flowed into Chagatai-speaking towns and agricultural districts [38] [82]. Up on the alpine pastures of Issyk-Kul and the high Tian Shan valleys, however, pastoral Chigil, Karluk, and Türgesh clans remained on their mountain grounds and were later absorbed into the forming Kyrgyz tribal union [6] [78]. Apart from al-Marvazi's border notice and Esin's al-Idrisi hypothesis, no medieval source places Kyrgyz in these mountains before the 15th century; as Soucek summarizes, the Tian Shan Kyrgyz "evolved from nomadic elements over the 13-16th centuries, immigrating to this area during the Chinggisid times" [4]. Even when the Qara Khitai governed from Balasagun in the 1130s across a sphere extending to the northern Kyrgyz forest edge [75], the Chüy basin and the upper Yenisei remained separate worlds.

##### Who Cut the Tian Shan Runic Stones? Script Variants and Clan *Tamgas*

That leaves one vivid visual riddle from Chapter 1: if Chigils and Karluks held the Tian Shan from the 7th to the 11th century, who carved the dense clusters of Old Turkic runic stones around Barskoon, Kochkor, and Talas? Paleography and carved clan emblems (*tamgas*) point to a layered answer: most of Kyrgyzstan's runic monuments belong to the Western Türk, Türgesh, and Karluk–Chigil elites who governed these valleys between the 7th and 10th centuries, accented by only isolated paleographic signs of direct Yenisei contact [59] [83] [84].

Across the **Talas** and **Kochkor** valleys, memorials cut onto boulders and river pebbles use a distinctive regional script—often called the **Talas alphabet**—whose letterforms diverge systematically from the Yenisei standard and stand beside *tamgas* matching emblems struck on 8th-century Türgesh coinage and carved on Karluk stelae [59] [83] [84]. Mapping carved *tamgas* across the Altai, Mongolia, and Semirechye, Alexey Rogozhinsky and Dmitry Cheremisin track dynastic marks of the Karluk confederation and the Uyghur Yaglakar clan spreading southwest from central Mongolia and the Altai into Semirechye and the Issyk-Kul basin during the second half of the 8th century—the exact decade when the Karluks seized Suyab around 766 [39] [83].

Yet at least one eastern Tian Shan inscription cuts across that regional divide. Analyzing the **Chiyin-Tash** rock inscription, Rysbek Alimov, Kubatbek Tabaldiev, and Kayrat Belek found that several diagnostic runic letters (notably the signs for *g*, *d*, and *kẅ*) and the invocation *teŋride(ki) yerde(ki)* ("in heaven and on earth") match the **Yenisei** epigraphic standard rather than neighbouring Talas and Kochkor monuments [84]. Modern clan brands echo the same composite layering: when S. M. Abramzon matched the livestock and boundary *tamgas* of living Kyrgyz tribes (*uruu*) against medieval stone emblems, he traced parallels across all three formative strata—early medieval Semirechye tribes, Altai-Siberian clans, and Kipchak-Mongol confederations [6] [83]. Kyrgyzstan's runic stones therefore record the literate Western Türk, Türgesh, and Karluk–Chigil elites whose pastoral descendants later folded into the Kyrgyz, alongside faint epigraphic footprints of northern contact.

By 1207, then, nearly four centuries after the burning of the Uyghur golden tent, the northern Yenisei world and the southern Tian Shan world still lay largely separated—linked by intermittent Altai outposts, caravan corridors, and a thin imperial frontier under the Qara Khitai. What finally tore open that northern isolation, drew the Yenisei Kyrgyz into the western Mongol Oirat orbit, and rearranged both ends of the 1,650-kilometre corridor was the rise of Chinggis Khan.

## Act II: The Crucible and the Split

### Chapter 5: The Mongol and Oirat Storm (1207–c. 1450)

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 5**
>
> - **Mongol rule destabilized the Yenisei—and sent deportees east, not south-west:** Submitting alongside the Oirats in 1207 and rebelling in 1218–19, the Yenisei Kyrgyz were folded into Ariġ Böke's Altai–Yenisei appanage, swept by Qaidu's wars, and cleared in 1293 by Qubilai's general Tuq Tuqa, who relocated households eastward to Manchuria (Zhaozhou) rather than toward Issyk-Kul.
> - **The Altai became the linguistic and cultural crucible:** Between the 12th and 14th centuries, northern cremations gave way in the Altai to local Mongol-period log-coffin inhumations, while shared language, political titles (*inal*, *idi*), and exclusive clan names (Mundus, Teles) bind modern Kyrgyz closely to Southern Altaians—even though Kyrgyz lack Southern Altaians' Cis-Baikal ancestry and high haplogroup Q.
> - **Oirat entanglement bridged the northern steppe and Moghulistan:** After the Yuan fell in 1368, the Kyrgyz dynasty of Ugechi-Kashka led the Oirat confederation (from 1399, for about forty years on Kyzlasov's estimate), and Oirat wars against Moghulistan in the 1420s immediately preceded the earliest retrospective Qing (~1430s) and Persian (mid-15th-century) notices placing Kyrgyz in the Tian Shan.
> - **Western Mongolia served as a corridor rather than a dated medieval homeland:** Lake Khyargas Nuur is first attested on a dated map only in 1766 (*Kirgis noor*), "Kyrgyz graves" (*khirigsuurs*) are Bronze Age, and the Turkic-origin Khotons of Uvs (82.5% bottlenecked R1a1) descend from Dzungar-era deportees moved northward from Turkestan.
> - **Chagatayid Moghulistan and the *Manas* epic frame the arrival:** Moghulistan (Muslim at court from 1354) supplied the receiving society from which Mirza Haidar saw the Kyrgyz breaking away, while the *Manas* epic preserves genuine memories of 13th–15th-century life among Kalmaks in the Altai even as it reverses the direction of migration.

No straight road ran from Minusinsk to Issyk-Kul. The real hinge between southern Siberia and the Tian Shan lay in the Altai Mountains and western Mongolia, and it was swung shut and then forced open by two centuries of imperial upheaval. Under Chinggis Khan and the Yuan dynasty, the Yenisei Kyrgyz became a frontier province swept by civil wars and imperial deportations; after the Yuan fell in 1368, they entered the confederation of the western Mongol Oirats, whom a Kyrgyz dynasty briefly led. Over those same centuries, the Tian Shan passed from the Qara Khitai to the Chagatai khans and the Muslim rulers of Moghulistan—the exact realm where Kyrgyz groups first appear in the 15th century, riding alongside Oirats.

#### 5.1 Forest Vassals and Yuan Clearances

In **1207**, Chinggis Khan sent his eldest son Jochi northward against the forest peoples. As *The Secret History of the Mongols* records, the Oirat chief Quduqa Beki rode out ahead of his people to submit, followed by the Kyrgyz lords Yedi-inal, Aldi-er, and Örebek-digin, who arrived bearing tribute of white gerfalcons, white geldings, and black sables [85] [86]. Writing a century later, the Persian statesman Rashid al-Din independently confirms the submission: after Chinggis dispatched envoys, the Kyrgyz rulers "sent back three of their emirs" bearing a white falcon [87]. At that moment, the Oirats—whom Turkic speakers later knew as Kalmaks [88]—lived along the Shishkhid River just west of Lake Khövsgöl, among the headwaters of the Yenisei [86]. Kyrgyz and Oirats began the Mongol century as immediate neighbours.

That submission broke within a decade. When a Tumat uprising flared and the Mongols levied troops from the forest frontier, "the Kyrgyz... refused and rebelled" in **1218 or 1219** [87]. Chinggis sent Jochi back north; his vanguard crushed a Kyrgyz force under Kurlun, and Jochi "crossed the frozen Kem-Kemjiut River" to bring the region to heel [87]. A winter punitive campaign across frozen rivers was only the first of several imperial shocks that repeatedly uprooted populations across the upper Yenisei over the next two centuries.

Soon the Kyrgyz heartland was folded into a single Chinggisid estate spanning both ends of the Sayan–Altai corridor. Tracing the hereditary *appanage* granted to **Ariġ Böke**—youngest son of Tolui and brother of Qubilai Khan—from **1252**, Michael Hope places its summer pastures "around the Altai mountains" and its winter quarters in "Uriangqat and Qirghiz" territory along the Yenisei, an estate that at its broadest may have stretched "from modern Krasnoyarsk to Buryatia and western Mongolia" [89].

The official *Yuan shi* (1370) captures the Mongol state's view of the Kyrgyz country, which it transcribes as **Jilijisi** 吉利吉思 [47]. Lying "more than ten thousand *li*" from the Yuan capital and spanning 1,400 *li* by roughly 700, its people "live in felt tents and herd according to water and grass, know some farming, and when it snows ride wooden skis to hunt" [47]. The Yuan court "divided its people into nine thousand households" and in **1270** dispatched Liu Haoli as judicial commissioner over Jilijisi and the adjacent districts of Hanna, Qianzhou, and Yilanzhou [47]. That administrative census left a remarkably durable imprint: centuries later, Khakas oral tradition still remembered the Yenisei confederation as "the Kyrgyz nine-thousand union Khoorai" [7] [56]. Crucially, the Yenisei lands answered to the Yuan court in north China rather than to the Golden Horde named in some secondary summaries [4].

Ariġ Böke's appanage dragged the Kyrgyz straight into the front lines of the prolonged war between Qubilai Khan and **Qaidu**, the Ögedeid ruler of Central Asia. From **1276**, Ariġ Böke's sons Yobuqur and Malik Temür based their herds on the Irtysh under Qaidu's protection and raided Yuan outposts between 1289 and 1292 [89]. In **1292**, Qubilai dispatched Bayan the Barin to clear the northern frontier: Bayan "immediately advanced on the Qirghiz domains, driving Qaidu's supporters from that region and depriving Yobuqur and Malik Temür of their father's yurts" [89]. Five years later, in **1297**, an imperial force operating from the Kyrgyz domains marched all the way back to the Irtysh [89].

The decisive blow fell in **1293**, and two independent archives lock together on that exact year. As Michal Biran documents, Qubilai's Kipchak general **Tuq Tuqa (Tutugha)** campaigned deep into the Qirghiz lands (*Qilijisi* / *Jilijisi*) in 1293 to snap Qaidu's grip on the Yenisei [90]. That exact year marks the abrupt endpoint of O. A. Mitko's excavated Usinsk Basin sites in the Western Sayan, where Mongol deportation emptied the cemeteries [61]. *The Cambridge History of the Mongol Empire* identifies where those deportees went: in 1293, Qubilai transferred **Urusud, Qamqanas, and Qirghiz households** from the lands between the Ob and the Yenisei **eastward to Manchuria**, planting military-agricultural garrisons—notably at Zhaozhou in the Songhua and Nen river region—alongside parallel Yuan relocations into north China and Shandong recorded in the *Yuan shi* [91]. That imperial transfer independently backs O. Karatayev's dating of the earliest Yenisei Kyrgyz moves toward Mongolia and Manchuria to Qubilai's reign [92], and it carries an essential directional lesson: the great 1293 clearance sent Yenisei Kyrgyz eastward to Manchuria, not south-west toward Issyk-Kul. Yet a century of winter conquests (1218–19), Irtysh–Yenisei appanage wars (1276–97), and mass deportations (1292–93) repeatedly shook loose communities across the entire northern frontier.

Some fragments drifted north rather than east or south. As reported in D. U. Sapalova's survey, Fyodor Vasiliev proposed that groups bearing the names *kyrgys*, *khoro*, and *tumat* entered central Yakutia during the 13th- and 14th-century Mongol wars around Lake Baikal, and Sakha (Yakut) oral tradition remembers the turbulent era before Russian arrival as *kyrgys üyete*, "the age of the Kyrgys"—an echo A. I. Gogolev linked to Yenisei Kyrgyz influence [93]. The ethnonym also endures as a clan name among Tuvans, Khakas, and Altaians [94]. Among the Sakha, however, any incoming *kyrgys* bands melted into the local gene pool: as Chapter 10.3 confirms, Sakha men carry almost none of the Kyrgyz paternal lineages.

Around **1310**, **Rashid al-Din** (*Jami' al-Tawarikh*) surveyed the Kyrgyz country from the far western edge of the Mongol world in Ilkhanid Iran [87]. Treating "Kyrgyz and Kem-Kemjiut" as twin upper-Yenisei regions that "together form one state," he records ***inal*** as their ruler's title and ***idi*** for leading notables [87]—the exact titles A. Mokeev traces from the Yenisei into Altai Kyrgyz political vocabulary—and names the ***kishtyms*** (tributary subject communities) around 1300 [87], some three centuries before Russian tax records documented the same institution. One observation in Rashid al-Din pulls sharply against earlier Muslim geography: he describes the Kyrgyz country as "rich with cities, villages, and numerous nomads" [87], aligning with Tang accounts of farming [45] while contradicting the *Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam*'s claim three centuries earlier that they possessed no towns beyond the khaqan's seat [64]. Three informed outsiders across five centuries could not agree on how sedentary the Yenisei Kyrgyz were—likely because farming pockets and nomadic districts coexisted across the basin, and each observer generalized from whichever informants reached his court.

Both linguistics and genetics preserve fingerprints of this Mongol century. Rysbek Alimov identifies a Yuan-period Middle Mongolian loanword stratum in Kyrgyz shared strictly with Siberian Turkic languages and absent from Kazakh [95]—evidence placing ancestral Kyrgyz speakers inside the Siberian Turkic zone during the 13th and 14th centuries (weighed against Jonathan Washington's competing loanword count in Chapter 8.3). Across the genome, Uyghurs, Kazakhs, and Xinjiang Kyrgyz share a pulse of eastern ancestry roughly 30 generations ago that Chen and colleagues tentatively tie to Mongol rule [42], while a Eurasia-wide Turkic genomic survey dates South Siberian and Mongolian admixture across Kazakhs, Kyrgyz, and neighbouring Kipchak and Karluk peoples to the 13th and 14th centuries [96].

> **A Lookalike Official: Körgüz (George)**
>
> Persian histories of Ögedei Khan's reign describe an influential governor of Khurasan named **Körgüz (Kūrgūz)**, who administered eastern Iran from 1236 until his execution around 1242 [97] [98]. Because Arabic-script manuscripts often spell the personal name *Körgüz* and the ethnonym *Qırqız* identically, the governor has occasionally been mistaken for a 13th-century Kyrgyz noble operating in Iran. Ata-Malik Juvayni's *Tarikh-i Jahan-gusha* (*History of the World-Conqueror*, c. 1260) closes the case: Körgüz was a **Uyghur** born in a village four *farsakhs* (roughly 20–25 kilometres) from Beshbalyk, north of the eastern Tian Shan, who rose inside the Mongol civil service as a scribe and instructor in the Uyghur script [97] [98]. As Paul Pelliot established, *Körgüz*—also borne by the Christian Ongud prince of that name—is the Turkic-Mongolic form of the Syriac Christian name *Giwargis* (**George**), reflecting the Nestorian milieu of steppe elites before Körgüz converted to Islam late in life [97] [99].
>
> Untangling the misreading illuminates the wider geography of Mongol rule. Körgüz belonged to the literate Uyghur administrative class that carried the Karakhanid–Uyghur written tradition into the Chagatai ulus [81]—a career anchored squarely in the Karluk–Uyghur oasis world rather than among the Kyrgyz of the Yenisei or Altai.

#### 5.2 The Altai Bridge and the Western Mongolia Mirage

Across several major reconstructions, the Altai Mountains between the 10th and 15th centuries serve as the crucible where the Tian Shan Kyrgyz began to take shape [4] [5]. K. I. Petrov situated that core on the upper Yenisei and in the southern Altai among Kimak-Kipchak and Mongol tribes; L. R. Kyzlasov proposed "Inner Asian Kyrgyz" ranging between the Altai and the Khingan in northern Mongolia during the early Chinggisid period; Koychuev, Mokrynin, and V. M. Ploskikh traced a 9th–10th-century Kyrgyz population in the Altai and Dzungaria absorbing Kimak-Kipchaks while retaining its ethnonym; and R. A. Abdumanapov reconstructed a post-840 community in the western and southern Altai spurs where a Kimak-Kipchak majority under Yenisei Kyrgyz rulers gave the group Kipchak speech [4] [5].

Political titles point along the same route. In A. Mokeev's reconstruction, the Kyrgyz polity moved sequentially from the Yenisei to the Altai and then into the Tian Shan [53]. Linking Rashid al-Din's testimony to Altai Kyrgyz principalities governed by *inals* until the Mongol conquest, Mokeev argues that the old Yenisei title *ajo* shifted to *idi* in Altai Kyrgyz speech—and may survive in **Adigine**, the name of one of the two branches of the modern Kyrgyz right wing [46] [53]. Drawing on Tenishev and Oruzbaeva, he concludes that "long-term linguistic interaction with the Altai tribes led to the formation of a common Kyrgyz colloquial language" [53].

For the 12th through 14th centuries, however, that Altai bridge rests on language, titles, and clan memory rather than on Kyrgyz-attributed graves. The intermittent Chineta II cremations ceased in the 11th century (Chapter 4.2) [73], and the burial record that follows changes completely. Surveying twenty-six excavated Mongol-period sites across the Altai, N. N. Seregin, N. A. Konstantinov, and A. V. Ebel identify a standardized 12th–14th-century rite attributed to the local Altai population: a small oval stone barrow covering an individual laid in a hollowed log coffin or plain pit, head to the north or west, invariably an inhumation "with the dead laid on their backs, extended," accompanied by horse gear though rarely by a buried horse [74]. That three-century horizon changes the question of why northern cremation disappeared. While al-Marvazi credited one southern border group's switch to burial around 1120 to Muslim neighbours [20] [76], in the Altai the Kyrgyz cremation horizon was followed by a standardized Mongol-period inhumation rite that Seregin and colleagues attribute to the local Altai population rather than to Kyrgyz [74]; whether any Kyrgyz-descended groups adopted it cannot yet be read from the graves.

Living communities in the Altai still preserve striking threads of that shared past. Today's **Southern Altaians** (Altai-kizhi, Telengits, and Teleuts) remain the closest linguistic relatives of the Kyrgyz—their southern dialects stand nearest to Kyrgyz speech and share structural isoglosses widely interpreted as pointing to a South Siberian and Altai origin [12] [13] (analyzed in Chapter 8). Clan nomenclature echoes the linguistic proximity: L. P. Potapov tracked the clan name **Mundus** across Southern Altaians, Teleuts, and the Kyrgyz (including a *Kotkar-Mundus* subdivision), reading it as genuine kinship and suggesting that part of the **Teles** clan (*seok*) Yary moved into Kyrgyz territory beside Teles and Mundus groups in the 17th century [100]. R. Zh. Zholdoshov observes that Mundus and Teles occur "only within the tribal composition of the Kyrgyz and Altaians," traces the Altaian *seok* Dyeti-sary to the Yenisei Kyrgyz, and concludes that "the greatest number of Kyrgyz ethnic ties can be traced to Altai" [101]. Oral traditions agree: an Altaian narrative recorded by Butanaev remembers the Kyrgyz as former residents of the Southern Altai, while a legend recorded by Moldobaev describes Kyrgyz and Altaians once living together as "the people of one khanate" [101].

Yet genetics draws a sharp line beneath those cultural ties. On the paternal line, haplogroup **R1a1** reaches **50%** among Altai-kizhi men (Dulik et al. 2012 [102]) and up to **63%** in central Kyrgyzstan samples [103]—a frequency compatible with a shared steppe pool, though too widespread across Central Asia to prove direct descent on its own. Meanwhile, **haplogroup Q** splits them cleanly, standing at **16.7%** among Altai-kizhi men [102] against 0–2% among the Kyrgyz [104]. Across the whole genome, the contrast is sharper still: Southern Altaians carry the **Cis-Baikal Yeniseian ancestry** of the Yenisei basin, whereas genomic models for modern Kyrgyz—despite substantial broader Siberian ancestry [42]—do not require it [28] (examined statistically in Chapter 10.2). The Altai was almost certainly where Kyrgyz speech and core clan ties took shape, yet the groups that carried them south must have been small—or drawn from populations with far less Cis-Baikal ancestry—relative to the mountain society they joined in the Tian Shan.

Politics soon drew those Altai and Yenisei groups into the western Mongol whirlwind. After the Yuan dynasty collapsed in **1368**, the middle and upper Yenisei passed under **Oirat** control. In **1399**, as S. G. Klyashtorny documents, a Kyrgyz dynasty took command of the Oirat confederation: its founder, **Ugechi-Kashka**, discarded the Yuan dynastic label and renamed the realm and its people **"Dadan" (Tatar)** [56]. I. L. Kyzlasov cautions against inflating that episode, estimating that Ugechi-Kashka's dynasty lasted only about **forty years** [1]. Counted from 1399, that gives an end around **1440**, close to the **1430s** date implied by the Qing *Xiyu zhi*'s retrospective account of Kyrgyz fleeing turmoil into the mountains (Chapter 7.1)—but the endpoint is an arithmetical estimate rather than a dated event, Mirza Haidar already names a different Oirat leader, Isan Taishi, as Vais Khan's captor in the 1420s [15], and no surviving chronicle links the two episodes.

By the **1420s**, Oirat armies were pressing relentlessly on Moghulistan. Mirza Haidar writes that the Moghul ruler **Vais Khan** (d. 1428 or 1429) "had sixty-one engagements with the Kalmaks: once only was he victorious," and was once captured and brought before the Oirat leader **Isan Taishi** [15]. During Vais Khan's reign, however, the *Tarikh-i Rashidi* names only Oirats (Kalmaks)—no surviving Persian chronicle explicitly attests Kyrgyz inside Moghulistan in the 1420s [14] [15]. Retrospective Kyrgyz notices begin shortly afterward: a Qing countback places a Kyrgyz flight into the mountains during the first half of the 15th century (~1433–1440), while the earliest-dated Persian episode—preserved in Mahmud ibn Wali's 17th-century *Bahr al-asrar*—records Kyrgyz alongside Kalmaks in Semirechye around the mid-15th century during the reign of Esen Buqa II [14]. Historians differ over what set the movement in motion: Soucek argues that "it was not the Chinggisid but the Oirat pressure" that pushed the Kyrgyz out of the Irtysh–Yenisei zone, whereas Abramzon attributed "the different migrations" to the Mongol invasions, with some bands moving earlier [4]. Because later Persian histories show Kyrgyz and Oirat tribes raiding Moghulistan's agricultural districts during the same mid-15th-century crisis [14] without separating forced flight from joint expansion, both pressures almost certainly operated across three centuries and dozens of clans.

Because the Oirats dominated **western Mongolia**—dwelling west of Lake Khövsgöl from 1207 [86], coming under Ugechi-Kashka's Kyrgyz dynasty in 1399 [56], and later giving way to the Khalkha **Altan Khans** of the Khotogoids who taxed the Yenisei Kyrgyz in the 17th century [17] [105]—several Mongol-era theories treat western Mongolia itself as an intermediate Kyrgyz homeland (including L. R. Kyzlasov's "Inner Asian Kyrgyz" between the Altai and the Khingan [4] [5]). Three local landmarks are routinely cited as proof, yet none establishes a dated medieval homeland:

- **Lake Khyargas Nuur (*Kyrgyz-Nor*, Uvs province):** Vasily Bartold floated the possibility that Han-era Kyrgyz ranged as far south as Lake Kyrgyz-Nor while admitting "there is no information about when or why the lake received this name" [78], and Savinov found later attempts to link the lake to the Yenisei Kyrgyz unconvincing [44]. Although *khargas* is almost certainly a Mongolian rendering of *Kyrgyz* [106], Bartold's archival verdict still holds: no medieval chronicle or map attests the toponym. The earliest dated attestation identified so far is *Kirgis noor* on the **1766** Manchu version of the Qianlong imperial atlas (British Library IOR/X/3265, catalogued by N. Kenzheakhmet [107]), alongside undated Mongol maps of the Uvs basin. Whether coined after 840 (if Mount Lao lay nearby) or in the 17th century under the Altan Khans, the lake's name carries no medieval date stamp.
- **The "Kyrgyz graves" (*khirigsuurs*):** Mongols call the monumental stone mounds dotting their steppe *khirigsuur*, a word Yu. S. Khudyakov derives from *khargas uur* ("Kyrgyz nest" or "Kyrgyz tomb"), reflecting the folk habit of crediting older monuments to whoever came before [106]. Archaeologically, *khirigsuurs* belong to the **Late Bronze and Early Iron Age** [106] [108], centuries older than any record of the Kyrgyz name. The label preserves memory of a Kyrgyz presence on the plateau—fitting their post-840 ascendancy—rather than an actual burial horizon. While scattered 9th–10th-century Kyrgyz cremations appear across northern and central Mongolia, Khudyakov and Borisenko confirm that the overwhelming concentration of 9th–10th-century Kyrgyz kurgans sits in **Tuva** and the Sayan–Altai corridor toward the Uyghur lands, and surveys hunting for early Gyangun (Jiankun) graves in north-western Mongolia found nothing [109]. No published inventory identifies a 13th–15th-century Yenisei Kyrgyz cemetery in western Mongolia; excavated Altai graves of those centuries belong to Seregin's local Mongol-period inhumation rite [74].
- **The Khotons of Uvs:** In Uvs province's Ulaangom district lives a Muslim, now largely Mongolised community of Turkic origin numbering more than 10,000 who still declare, "We are Kyrgyz, grandchildren of Kün-ker" [110]. Rather than marking an ancient pre-migration homeland, both history and genetics point the other way. Historians trace the Khotons to Turkestani captives relocated north under the Dzungars [110]. Published Y-chromosome data from Katoh and colleagues (40 Khoton men [111]) show an astonishing **82.5% haplogroup R1a1** (33 of 40 men) with severe founder-bottleneck diversity (**0.3141**) and only **10.0% C2** (reported as C3c)—standing completely apart from Oirats and Mongols (56–78% C2) and clustering with Central Asian populations [111]. Although Katoh's markers did not resolve Kyrgyz-specific R1a subclades, a severely bottlenecked Central Asian R1a1 profile fits a small deported Turkestani or Tian Shan Kyrgyz captive group settled in Uvs under the Dzungars—more than a century after Kyrgyz were already recorded in the Tian Shan—documenting movement northward inside the Oirat empire rather than an ancestral western Mongolian homeland.

Western Mongolia therefore belongs squarely inside the Oirat corridor through which the Kyrgyz name travelled, without yet yielding a dated medieval Kyrgyz settlement. Only ancient genomes from 13th–15th-century burials in the Uvs basin and Mongolian Altai could settle who lived beside Khyargas Nuur before the southward move.

#### 5.3 The Receiving World: From Chagatai to Moghulistan (1218–1420s)

While the northern Kyrgyz passed from Yuan administration into the Oirat sphere, the Tian Shan world they were about to enter underwent its own political remaking—forging the Moghul society from which Mirza Haidar would later describe the Kyrgyz as breaking away [15].

In **1211**, the fugitive Naiman prince **Küchlüg** usurped the Qara Khitai empire centred at Balasagun in the Chüy valley [75]. Mongol forces under Jebe ended his reign in **1217 or 1218** (Vásáry places the conquest in 1217, Rossabi in 1218) [75] [98], folding the Chüy basin and Tian Shan into the Mongol realm. Chinggis Khan awarded his second son **Chagatai** a domain reaching from the Uyghur borderlands to Samarkand and Bukhara, with his royal seasonal camps along the Ili River near **Almaliq** [112]. An autonomous Chagatai khanate emerged in the 1260s, soon caught for three decades after c. **1271** in Qaidu's prolonged conflict with the Yuan, whose armies crossed the Tarim and Dzungarian basins repeatedly [98] [112]. By **1305**, the Chagatayids held the entire Tarim basin, and by the 1320s the Yuan abandoned Turkestan to them [98].

From the **1340s**, the khanate split in two. Western Transoxiana passed under tribal amirs, while the eastern realm—**Moghulistan**—kept Chinggisid khans ruling successively from Almaliq, Kashgar, and Aksu [112]. In **1354**, the Moghul khan **Tughlugh Timur** made **Islam** the state religion of Moghulistan [20]. When he invaded Transoxiana after 1358, **Timur** of the Barlas rose initially under his patronage; even after Timur seized the west by 1370, neither he nor his Timurid heirs ever subdued Moghulistan [112]. Chinggisid Moghul khans still governed the Tian Shan when Oirat forces launched their destructive raids during Vais Khan's reign (d. 1428 or 1429) [15] [112].

Two features of this receiving society explain what happened next. Politically, it was **Moghul**—Chinggisid in dynasty and Muslim at court since 1354—which is the exact population Mirza Haidar meant when he called the Kyrgyz a breakaway branch of the Moghuls [15]. Ecologically, its settled lowland Karluk and Chigil oases in Kashgaria and the lower Fergana Valley had already drifted into the Chagatai-speaking urban world (Chapter 4.3) [38], leaving the high alpine pastures to pastoral clans positioned to merge with newcomers arriving from the Altai and Oirat frontier [6].

#### 5.4 Inside Memory: What the *Manas* Epic Remembers

Chronicles track dates and frontiers from the outside; the monumental Kyrgyz oral epic ***Manas*** remembers the Altai centuries from the inside. Standing outside any single era—first noticed in writing in the late 15th and early 16th centuries, first set down from oral performance in the 19th, and layering memories from the Tang to the Qing—the trilogy of *Manas*, *Semetey*, and *Seytek* "describes the unification of scattered tribes into one nation" [113]. For centuries it lived purely in voice: an uninterrupted performance can stretch thirteen hours, bards take up the calling after a prophetic dream [113], Chokan Valikhanov recorded the earliest substantial written text in the mid-19th century [114], and prior to the 20th century fewer than one Kyrgyz in a hundred could read [115].

Across its major variants, the epic casts the foothills of the **Ala-Too** as the original Kyrgyz homeland, from which **Alooke**—ruler of the "Kara-Kytai" and the "Manchus"—drives them out. Manas's father **Jakyp** lands in the **Altai**, hemmed in by Kara-Kalmaks and Torghuts, where Manas is born "surrounded by alien tribes" [116]. In Iraj Bashiri's synopsis, the four sons of Nogoi are scattered to distant lands, Jakyp grows wealthy in the Altai, Kalmaks beat him, and the young Manas battles them in "many bloody encounters" before being chosen leader [117]. In Jusup Mamai's version, Jakyp's brother Jamgyrchi serves as a youth in the household of a Kalmak *bai* named Eshtek [118]. At last a council resolves to lead the Kyrgyz "from the Altai to Ala-Too, the land of their ancestors," sending the heroes ranging across the entire corridor from the Altai to the Tian Shan [88].

Scholars peel back different historical horizons inside that story. A. A. Bakirov anchors its central axis in the 16th–18th-century struggle against "the Oirat-Dzungar invasion, or Kalmaks, as the Turkic-speaking peoples called them" [88]. Bernshtam reached back to the 7th–9th centuries, and K. Zh. Sadykov proposes that the epic's prototype was an early-9th-century Kyrgyz khan who broke the Uyghur capital, with Kalmaks and Kara-Kytai layered over older rivals in collective memory [116]. Yet the Kalmak wars entered the tradition early: composed in Persian in the Fergana Valley during the early 16th century by **Sayf al-Din Akhsikandi** (and completed by his son Nur Muhammad), the Sufi chronicle *Majmūʿ al-tawārīkh* already weaves *Manas* episodes together with Kyrgyz holy war against the Kalmaks [16] [119] [120].

Measured against dated archives, the epic's Altai chapter aligns squarely with the **13th to 15th centuries**—when Oirats lived beside the Kyrgyz from 1207 [85] [86], a Kyrgyz dynasty led them from 1399 [56], and Oirat armies battered Moghulistan in the 1420s [15] right before retrospective Qing and Persian notices place Kyrgyz in Moghulistan during the mid-15th century [14]. Older and younger foes merged into the same tapestry: 10th–12th-century Khitans (Turkic *Kytai*) likely echo in "Kara-Kytai," recalling the 12th-century Qara Khitai realm spanning from the Chüy valley to the Kyrgyz forest frontier [75], while styling Alooke ruler of the "Manchus" folds in Qing-era adversaries [116].

One sharp inversion separates oral memory from external archives: *Manas* reverses the compass of travel. In the epic, the Altai is a place of exile and the Ala-Too the land of the ancestors. Written sources, by contrast, place the Kyrgyz name on the Yenisei and in the Altai centuries before it enters the Tian Shan; the closest linguistic cousin of Kyrgyz is Southern Altai [12] [13]; and genomic dating places modern Kyrgyz formation in the Mongol and post-Mongol centuries [42]. Because the earliest written traces of the epic emerge only in the late 15th and 16th centuries [16] [114]—once Kyrgyz tribes were already entrenched in Moghulistan—its portrayal of the Ala-Too as an ancestral homeland reads best as a claim of belonging to the mountains they now held, rather than a literal chronicle of where they began.

Even as Kyrgyz-named clans pressed south-west out of the Oirat world into Moghulistan during the 15th century, however, the ancient homeland on the upper Yenisei did not vanish. For nearly three centuries, two separate Kyrgyz histories ran concurrently—one enduring on the Yenisei until 1703, the other forging a new people in the Tian Shan.

### Chapter 6: The Branch That Stayed — The Yenisei to 1703

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 6**
>
> - **Two Kyrgyz histories ran in parallel for nearly three centuries:** While Tian Shan Kyrgyz coalesced from the 15th century onward, the northern "Kyrgyz Land" of principalities (Altyr, Altysar, Ezer) endured on the upper Yenisei until 1703—ruling out a wholesale departure of the Minusinsk population.
> - **On the Yenisei, "Kyrgyz" functioned as a political rank:** From Rashid al-Din (c. 1300) to 17th-century Russian tax books, tributary *kyshtyms* were counted inside the Kyrgyz polity, showing that the label marked political status rather than a closed ethnic boundary.
> - **The 1703 Dzungar deportation did not create the Tian Shan Kyrgyz:** Dzungar forces moved 15,000–20,000 Yenisei Kyrgyz and *kyshtyms* to Dzungaria—not Semirechye—two full centuries after Persian chronicles already recorded Kyrgyz firmly entrenched in the Tian Shan.
> - **The Khakas inherited the Minusinsk Basin through "kyshtymization":** Once the Kyrgyz aristocracy was broken and deported, surviving clans and former *kyshtyms* merged under Russian administration as *tadar* ("Tatars," reviving "Khakas" in 1917–18), preserving high Cis-Baikal ancestry and ~50% haplogroup N largely absent among Tian Shan Kyrgyz.
> - **The Fuyu Kyrgyz provide a decisive linguistic benchmark:** Deported from Dzungaria to Manchuria in 1733 and 1755–57, they kept the ethnonym *Kyrgyz* for nearly three centuries—and kept speaking a dialect of Khakas rather than Kipchak Kyrgyz.

From roughly 1400 onward, Kyrgyz history ran along two separate tracks for nearly three centuries. Far to the south-west, Kyrgyz clans began surfacing in Moghulistan during the first half of the 15th century, entered Persian records written within living memory with the events of 1503–04, and over the next three hundred years forged the modern people of the Tian Shan. Yet on the upper Yenisei, the older Kyrgyz polity did not disappear: it endured under Oirat and Dzungar overlordship, collided with Russian expansion in the 17th century, and ended only when Dzungar forces deported much of its population in 1703.

That three-century overlap is one of the sharpest constraints in the entire puzzle. It rules out the cleanest popular image—that the Yenisei Kyrgyz simply packed up their tents, migrated south-west en masse, and became the Kyrgyz of Kyrgyzstan. Some clans moved south by stages across centuries; the bulk of the northern population remained in the Minusinsk Basin and became the core of the modern **Khakas**, while a deported fragment carried the name all the way to Manchuria as the **Fuyu Kyrgyz**. Following the northern branch to its close in 1703 also disposes of an influential 18th-century theory claiming that the Tian Shan Kyrgyz were deportees from that very upheaval.

#### 6.1 The "Kyrgyz Land" and Its *Kyshtyms*

Between the collapse of the Yuan dynasty in 1368 and the arrival of Russian tribute collectors in the early 1600s stretch two and a half thinly documented centuries on the upper Yenisei. What clues survive point to institutional resilience rather than collapse. Once Mongol authority lifted, V. V. Ushnitsky notes, Kyrgyz descendants "re-established five principalities that had existed during the pre-Mongol period" [121]—overlapping in time with Ugechi-Kashka's brief 1399 Kyrgyz dynasty over the Oirats (Chapter 5.2) [56]. On that reading, the principalities Russian clerks recorded in the early 17th century—**Altyr, Altysar, and Ezer** [17]—inherited an older administrative order, explaining why a 14th-century Persian historian [87] and 17th-century Russian scribes [122] describe the exact same structure: a Kyrgyz ruling stratum extracting tribute from dependent communities.

Russian colonists first encountered the Yenisei Kyrgyz near **Tomsk in 1606** and **Krasnoyarsk in 1628**, naming the principalities Ezer, Altyr, and Altysar [17]. By **1634**, the Kyrgyz were rendering tribute simultaneously to the Mongol Altan Khans and to the Russian fort at Krasnoyarsk, leading Russian officials to call them people of "two states" [17].

In V. K. Chertykov's archival reconstruction, the **"Kyrgyz Land"** (*Kyrgyzskaya zemlya*) functioned as a cluster of principalities (*ulus*) whose Kyrgyz aristocracy collected fur and goods from dependent tribes known as ***kyshtyms*** [122]. During the **1660s**, **Prince Irenak** began consolidating these rival principalities into a tighter front [122]. Three competing powers pressed on them from every side [122]:

- **Muscovite Russia**, whose suzerainty Kyrgyz princes acknowledged "purely nominally," oscillating between truces and frontier raids over who held the right to tax the *kyshtyms*;
- **The Altan Khans of north-western Mongolia**—a Right Wing Khalkha line founded by Sholoi Ubashi Khungtaiji (not an Oirat dynasty) ruling the Khotogoids around the Uvs basin [105]—whose heavy exactions in the early 17th century drove Kyrgyz princes to ally with the Dzungars against them; and
- **The Dzungar Khanate**, under whose overlordship the Yenisei Kyrgyz passed once Dzungar armies defeated the Altan Khan in **1666** [122].

Around the rim of these Yenisei principalities lived tributary communities called ***kyshtyms***—the same arrangement Rashid al-Din had already recorded under the term *kishtyms* around 1300 [87] and Russian tax books documented under the identical word more than three centuries later [122]. Crucially, contemporary observers still counted those peripheral tributaries "as part of the Kyrgyz people"; as V. V. Ushnitsky concludes, "the distinction between kyshtyms and Kyrgyz was not ethnic or linguistic but rather social" [121]. That archival finding reinforces what other disciplines show from the southern end of the corridor: on the Yenisei, just as later in the Tian Shan, *Kyrgyz* marked political standing rather than a closed biological lineage. A polity that counted Yeniseian- and Samoyedic-speaking dependants within its Kyrgyz body politic was exactly the kind of political structure whose name could travel far beyond Siberia without carrying its local gene pool along.

How cohesive the 17th-century Kyrgyz Land remained is still debated. N. Kozmin judged the confederation "indeed a state, but one in decline," whereas V. I. Ogorodnikov contended that the old Kyrgyz state "survived into the 17th century only as fragments" [121]. Muscovite diplomats, meanwhile, routinely addressed its chief as the **"Kirbitsky Tsar"** [121]—an inflated title telling us more about the royal counterparts Russian court protocol expected to find at the negotiating table than about actual state capacity on the Yenisei.

#### 6.2 1703 and Its Aftermath: Khakas and Fuyu Kyrgyz

In **1703**, Dzungar forces moved into the Minusinsk Basin and drove a large share of the Yenisei Kyrgyz and their *kyshtyms* south-west to **Dzungaria**. A contemporary Russian estimate counts roughly **3,000 households** [122]; M. A. Chertykov's 2013 study puts the number at **15,000–20,000 people** [9]. That single deportation broke the Kyrgyz Land permanently. Muscovite forts absorbed the basin, and after **1707** the remaining Kyrgyz clans "lost their socio-economic primacy" [122].

For nearly two centuries, historians tried to bridge the 1,650-kilometre mystery with that exact date. In the 18th and 19th centuries, Miller, Fischer, Klaproth, and Levshin argued that the Dzungars had relocated the Yenisei Kyrgyz straight into the Tian Shan during the 17th and 18th centuries—a theory now abandoned by specialists [5], yet still echoed in major reference works such as *Encyclopaedia Britannica*, which continues to state that Yenisei Kyrgyz "moved to the Semirechye" in 1703 [2]. Two independent archives bury that hypothesis. Russian records show the 1703 deportees taken to Dzungaria rather than Semirechye [122], with no record of them joining the Tian Shan Kyrgyz—and Persian chronicles already document Kyrgyz entrenched in the Tian Shan two full centuries before 1703, beginning with the events of 1503–04 [14] [15].

Back in the Minusinsk Basin, the 1703 removal left the underlying population standing. V. K. Chertykov emphasizes that the deportation did not break up the "forming Khakas people" [122]: surviving Kyrgyz clans and former *kyshtyms* merged in place, entering tsarist administration as the **Minusinsk** or **Abakan Tatars** [123]. Today their descendants, the **Khakas**, speak traditional dialects—Kacha, Sagai, Beltir, and Kyzyl among them—classified within **South Siberian Turkic** [8].

Yet their everyday self-name is ***tadar***—their rendering of "Tatar"—a label that signals no kinship with the Volga or Crimean Tatars, whose languages belong to the **Kipchak** branch alongside Kyrgyz and Kazakh rather than to South Siberian Turkic [8] [13]. In a landmark **1992** debate in *Etnograficheskoe obozrenie*, three specialists clashed over how the names *tadar* and *Khakas* took hold—while all three agreed that the Yenisei Kyrgyz stand among the ancestors of the Khakas:

- **I. L. Kyzlasov** traced *tadar* to Russian colonial administration: having learned "Tatar" during the Golden Horde era and applied it to Volga Turkic speakers, Russians extended it across Siberia "on the principle of linguistic similarity," fixing it bureaucratically [1]. On "Khakas," he held that *Kyrgyz* named "only the ruling clan" of a broader Yenisei state while *Khakas* designated its wider population in Chinese records, making both "parts of one and the same people" [1].
- **V. Ya. Butanaev** dated the everyday use of *tadar* to the early 19th century, calling it "an echo of the tsarist administrative system" that "displaced the earlier historical name" (a term equally used by Shors, Teleuts, and northern Altaians), while showing that Khakas folklore and place names preserve ***khyrgys*** (Kyrgyz) and ***khoorai*** beneath it [7]. He noted that the modern ethnonym **"Khakas"** entered political life at the national congresses of **1917–1918** led by **S. D. Moinagashev**, who revived a Chinese-recorded form [7].
- **S. G. Klyashtorny**, following the sinologist Yakhontov, countered that "Khakas" was simply an inaccurate Chinese transcription of "Kyrgyz," and traced *tadar* instead back to Ugechi-Kashka's 1399 Kyrgyz-led Oirat state, which adopted "Dadan" (Tatar) as its official name [56]—an argument I. L. Kyzlasov rejected because Ugechi-Kashka's dynasty lasted barely forty years [1].

V. V. Ushnitsky supplies the concrete social mechanism linking those three positions. Under Russian tribute pressures, the Kyrgyz ruling stratum and principalities broke apart; the administrative districts that replaced them took the clan names of the former *kyshtyms*; and after the 1703 Dzungar deportation, "a process of kyshtymization occurred," during which "the term 'Tatars,' imposed from the outside, became widespread" [121]. Kyrgyz who later drifted back from Dzungaria "reintegrated into new ethno-territorial groups" rather than restoring the old principalities [121]. Ushnitsky's verdict is unambiguous: "Modern Khakas are undeniably descendants of the Yenisei Kyrgyz in both political and historical terms" [121].

Seen through *kyshtymization*, Butanaev's observation that *tadar* was a late replacement gains both a date and a trigger: once the Kyrgyz aristocratic tier was shattered and deported, the tributary population supplied the names and demographic core of the society that followed [7] [121]. Social history and genetics point the same way here. If the Yenisei elite was broken and carried off while the tributary *kyshtyms* stayed in the basin, the surviving Khakas ought to preserve heavy ancestry from the basin's older Yeniseian- and Samoyedic-speaking populations—and modern Khakas genomes show exactly that, carrying both the **Cis-Baikal Yeniseian ancestry** of the upper Yenisei [28] and roughly **50% haplogroup N** on the paternal line [124], signatures largely absent in the Tian Shan Kyrgyz (examined in Chapter 10.2–10.3). Because no genomes from the Yenisei Kyrgyz elite itself have been sequenced, this agreement is a consistency rather than a proof. The Khakas inherited the Kyrgyz Land on the Yenisei, descending primarily from its tributary subjects alongside its remaining clans.

One final branch of the 1703 deportation proves even more revealing as a linguistic control. Part of the Yenisei population carried into Dzungaria in 1703 was deported twice more—relocated eastward to **Manchuria** in **1733** and **1755–1757** [9]. Their descendants in **Fuyu County** (Heilongjiang Province, north-east China) have kept the ethnonym *Kyrgyz* for nearly three centuries, yet their speech exposes their exact northern origin. Glottolog classifies **Fuyu Kirghiz** as a dialect of **Khakas** inside South Siberian Turkic [8], and M. A. Chertykov's 2013 study finds that its lexicon "corresponds to the vocabulary of common Khakas"—closest to the **Sagai** dialect—describing the Fuyu Kyrgyz as "an isolated group of the Khakas" [9].

Linguistically, Fuyu Kyrgyz stands at the exact same remove from Tian Shan Kyrgyz as Khakas does: both descend from Common Turkic—whose divergence Bayesian phylolinguistics places around the 5th century CE (95% probability interval: 7 BCE to 809 CE)—and sit on separate branches of the family, South Siberian versus Kipchak, even though Tian Shan Kyrgyz retains pronunciation and grammar isoglosses with South Siberian Turkic widely read as tracing a South Siberian origin [13]. Cut off from other Turkic speakers for more than 250 years, Fuyu speech absorbed substantial Mongolian and Chinese influence; the community counted just **1,473 people in 2002**, and Chertykov describes the language as nearing extinction [9].

The Fuyu Kyrgyz leave an unmistakable benchmark as the narrative turns south. When actual Yenisei Kyrgyz communities were uprooted in 1703 and transported thousands of kilometres away, they kept the Kyrgyz name—and kept speaking Khakas. Three centuries earlier, far to the south-west across the Altai and Dzungaria, the name *Kyrgyz* had already entered the valleys of Moghulistan—where a wholly different linguistic and demographic transformation was beginning.

### Chapter 7: The Branch That Moved — Forging the Tian Shan Kyrgyz (c. 1430–1876)

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 7**
>
> - **Arrival shifts from retrospective memory to living memory and eyewitness record between 1430 and 1547:** Retrospective Qing (*Xiyu zhi*, ~1430s) and Persian notices precede Mirza Haidar's record of 1503–04 (written in 1547, within living memory), which treats the Tian Shan Kyrgyz as a rebellious, still non-Muslim offshoot of the Moghuls, echoed from Fergana by Akhsikandi's *Majmūʿ al-tawārīkh*.
> - **Both steppe push and Silk Road pull drew herders to the Tian Shan:** Rather than retreating into an isolated refuge, pastoralists balanced defensible snow-fed alpine *jailoo* pastures with structural dependence on lowland oasis markets (Chüy, Fergana, Kashgaria)—acting as armed kingmakers in 17th-century Yarkand and Khoja civil wars down to 1705.
> - **Open clans welded three historical strata into one people:** Roughly 35 patrilineal *uruu* across the *ong*, *sol*, and *ichkilik* wings—governed by seven-generation exogamy (*jeti ata*) and schematic *sanjyra* genealogies that admitted outsiders—merged resident Tian Shan tribes (Türgesh, Karluk, Chigil), Altai–Yenisei newcomers, and Mongol/Kipchak clans.
> - **Kyrgyz and Kazakh politics grew up side by side:** Emerging in the Chüy valley in the exact same decades (1459–1466), both peoples shared Kipchak clans and acknowledged common khans (Haq-Nazar, Tauekel, Ishim, Tauke) across two centuries of alternating alliance and pasture rivalry.
> - **Dzungar wars, Qing markets, Kokand, and Russia closed the pre-modern era:** Dzungar pressure drove large Kyrgyz groups into the Fergana rim and Pamirs before the Qing shattered Dzungaria (1755–57) and opened Kashgar/Ili frontier trade; Atake Biy sent envoys to St. Petersburg (1785–87), Kokand imposed rule (1825–30), and Russian annexation followed in 1855–76.

Between the 15th and 18th centuries, the Kyrgyz people as it exists today coalesced across the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay. Incoming groups from the Altai and Oirat frontier merged with resident mountain Turkic clans, Moghul nomads, and Kipchak tribes—unfolding in the same valleys and the exact same decades that gave birth to the Kazakh Khanate.

#### 7.1 Entering the High Pastures: Moghulistan and Kashgaria

The earliest retrospective clue to Kyrgyz movement inside the Tian Shan comes from an unlikely archive: an unpublished Qing gazetteer compiled between 1763 and 1770, the ***Xiyu zhi*** ("Description of the Western Lands," preserved in the Russian State Library, Moscow, Fond 274, No. 287, Book 1) [14]. Drawing the countback from T. O. Beishenaliev alongside the 2003 Bishkek Russian translation (*MIKK*, Vol. 2, p. 200), R. A. Abdumanapov notes that part of the Kyrgyz fled turmoil "about 330 years ago" and "found refuge in the mountains" [14] (a separate later passage in the *Xiyu zhi* stating that "220 years ago" the Kyrgyz negotiated peace and split into two equal divisions refers instead to a mid-16th-century political division, around 1543–1550). Counting back 330 years from 1763–1770 puts the earlier flight around **1433–1440**—the first half of the 15th century—which Abdumanapov reads as a relocation from the eastern spurs of the Tian Shan into its central basins [14]. Drawn from a Qing tradition independent of Persian histories, the notice records a move *within* the mountain system rather than testifying directly to a journey out of Siberia. Meanwhile, in Mahmud ibn Wali's *Bahr al-asrar*, Kyrgyz and Oirat groups appear in Semirechye by the mid-15th century under Esen Buqa II, pressing on Moghulistan's agricultural districts [14].

All 15th-century notices of Kyrgyz in Moghulistan share one crucial limitation: every one of them is retrospective. The *Xiyu zhi* was assembled three centuries after the flight it dates; the Persian narratives come from Mirza Haidar Dughlat in **1547** and Mahmud ibn Wali's *Bahr al-asrar* in the 1630s–40s [14] [15]. The earliest episode recorded within living memory is the appointment of a Moghul prince as ruler of the Kyrgyz in **1503–04**, reported by Mirza Haidar [14] [15]. Kyrgyz are therefore *reported* retrospectively in the Tian Shan from the first half and middle of the 15th century (~1430s in the *Xiyu zhi*; mid-15th century in the *Bahr al-asrar*) and *recorded* there within living memory from 1503–04, with Mirza Haidar's own eyewitness testimony beginning in the 1510s—a gap that separates plausible arrival from firm attestation.

The *Xiyu zhi*'s phrase—"found refuge in the mountains"—also deserves scrutiny, because alpine pastures were never barren backwaters left alone by stronger empires. Studying the Kyrgyz and Wakhi of the Pamirs, Hermann Kreutzmann challenges "the thesis that mountain areas are regions of refuge," showing that highland pastoralists stayed woven into regional markets, caravan trade, and state politics throughout [125]. What drew herders upward, Kreutzmann demonstrates, was "the availability of water stored in glaciers and snow," driving movements shaped by "the search for suitable pastures and low outside interference" [125]. That geographic logic sits squarely atop what anthropologist Anatoly Khazanov identified as the structural non-autarky of pastoral nomadism: herds supply meat, milk, wool, and hides, yet pastoralists depend on settled markets for grain, cotton cloth, tea, and oasis goods [126]. Once Mongol and Oirat shocks dislodged mobile groups from the Altai and Irtysh, the Tian Shan exerted a powerful southward pull—sitting as far south as vertical alpine herding could extend, combining snow-fed, defensible high pastures directly above the Silk Road markets of Chüy, Fergana, and Kashgaria.

Nor could mountain pastoralists seal themselves off behind snow peaks. Because their summer *jailoo* at 2,500–3,500 metres functioned only as half of a vertical cycle paired with drier lowland winter *kyshtoo* (Chapter 4.1) [70] [71] [72], every autumn brought herds back into contact with lowland states and markets. Two further facts fit a seasonal, market-dependent mountain economy rather than an isolated refuge: even the remotest Pamir Kyrgyz cultivate no crops and trade livestock for flour and rice [70], and modern Kyrgyz genomes lack classical high-altitude genetic adaptations such as *EPAS1* [42] (examined in Chapter 10.2)—consistent with seasonal rather than permanent life at altitude, though not by itself evidence of how recently Kyrgyz ancestors reached the mountains.

Just as Kyrgyz clans were consolidating in those valleys, the neighbouring Kazakh polity took shape on the exact same frontier. In **1459**, the Moghul khan **Esen Buqa II** (d. 1462—distinct from the earlier Chagatayid khan Esen Buqa I, r. 1309–c. 1320) sheltered the fugitive Jochid sultans **Janibek and Kerey**, who had broken with the Uzbek khan Abu'l-Khayr, granting them winter grounds at **Kozy-Bashy** along the Chüy River [112] [127]. Mahmud ibn Wali's *Bahr al-asrar* notes that their followers promptly "raided and plundered the tribes of the Kalmyks and Kyrgyz" [14]. Mirza Haidar dates the birth of their state—the **Kazakh Khanate**—to 870 AH (**1465–66**) [127]; once Abu'l-Khayr died in 1468–69, the sultans led their tribes back out onto the Kipchak steppe [14]. Kyrgyz and Kazakh political histories thus open side by side, in the same valleys and the same decade.

For the early 16th century, living memory and eyewitness testimony finally replace retrospective tradition. Writing in **1547**, the Moghul prince **Mirza Haidar Dughlat** (*Tarikh-i Rashidi*)—born around 1500, and a witness to Sultan-Said's campaigns from the 1510s—looked straight at the Kyrgyz who had seized his dynasty's mountain heartland [15]:

> "Moghulistan has been seized by the Uzbeg and the Kirghiz."
>
> "Although the Kirghiz belong to the tribe of Moghul, on account of their repeated rebellions against the Khakans, [they have] become separated from them. All the Moghuls have become Musulmans, but the Kirghiz are still infidels, and hence their hostility to the Moghuls."

Mirza Haidar's "Uzbeg" almost certainly includes the Uzbek-Kazakhs of Janibek and Kerey [127]. His assessment of the Kyrgyz carries immense weight: to a well-informed Moghul aristocrat of the 1540s, the Tian Shan Kyrgyz looked like a rebellious offshoot of the Moghuls themselves rather than exotic Siberian migrants—and they had not yet adopted Islam.

Political milestones now lock onto the calendar [14] [15]. In **1503–04**, the Moghul prince **Sultan-Khalil** was proclaimed ruler (*hakim*) of the Kyrgyz, the event Vasily Bartold treated as the first solid attestation of Kyrgyz in their modern homeland [5]. Around **1514**, Kyrgyz tribes led by **Muhammad Kyrgyz**—whom some historians equate with the legendary right-wing ancestor **Tagay biy**—backed **Sultan-Said Khan's** return to power; that year Sultan-Said wrested Kashgar from its Dughlat governor and founded the **Yarkand Khanate** (*Mamlakat-i Yarkand*, 1514–1705) [14] [15] [112]. Soon the alliance fractured. Sultan-Said routed the Kyrgyz at **Barskoon** on Issyk-Kul and kept Muhammad Kyrgyz imprisoned until **1533**, in campaigns where Mirza Haidar praised the khan's bowmanship "in the battles he fought against the Kirghiz" [15].

An independent early-16th-century witness confirms the Kyrgyz presence from the opposite flank of the range. Composed in Persian in the Fergana Valley by **Sayf al-Din Akhsikandi** (and completed by his son Nur Muhammad), the Sufi chronicle ***Majmūʿ al-tawārīkh*** already incorporates *Manas* traditions alongside Kyrgyz holy war against the Kalmaks [16] [119] [120]. It locates the Kyrgyz in northern Moghulistan [14] while styling Manas and his kin Kipchaks from Karkara (anchoring Manas's birth in the Karkara–Talas region) [5] [119] [120]; independently, a six-line fragment of *Manas* survives in a Persian historical work of the late 15th or early 16th century [114].

Nor did the Kyrgyz fade from Kashgarian politics after Muhammad Kyrgyz's release in 1533. As Sultan-Said's Yarkand Khanate splintered among rival princes at Kashgar, Yarkand, and Aksu [112], **Shah Mahmud Churas's *Tarikh*** (written in Yarkand around 1670) and the UNESCO *History of Civilizations of Central Asia* show Kyrgyz tribes acting as armed kingmakers throughout the 17th century [128]. Churas records Kyrgyz communities encamped around **Kashgar, Yarkand, and Barchuk (Maralbexi)** [128]—remarkably, the exact town Yu. N. Esin proposed for al-Idrisi's medieval Kyrgyz possession (Chapter 4.1) [63], confirming how reliably the Aksu–Barchuk rim drew Tian Shan herders down across the Bedel Pass, whether or not any unbroken presence bridged the five intervening centuries. During the 17th-century struggle between rival Naqshbandi Sufi orders—the **Aq Taghliq** ("White Mountain") Khojas of Kashgar under Muhammad Yusuf and his son Apaq Khoja, and the **Qara Taghliq** ("Black Mountain") Khojas of Yarkand—Kyrgyz warriors backed both Chagatayid khans and Khoja factions [128]. When Ismail Khan drove out Apaq Khoja around **1670**, Apaq appealed to the 5th Dalai Lama and Galdan's Dzungars, who conquered Altishahr in **1678–1680** and installed client rulers [112] [128]. After Apaq Khoja died in **1694**, renewed civil war pitted Yarkand's court, White Mountain Khojas, local *begs*, and the **Kyrgyz** against one another: Kyrgyz forces struck Yarkand and captured **Akbash Khan** before Dzungar troops intervened in **1705**, ending the Chagatayid dynasty permanently [128]. That deep 17th-century entanglement in Kashgaria also opened one of the main channels by which Naqshbandi Sufism entered Kyrgyz tribal life (Chapter 9.3).

#### 7.2 Open Clans and the Kazakh Partnership

How did incoming northern and Altai bands weld themselves together with resident Tian Shan nomads, Moghul clans, and Kipchak horsemen into a single society? The answer is embedded directly in Kyrgyz tribal architecture. The Kyrgyz organized themselves into roughly **35 top-level patrilineal divisions (*uruu*)** arrayed across three unequal wings [129]: the **right wing (*ong*)**, the **left wing (*sol*)**, and the ***ichkilik***. S. M. Abramzon split the right wing into two overarching branches—**Tagay** and **Adigine**—observed that the *ichkilik* confederation mirrored the same internal right-and-left pairing, and concluded that *Kyrgyz* named "a union of forty tribes" assembled through socio-political alliance rather than biological descent [46]. *(The two distinct Kipchak waves that reshaped both these confederations and their speech are traced in detail in Chapter 8.1.)*

Vasily Bartold and S. M. Abramzon resolved the resulting people into three overlapping historical strata [6] [78]:

- A **Tian Shan layer**, rooted in the ancient Saka and Wusun and the early medieval Turkic tribes of Semirechye and Issyk-Kul (**Karluks, Türgesh, Chigils, and Yaghma**);
- A **southern Siberian layer** from the Altai, Irtysh, and Yenisei, which carried the ethnonym *Kyrgyz* alongside Kipchak and South Siberian linguistic and cultural elements; and
- A **Mongol and post-Mongol layer** of the 13th to 15th centuries, incorporating **Kipchak, Naiman, Kereit, and Kongirat** tribes together with the clans of Moghulistan.

Abramzon hunted for that oldest Tian Shan stratum directly on the clan map. Because the **Bugu** tribe occupied the exact grazing around Issyk-Kul, Barskoon, Karkara, and the Tekes valley once held by the **Chigils**, Abramzon inferred direct continuity between the lake's medieval Chigil–Karluk population and the Bugu—pointing as well to the veneration of the horned Mother Deer (*Bugu-Ene*) centred on Issyk-Kul [6]. More tellingly, early medieval ethnonyms—**Törgösh (Türgesh), Karluk, Az, and Kipchak**—survive intact as Kyrgyz *uruu*, while Chigil lineages folded into Tian Shan and Fergana Kyrgyz subdivisions [6].

Those two halves of Abramzon's proof do not carry equal weight. Incoming pastoralists seize the richest alpine-lake grazing simply because it is the finest grazing, and readily adopt local shrines standing on the shore whether or not a single Chigil ancestor lived in their tents. Surviving tribal ethnonyms offer firmer footing, since a tribe's name has no ecological reason to linger in a valley unless bearers of that name remained there. Settling how much biological ancestry the Chigils contributed awaits ancient genomes from 10th–14th-century Issyk-Kul cemeteries, which have not yet been sequenced.

In daily practice, three tribal institutions held this composite world together. Patrilineal membership in an ***uruu*** anchored a person's social identity [129], while clusters of yurts camped as an ***ayil*** working pastureland assigned to its lineage [130]. Every man was expected to recite his **"seven fathers" (*jeti ata*)**—"the basic personal genealogy"—enforcing strict exogamy prohibiting marriage within seven generations [129]. Prior to Soviet rule, political authority and land allocation flowed through chiefs known as ***manaps*** [130], a rank—alongside *kul* ("slave")—that Abramzon documented most thickly among the Sarybagysh [46].

Crucially, belonging to a Kyrgyz clan was never locked strictly to bloodlines. Oral genealogies (***sanjyra***) were "highly schematic accounts of patrilineal descent and history" [129], and outsiders "can even join a dominant uruu in some villages if they demonstrate sufficient commitment" [129]. The Oirat-descended **Sart-Kalmaks** of eastern Issyk-Kul—arriving from Xinjiang between 1863 and 1894 and already regarded by most neighbours as one of the large Kyrgyz lineages (Chapter 1.2)—offer a documented modern replay of that exact absorption mechanism [10] [11]. An open clan system built to graft allies onto shared genealogies explains how a prestigious political name could spread across diverse tribes without requiring demographic replacement.

Outside their own valleys, the Kyrgyz spent the 16th and 17th centuries in tight, volatile partnership with the **Kazakhs**. Both peoples coalesced in the same decades across overlapping frontiers, shared Kipchak clan names [5], and repeatedly acknowledged the same khans [14]:

- **Haq-Nazar** (1570s) is titled "khan of the Kazakhs and Kyrgyz" in both the *Haft Iqlim* and the *Bahr al-asrar*;
- Kyrgyz detachments campaigned in **Tauekel's** army during the 1590s;
- The Kyrgyz raised **Ishim** as khan in the early 17th century; and
- Under **Tauke**, the Kyrgyz formed a distinct "Kyrgyz alash," which Chokan Valikhanov records Tauke governed through their *biy* **Tiyes** [14].

R. A. Abdumanapov stresses that these joint fronts alternated steadily with sharp fights over grazing [14]. Yet two centuries of shared khans, neighbouring winter quarters, and overlapping clans left Kyrgyz and Kazakhs so intertwined that disentangling where they converge—and where they sharply part company—requires the linguistic and genetic cross-examinations of Act III.

#### 7.3 Dzungar Hammer, Kokand Anvil, Russian Advance (1680–1876)

Across the late 17th and first half of the 18th century, the regrouped western Mongols of the **Dzungar Khanate** drove a wedge through Central Asia, hammering Kyrgyz pastures and pushing large populations south-west into the **Fergana Valley** rim [20]. Because the 16th-century Fergana chronicle still places the bulk of the Kyrgyz in northern Moghulistan [14]—even while documenting early Fergana contacts [16]—the Dzungar wars represent the main wave carrying Kyrgyz tribes into the southern alpine arc. Russian frontier intelligence of **1707** calls the Kyrgyz ***Burut***, adopting the Oirat exonym [17], while shared existential threat ensured that Kazakh–Kyrgyz pasture disputes were "often replaced by periods of military alliances against the invasions of Oirat rulers" [14].

The Dzungar threat ended abruptly in mid-century. *Encyclopaedia Britannica* summarizes the wars as running "until China's defeat of the Dzungar leader Amursana in 1757" [2]—compressing two distinct stages: Qing armies shattered the Dzungar state by **1755**, after which **Amursana**, who had aided the Qing conquest, mounted an anti-Qing revolt finally crushed in **1757** [131].

By those centuries, Kyrgyz pastures already spanned far beyond the future borders of Kyrgyzstan. To the east lay the communities of Xinjiang's **Kizilsu Kyrgyz Autonomous Prefecture**—including Ulugqat County on the modern Kyrgyzstan border, whose population remains virtually identical genetically to Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz (an $F_{ST}$ genetic distance of just 0.13%) [42]. To the south-west, Kyrgyz herders had moved into the Little and Great **Afghan Pamirs** beginning in the 16th century [70]. Late-19th-century imperial boundary commissions left those high-valley communities de facto Afghan citizens, later joined by refugees fleeing Osh in **1916** [70]. In **July 1978**, roughly 1,300 Pamir Kyrgyz fled into Pakistan [70] (a Turkish thesis puts the group near 1,500 [132]); Turkey resettled them under a special law in **1982** at **Ulupamir** in Van province, which counted 1,720 residents in 2012 [133] [132], while roughly 300 families remained in the Afghan Pamirs in 2021 [70].

Once the Dzungar Khanate collapsed and Qing armies suppressed the Altishahr Khojas in **1758–59**, northern and southern Kyrgyz chiefs dispatched tributary envoys to Beijing, entering a loose nominal suzerainty (*fanshu* 藩屬) under the Qing court while maintaining full internal autonomy across their mountain pastures [2] [128]. Nominal submission brought tangible commercial rewards, opening regulated Qing frontier markets at Kashgar and Ili where Kyrgyz pastoralists traded sturdy mountain horses and sheep for oasis cotton cloth (*böz* and *mata*), brick tea, grain, and diplomatic silks [134] [128]. In the wake of the Dzungar ruin, Rysbek Alimov argues, scattered Western Mongol groups also folded into the Kyrgyz through language shift [95].

Removal of the Dzungar buffer immediately reignited steppe rivalry over Semirechye: Chinese records confirm that the Kazakh ruler **Ablai Khan** struck Kyrgyz territory in **1774** [135]. Caught between Kazakh pressure and shifting imperial frontiers, **Atake Biy**, chief of the northern Sarybagysh tribe, initiated direct diplomatic contact with St. Petersburg seventy years before annexation. In **1785**, he dispatched envoys **Abdurahman Kuchakov (Quchaq uulu)** and **Shergazy** to Russia seeking trade security and protection, and **Empress Catherine II** personally received Abdurahman in **1787** [128].

Before Russia pushed south in force, however, the regional power that moved into the Tian Shan came from Fergana. Between **1825 and 1830**, **Muhammad Ali**, khan of **Kokand**, subjugated the Kyrgyz and built frontier garrisons including **Pishpek**, the future Bishkek [2]. Under Kokandian authority, Kyrgyz *manaps* invited Uzbek mullahs into the highlands, accelerating the spread of formal Islam [20]. Yet northern clans faced outside encroachments bitterly split among themselves: between **1835 and 1858**, the **Sarybagysh** and the **Bugu** waged a "fratricidal war," each faction alternately courting Kokandian or Russian backing, while southern Kyrgyz revolted repeatedly against Kokand on their own [2].

In **1855**, the Bugu submitted formally to the Russian Empire, which constructed the fort of **Aksu** at their request in **1863** [2]. Two decades later, in **1875–76**, Russia annexed the Khanate of Kokand and with it the southern Kyrgyz [20]. As *Britannica*'s historians observe, "the Kyrgyz tribes thus entered the modern era divided, harassed by Russians and Kokandians alike" [2].

With Russian garrisons holding both Issyk-Kul and Fergana in 1876, the written chronicle reaches its end. Across two millennia and 1,650 kilometres, texts have traced an ethnonym from Han scouts on the northern steppe through the Yenisei khaganates, the Oirat crucible, and Moghulistan into modern Kyrgyzstan. Yet chronicles track rulers, raids, and political labels—never the full biology or everyday speech of the people living inside those names. To test what actually happened beneath the historical record, Act III turns to three independent forensic archives, beginning with the deepest structural fossil the Kyrgyz still carry in their own mouths: their language.

## Act III: Three Forensic Tests & Synthesis

### Chapter 8: Forensic Test I — Layers of a Language

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 8**
>
> - **Kyrgyz combines Altai structural bones with Kipchak vocabulary:** While Bayesian basic vocabulary groups Kyrgyz with Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Nogai in South Kipchak, deep phonology and grammar pair it most closely with **Southern Altai** (Baskakov's Kyrgyz–Kipchak subgroup), on a separate branch from Khakas and Fuyu Kyrgyz.
> - **Two Kipchak waves reshaped speech roughly four centuries apart:** An early Irtysh–Altai Kimak–Kipchak stage (9th–13th centuries) shifted South Siberian *z*-type speech to *y*-type Kipchak, followed by an Eastern Kipchak wave in the Tian Shan and Fergana (13th–17th centuries), while Kyrgyz avoided the late-completing western Kipchak–Nogai `*š > s` and `*č > š` shifts.
> - **Four structural fingerprints anchor Kyrgyz to the Altai–Sayan zone:** Full labial vowel harmony with 12-form plural/accusative/genitive (vs. 8-form dative/locative/ablative) paradigms, the habitual *-chu* participle, secondary long vowels (*too* vs. Kazakh *tau*), and the everyday root *but* ("foot/leg," shared with Southern Altai rather than Khakas *azax* or Kazakh *ayaq*).
