> - **Layered Mongolic and late Perso-Arabic loans map directly onto history:** Specialists debate the total Mongolic tally (Alimov's >3,000 ethnographic count vs. Washington's ~200 strict phonological filter), yet both confirm intensive 13th–14th-century Middle Mongol contact—while Perso-Arabic loans (Arabisms alone make up 5.75% of Yudakhin's dictionary) entered late and secondhand via Uzbek, Uyghur, Tajik, and Kazakh, remaining scarce among northern tribes in the 1850s.
> - **Northern and southern dialects mirror recent political orbits:** Oirat and Kazakh contact shaped northern speech (later codified as the 1920s literary standard) while Fergana Uzbek and Perso-Arabic contact shaped the south; post-Mongol restructuring of alpine pastures, Kipchak reinforcements, and (most plausibly) an expanding prestigious patriline explain how Altai-shaped Kipchak prevailed across the range.

Chronicles record who claimed power; a spoken language records who actually lived, traded, and raised children together. Modern Kyrgyz is a **Kipchak** language whose closest living relative is **Southern Altai**. It does not descend in a straight line from the Old Turkic cut onto Yenisei stones, and it sits on a separate branch of Turkic from Khakas. Layered atop its grammar and phonology are one of the largest Mongolic loanword strata in the Turkic family—including items otherwise confined to Siberian Turkic—and a thinner, late Perso-Arabic vocabulary acquired secondhand through Turkic and Tajik neighbours.

#### 8.1 Altai Bones, Kipchak Flesh

Even classifying Kyrgyz has divided Turkologists, and that scholarly disagreement mirrors the historical puzzle directly. In the Russian linguistic tradition founded by **Nikolai Baskakov (1958)** [136]—maintained by **I. V. Kormushin** in the *Great Russian Encyclopedia* and echoed in **Claus Schönig's (1997–1998)** reassessments [137]—Kyrgyz and **Southern Altai** form a dedicated **Kyrgyz–Kipchak** subgroup of their own. On that tree, the closest living kin of Kyrgyz is not Kazakh, but the speech of the Altai Mountains.

Lexical phylogenetics draws a different branch. Using Bayesian analysis of basic vocabulary, **Savelyev and Robbeets (2020)** group Kyrgyz alongside Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Nogai inside **South Kipchak** [13]. Yet they immediately qualify that tree, noting that Kyrgyz shares diagnostic sound and grammar features (*isoglosses*) with South Siberian Turkic, "particularly with the South Altay dialects" [13]. The two trees are not truly at war: core vocabulary tracks centuries of Kipchak cohabitation in the Tian Shan, while deep phonology and morphology preserve the older Altai framework underneath. **Jonathan Washington** captures the exact same split, noting "strong parallels to Kazakh" (sharpest in the Talas dialects) alongside his verdict that the varieties "most similar to Kyrgyz are the southern dialects of Altay," concluding that Kyrgyz "essentially belongs to the Kypchak (Northwestern) branch," with early affiliation or convergence toward South Siberian Turkic [12].

**Table 8.1: Kyrgyz and Related Turkic Languages**

| Language | Classification | Relation to Kyrgyz |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Southern Altai (Altai-kizhi, Telengit, Teleut) | Kipchak, alongside Kyrgyz [8] | Closest relative; shared isoglosses [12] [13] |
| Kazakh, Karakalpak, Nogai | South Kipchak by basic vocabulary [13] | Very close; "strong parallels," especially in Talas Kyrgyz [12] |
| Old Turkic of the Yenisei and Orkhon inscriptions | Old Turkic [8] | Written by the Yenisei Kyrgyz; not the direct ancestor of modern Kyrgyz |
| Khakas, Shor, Fuyu Kirghiz | South Siberian Turkic [8] [13] | Different branch of Common Turkic, related through a common ancestor of about the 5th century CE [13]; spoken by descendants of the Yenisei population |

Read alongside one another, the historical reconstructions of Petrov, Abdumanapov, and Mokeev resolve the Kipchak remaking of Kyrgyz into **two waves** beginning roughly four centuries apart [5] [53]:

- **First wave — the Irtysh–Altai Kimak-Kipchak stage (9th–13th centuries):** Yenisei Kyrgyz bands moving into the western and southern Altai after 840 lived alongside a numerically larger **Kimak-Kipchak** population. Across generations of cohabitation, their everyday speech shifted from South Siberian (*z*-type) Turkic to an Altai-Kipchak (*y*-type) vernacular, even while the Kyrgyz political name and titles (*inal*, *idi*) endured [4] [5] [53].
- **Second wave — the Tian Shan and Fergana Eastern Kipchak stage (13th–17th centuries):** During the Mongol and post-Mongol centuries, **Eastern Kipchak** tribes from the Jochid and Chagatai spheres entered the forming Kyrgyz confederation across Moghulistan and the Fergana rim [5] [14]—a fusion already reflected in the 16th-century *Majmūʿ al-tawārīkh*, which casts Manas and his kin as Kipchaks from Karkara [5] [16]. This second wave shows up most thickly in the southern *ichkilik* division—where Abdumanapov places the **Kipchak, Dolloos, and Naiman** tribes—while leaving Kipchak ties across outside divisions (**Saruu, Monoldor, Azyk, and Kangdy**) and sub-clans such as **Jetti-kashka** and **Kytay-kipchak** shared with both Kazakhs and the Kipchak groups absorbed into the Uzbeks [5].

Where modern Kyrgyz diverges sharply from Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Nogai is in its sound system. Those three western steppe tongues (Baskakov's **Kipchak–Nogai** group) underwent two hallmark consonant shifts: Proto-Turkic `*š` became `s`, and `*č` became `š` [12] [136] [138]. Kyrgyz underwent neither shift, preserving *tash* ("stone"), *kysh* ("winter"), and *chach* ("hair") where Kazakh and Karakalpak have *tas*, *qys*, and *shash* [12] [138].

It is tempting to chalk that split up to mountain isolation—imagining that Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Kyrgyz began as one undifferentiated Kipchak language and high passes simply shielded Kyrgyz from steppe innovations. That shortcut breaks down on two fronts. Isolation might explain why Kyrgyz *lacks* a Kazakh consonant change, yet it cannot explain the *positive* traits Kyrgyz shares with Southern Altai—secondary long vowels, shared verbal morphology, and early Siberian-shared Mongolic loans [12] [13] [95] [139]. Nor were Kyrgyz speakers isolated from Kazakhs: winter transhumance (*kyshtoo*) brought herds down onto lowland steppe every year (Chapter 4.1), the two peoples shared khans across the 16th and 17th centuries (Chapter 7.2) [14], and Washington finds the closest parallels to Kazakh right along the shared border in **Talas** (where Talas *tow-u* "its mountain" sits beside standard Kyrgyz *too-su*) [12]—the exact gradient left by an Altai-rooted speech moving *toward* Kazakh along their border. By contrast, Karakalpak and Kazakh remain mutually intelligible because they branched off late from the same Jochid–Nogai population; when Karakalpak shifted into the Amu Darya delta in the 18th century, later neighbourhood contact pulled it toward Uzbek and Turkmen [21] [140].

One historical finding also limits how early the `*š > s` and `*č > š` shifts can be pushed. Examining 18th-century Kipchak wordlists compiled by P. S. Pallas, **Julia Normanskaja (2023)** discovered that 18th-century Nogai and Bashkir still recorded older `*č` and `*š` in places and that Kazakh and Nogai did not yet display their modern orthographic closeness—indicating late completion of the shifts and secondary convergence between Kazakh and Nogai [141]. Because those western consonant changes completed late, their absence in Kyrgyz cannot by itself date an early medieval divergence from Kazakh; the positive structural features binding Kyrgyz to Southern Altai carry the real chronological weight.

#### 8.2 Sound and Grammar Fingerprints

Four concrete structural markers anchor Kyrgyz to the Altai–Sayan zone and, with one caveat noted below, separate it from Kazakh.

The first is **full rounding (*labial*) vowel harmony paired with three-way progressive consonant assimilation across suffixes** [12] [136] [138] [142]. In Kazakh, rounding harmony fades in low vowels, leaving plurals after *o* or *ö* unrounded (*ot-tar* "fires," *qol-dar* "hands," *köl-der* "lakes"). Standard Kyrgyz and Southern Altai carry vowel rounding straight through the suffix (*ot-tor*, *kol-dor*, *köl-dör*) while conditioning suffix-initial consonants across three environments that split the plural from the accusative and genitive [12] [138] [142]. In the **plural** (`-LAr`), `-l-` follows vowels as well as *y* and *r* (*ay-lar* "months," *jer-ler* "lands," *shaar-lar* "cities"), `-d-` follows all other voiced consonants (*tal-dar* "willows," *köl-dör* "lakes"), and `-t-` follows voiceless consonants (*tash-tar* "stones," *ot-tor* "fires")—generating **twelve distinct surface forms** across four vowels [142]. In the **accusative** (`-NI`) and **genitive** (`-NIn`), `-n-` appears after vowels alone while `-d-` follows *every* voiced consonant including *y* and *r* (*jer-di*, *jer-din*; *ay-dy*, *ay-dyn*), with `-t-` after voiceless stems—likewise yielding **twelve forms** each, whereas on bare stems the **dative** (`-ga/-ka`), **locative** (`-da/-ta`), and **ablative** (`-dan/-tan`) each produce **eight** surface forms (taking `-na`/`-nda`/`-nan` shapes only after third-person possessive suffixes) [12] [138] [142].

The second fingerprint sits in the **verb system**. Kyrgyz shares directly with Southern Altai and South Siberian Turkic the **habitual past participle in *-chu/-chü*** (as in *bar-chu* "used to go") [12] [138]. The caveat concerns its expressive bank of **auxiliary verb constructions** built on converbs in *-p* or *-a/-e*—*-p jat-* for ongoing action, *-p jür-* for habitual motion, *-p kal-* for sudden completion, *-p koy-* for deliberate completion, and *-a ber-* for sustained action—which Kazakh also uses, so these add nothing to the case for an Altai link [12] [138].

The third is **secondary vowel length**. Holding a vowel changes word meaning in Kyrgyz, yet those long vowels are secondary innovations rather than Proto-Turkic retentions: they formed when an internal consonant dropped out and adjacent vowels fused, converting Proto-Turkic *\*tag* ("mountain") into Kyrgyz ***too*** where Kazakh generated the diphthong ***tau*** [12]. The identical contraction produced long vowels inside Mongolic loanwords whenever medial consonants eroded [143], and secondary vowel lengthening through consonant loss is a hallmark spanning the entire South Siberian Turkic area, Altai included [139]. By contrast, archaic *primary* Proto-Turkic long vowels vanished in Kyrgyz, surviving instead in Yakut (Sakha), Turkmen, and Khalaj, with residual traces in Azeri, Gagauz, and the Bolghar branch [144].

The fourth test—the everyday word for **"foot" or "leg"**—cuts an unexpectedly sharp boundary *between* Altai and the Yenisei. Proto-Turkic carried two roots for the lower limb, *\*adaq* and *\*but*. Modern Kyrgyz and Pamir Kyrgyz use ***but*** as their ordinary term, aligning with every Southern Altai dialect, Tuvan, Tofa, Soyot, and the Uyghur varieties of the Tarim basin (whose inclusion leaves open areal diffusion across the South Siberian–Tarim belt alongside an Altai core). Kazakh instead uses ***ayaq***, and descendants of *\*adaq* remain the standard word across the linguistic heirs of the Yenisei basin: **Khakas (*azax*), Mrass Shor (*azaq*), Sakha (*atax*), and Fuyu Kyrgyz in Manchuria (*azïx*)** [145]. Unlike secondary vowel length, which clusters Kyrgyz with Khakas [139], the *but*/*adaq* split peels Khakas, Shor, and Fuyu Kyrgyz away, pairing Kyrgyz with Altai–Sayan rather than with the descendants of the Yenisei population. One caution checks how much weight this single word can bear: Kyrgyz never lost the rival root—K. K. Yudakhin's dictionary enters ***ayak*** as "leg, lower part of the leg" with no archaic marker alongside "end, bottom, lower part," "consequence," and "drinking bowl" [146], just as Old Uyghur retained both *but* and *adak* [145]. Measuring which term dominates live everyday speech across northern and southern dialects demands a spoken corpus rather than a dictionary.

#### 8.3 Loanword Stratigraphy and the Dialect Map

Stacked above those structural bones lie two massive lexical strata—one Mongolic, one Perso-Arabic—whose geography maps straight onto Kyrgyz history.

The **Mongolic stratum** is among the richest in the Turkic world, though specialists dispute its scale by an order of magnitude. **Rysbek Alimov**—who led the 2021–2023 **TMCKyrgyz** project at Johannes Gutenberg University Mainz (Grant 101024434) [147]—counts "more than three thousand" Mongolic loanwords in Kyrgyz [95] (publishing his lexical analyses in *Orientalia Suecana* [95] alongside a 2024 companion study in *Hacettepe Üniversitesi Türkiyat Araştırmaları Dergisi* arguing that early Turkic loans entered Mongolian partly via Yenisei Kyrgyz rather than exclusively through Old Uyghur [148], while the project's announced aim of evaluating potential Mongolic phonological, morphological, and syntactic substrate effects in Kyrgyz has not yet appeared in print [147]). Sorted by age and source, Alimov divides those loans into four chronological horizons [95]:

1. An early **Yuan-period Middle Mongolian layer (13th–14th centuries)** shared exclusively with Siberian Turkic languages and absent from Kazakh;
2. A **Common Middle Mongolian layer (13th–14th centuries)** distributed across Turkic from Siberia to Anatolia;
3. A small later set of **Northern Mongolic (Buryat-type)** words; and
4. A huge **Western Mongolic (Oirat–Kalmyk) layer of the 16th–18th centuries**, the largest and youngest of all four.

That Oirat vocabulary testifies to intimate everyday and ritual exchange rather than battlefield friction alone [95]. Alimov highlights terms such as *albın* ("demon; a spell against female evil spirits," from Literary Mongolian *albin*, Oirat *aljvan*), *dambır* ("a spell cast at the first spring thunder," from Literary Mongolian *dambaru*, a ritual hand drum), *seter* ("an animal dedicated to a deity, not to be slaughtered or shorn," from Kalmyk *setr*, ultimately Tibetan), *kökšün* ("old man, old woman," from Oirat *kögšin*), *öödö* ("upward," from Oirat *ööde*), and *zoo* ("rock, cliff, ridge," from Written Oirat *zoo* "spine") [95]. Because Tibetan Buddhist terms concentrate in north-eastern dialects, Alimov concludes that Oirat–Kyrgyz entanglement was "more intensive than is often recognized" and that the Tian Shan functioned as a Turko-Mongolic contact zone across the 16th to 18th centuries [95]. Other surveys document Mongolic entries spanning kinship terms, body parts, animal age names, medicine, adjectives, and verbs [149].

**Jonathan Washington (2023)** arrives at a starkly leaner portrait [150]. Filtering candidates strictly against Mongolic sound shifts capable of dating them, he admits only "approximately 200 borrowings from Mongolic into Kazakh and/or Kyrgyz," concluding that "the majority of the loanwords appear to be borrowings from a variety of Middle Mongol, with a few potential later borrowings"—which implies "only a single relatively early period of strong contact... followed by little further linguistic exchange" [150]. While horse breeding, hunting and falconry, kinship, flora and fauna, tools, food preparation, anatomy, and textiles appear in his verified inventory, he marks **law and politics, military, and religion and folklore** as absent—directly contradicting Alimov's Lamaist vocabulary [150].

The fifteenfold gap reflects two different sieves rather than a simple error: Alimov draws from the full ethnographic, folkloric, and dialectal archive (capturing north-eastern Oirat ritual loans and Siberian-only items), whereas Washington restricts himself to standard lexical entries passing strict phonological tests across Kazakh and Kyrgyz. Neither count measures how often Mongolic loans appear in live conversation. Crucially, even under Washington's conservative filter, two core historical conclusions hold firm: intensive 13th–14th-century Middle Mongol contact—which Washington himself interprets as "a period of Mongolic dominance, followed by a period of Turkic dominance" involving intermarriage into Turkic families [150]—and every independent Altai phonological and grammatical tie outlined in Section 8.2. What rests specifically on Alimov's dialectal stratigraphy are the Yuan-era Siberian-only bridge and the scale of 16th–18th-century Oirat ritual borrowing.

Everyday anatomy and kitchen terms reveal both the reach and the floor of Mongolic penetration. Kyrgyz carries core Mongolic verbs, particles, and cuts of meat absent from Kazakh—including *törö-* ("to give birth"), the particle *ele* ("only, just") [95] [150], *bötögö* ("bird innards"), and *šıyraq* ("shank"), alongside *taqım* ("kneepit") shared with Kazakh [150]. Yet directly beneath them sits an unbroken Proto-Turkic butchery core: *boor* ("liver") descends from *\*biagɨr* (Old Uyghur *baɣɨr*, Kazakh *bawɨr*, Khakas *pār*, Tuvan *bār*, Yakut *bɨar*), *öpkö* ("lung") continues *\*öpke* (Old Uyghur *öpke*, Kazakh *ökpä*, Khakas and Tuvan *ökpe*) [151], and *karın* ("belly") appears directly in Old Uyghur texts [152]. Mongolic reached the Kyrgyz hearth, settling atop Turkic roots far older than any Kyrgyz presence in the Tian Shan.

Resting above the Mongolic strata sits the **Perso-Arabic layer**—and its late arrival confirms the northern chronology from a completely different direction. While Karakhanid Karluks and Chigils adopted Islam and Arabo-Persian literary vocabulary from the 10th century (Chapter 4.3) [20] [38], the ancestors of Kyrgyz speakers remained outside that sphere, still described by Mirza Haidar as non-Muslims in the 1540s (Chapter 7.1) [15]. Surveying **Yudakhin's *Kyrgyz-Russian Dictionary*** (building on Dyykanov's counts), **Taalaibek Abdiev (2020)** records **958** words tagged as Arabic, **1,115** Kyrgyz derivatives formed from them, and **103** Arabic–Persian compounds—**2,176 entries**, or **5.75%** of the dictionary, roughly a third religious [153]. Purely Persian loans fall outside that count, so the full Perso-Arabic layer is somewhat larger. Abdiev stresses that this vocabulary arrived "not directly, but through the neighbouring Uyghur, Uzbek, Tajik and Kazakh languages"—the first two descending straight from Doerfer's Karakhanid–Chagatai literary chain (Chapter 4.3) [81]—still bearing Persian-built suffixes in words like *amalköy* and *joopker* [153].

Taken in mostly by ear, those loans were "fully subordinated to the pronunciation features of Kyrgyz": *kitāb* reshaped into *kitep* ("book"), *fard* into *parız*, *khayr* into *kayır*, and *zakāt* into *zeket*, while Arabic pharyngeals vanished (*ḥajj* → *ajı*, *allāh* → *alla*)—showing they were taken in by ear rather than through learned spelling, though such adaptation cannot by itself date them [153]. Although Abdiev notes that their exact period of entry "is not well studied"—with most scholars placing the main wave after the 16th century and peaking in the 19th and early 20th centuries, against Yunusaliev's earlier 12th–15th-century proposal—eyewitness fieldwork reinforces a late, southern-gradient spread [153]. Travelling through northern pastures in the mid-19th century, Chokan Valikhanov found Arabic and Persian vocabulary almost completely absent among the Bugu and Sarybagysh, the very tribes facing the Kazakh steppe and Oirats rather than Fergana [153].

Those twin external gradients—Altai, Kazakh, and Oirat to the north-east; Karluk–Chagatai and Tajik to the south-west—carved the primary **dialect map** of Kyrgyz [12] [95] [138]:

- **Northern dialect zone** (Issyk-Kul, Chüy, Naryn, and Talas—stronghold of the *ong* and *sol* wings on the Moghulistan, Kazakh, and Oirat frontier [14] [46]): maintains strong labial vowel harmony across the word, mirrors Kazakh closely in Talas, concentrates Oirat and Tibetan Buddhist loans in the north-east [12] [95], and still carried minimal Perso-Arabic vocabulary in the 1850s [153].
- **Southern dialect zone** (Osh, Jalal-Abad, and Batken—home to the *ichkilik* and southern right-wing clans inside the Fergana and Kokand orbit [2] [5] [20]): shows lexical and phonological convergence toward Uzbek paired with a much denser Perso-Arabic religious, urban, and farming lexicon [20] [12] [138].

Two cautions keep that dialect boundary in perspective. First, the **literary standard** codified in the 1920s was anchored in northern speech [115], meaning traits perceived as "northern" partly reflect standard Kyrgyz viewed from the south while schooling and media pull regional speech toward it. Second, the split records **recent neighbours rather than separate ancestries**: northern and southern clans spent the 16th through 19th centuries in contrasting political orbits and absorbed the vocabulary of the people beside them [2] [14] [20].

One sharp sociolinguistic puzzle rises straight out of this linguistic stratigraphy. If incoming northern and Altai bands were numerically modest beside the mountain tribes of the Tian Shan, why did their **Altai-shaped Kipchak** language triumph across the entire range instead of being swallowed by resident Karluk–Chigil speech, as small ruling elites so often are? Three converging forces explain why Karluk never held the alpine pastures as a single wall. First, the post-1218 Mongol, Qaidu-era, and Moghulistan upheavals had already diverted settled Karluk and Chigil oases into Chagatai towns while reorganizing the high pastures into mixed Moghul and Kipchak pastoral confederations (Chapters 4.3 and 5.3). Second, the second Eastern Kipchak wave and two centuries of Kazakh political alliance continuously reinforced Kipchak grammar and basic vocabulary from the west. Third, the carriers need not have been numerous, provided they sat in the right place in the kinship system. In patrilocal, exogamous clans, in-marrying wives usually adopt their husbands' speech, and across populations language tends to follow the paternal line more closely than the maternal one [202]. The likeliest vehicle for Altai-shaped Kipchak is therefore a prestigious, expanding patriline—such as the small northern R1a-Z2125 founder line that Chapter 10 cannot yet exclude—rather than the female-mediated Siberian ancestry the X chromosome hints at; which channel actually carried the language awaits clan-stratified genomes.

Before turning to DNA, however, a second forensic archive tests this exact north–south layering in visible, tangible form: the spirits people invoked, the crops they sowed, the clothes married women wore, and the tombs they raised over their dead.

### Chapter 9: Forensic Test II — Faith, Food, Dress, and Tombs

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 9**
>
> - **Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan cosmology survives beneath late Islam:** *Tenir Ata*, *Umai-Ene*, landscape spirit-masters (*ee* / *yir sub*), votive tree ribbons (*jalama*), and juniper fumigation (*archa*) match 8th-century runes and living Altaian and Khakas practice.
> - **Oasis borrowings sit visibly alongside northern rites inside the same home:** Ritual wild rue (*ysyryk*) against the evil eye (absent from Khakas/Altaian shamanism) and the Persian frame and *sümölök* of *Nooruz* (layered onto a shared Turkic equinox holiday like Khakas *Chyl Pazy*) were absorbed in place from Iranian and Central Asian oasis neighbours.
> - **Subsistence and dress fuse pastoral mobility with Silk Road trade:** Seasonal foothill grain farming (*bozo*, *talkan*, *kurut*) suggests by analogy how Tang accounts of Kyrgyz crops and Persian reports of no villages could both hold, while oasis markets supplied *nan*, *paloo*, tea, and cotton (*böz*, *mata*) for *elechek* turbans—worn right above the Altai–Siberian *beldemchi* riding skirt.
> - **Tombs close the regional mortuary sequence:** After Yenisei/Altai Kyrgyz cremations (to the 11th century) gave way in the Altai to a local Mongol-period inhumation rite (12th–14th centuries), Tian Shan Kyrgyz adopted Karakhanid–Chagatayid domed brick mausoleums (*kümböz*)—incorporating the 734 AH (1334–35) Chagatayid tomb of Kenizek-Khatun in Talas directly into *Manas* memory.
> - **Islam advanced in five late stages, and "Tengrism" conflates three separate things:** Formal Islam spread gradually from the 16th to 19th centuries via Sufi orders, Fergana displacement, anti-Oirat wars, and Kokand mullahs; assessing pre-Islamic survivals requires separating ancient practices, a modern external scholarly label, and a post-Soviet nationalist movement.

Islam became the state religion of the Chüy valley in 960, yet nearly six centuries later Mirza Haidar still described the Kyrgyz as "infidels" [15]. Formal conversion moved slowly northward—carried by Sufi networks, Dzungar-era displacements into the Fergana Valley, and prolonged warfare against the Buddhist Oirats—reaching the northern mountain tribes thoroughly only in the 19th century. Because Islam settled late, older religious practices and everyday material traditions endured openly inside Kyrgyz life [154] [155]. Sorting those layers by geography exposes the same composite design etched into Kyrgyz grammar: an Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan core overlaid in place by Iranian, Karluk–Chagatai, and Islamic oasis customs.

#### 9.1 Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan Traces

On the medieval Yenisei, the *Xin Tangshu* records that the Kyrgyz worshipped spirits "of water and grass," sacrificed at no fixed calendar date, called their shamans *gan*, and cremated their dead—circling the body three times while weeping before burying the bones a year later [45]. Centuries later, the Khakas heirs of that northern basin kept a closely related stratum beneath a different world religion: "shamanism in its southern Siberian form," centred on helping spirits housed in small figurines, blending with Russian Orthodox Christianity "actively propagated among the Khakas during the last decades of czarist rule" [123].

Among the Tian Shan Kyrgyz, ethnographers have documented a parallel northern bedrock surviving inside folk Islam [154] [155]. At its apex stand the sky deity **Tenir Ata** (Old Turkic *Täŋri*) and **Umai-Ene**, "the holy foremother responsible for birth and protection of children and strengthening marriages" [154]. Both names are carved directly onto 8th-century Old Turkic runic monuments—including the Kül Tegin and Tonyukuk inscriptions [52] [156]—and live on in traditional Altaian and Khakas belief [6] [123]. Because both belonged to the shared cosmology of the early medieval Turkic steppe and southern Siberia, they point to broad Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan heritage rather than uniquely to the Yenisei basin [6] [156]. Beside them stands the veneration of local **spirit-masters (*ee*)**, under which "every mountain, river or cave has its own spiritual benefactor or master" [154]—echoing the *Xin Tangshu*'s water-and-grass spirits [45], the Old Turkic cult of sacred earth and water (*yir sub*) [156], and the spirit-masters (*eezi*) revered by Altaians and Khakas [6] [123].

Ritual practice maps the same northern links onto the Tian Shan landscape. Pilgrims visiting ***mazars***—usually a saint's tomb, sacred spring, lake, or tree—tie strips of cloth (***chüprök*** or ***jalama***) to branches alongside animal sacrifice and ritual bread [154]. Traditional healers cure through chanted incantations (*dem saluu*), and shamans (*bakshy*), most of them women, still officiate at funerals and memorials [154] [155]. S. M. Abramzon and L. P. Potapov established that both the cloth-tying rite and the word *jalama* itself match the Southern Altaian *jalam* (or *kyira*) and Khakas *chalama*, ribbons knotted onto birches, larches, and mountain passes for local spirit-masters [6] [123] [100]. Because tying votive rags at shrines is also widespread across settled and nomadic Central Asia [154] [157], the Tian Shan custom grafted an Altai–Sayan rite seamlessly onto local Islamic *mazar* pilgrimage [6] [154].

The same mountain corridor survives in smoke. To purify a home, a cradle, or an afflicted person, the Kyrgyz burn aromatic **juniper (*archa*)** [154] [155]. Altaians burned juniper (*archyn*) long before the 1904 Ak Jang movement adopted it as a substitute for blood sacrifice; one of its founders, Chot Chelpanov, stated plainly that "we put archyn in the fire even before that" and that birch and juniper belonged "from the old faith" [158]. The Khakas kept the exact same juniper rite (*archyn* or *artysh*) [6] [123] [158].

#### 9.2 Oasis Borrowings and Everyday Material Culture

Step down from the alpine timberline into the arid valleys, and a second cultural layer comes sharply into focus: customs often mistaken for ancient nomadic heritage turn out, on closer inspection, to be Iranian and Central Asian oasis borrowings absorbed in place [6] [154] [157].

**Wild rue (*ysyryk*)** pairs directly with juniper to show how cleanly ecology separates the two strata. Burning aromatic plants for purification runs from the Altai all the way to the Iranian plateau, proving nothing about ancestry on its own. What carries diagnostic force is the plant and its ritual geography [154] [155]. Alongside northern juniper, the Kyrgyz burn seeds of wild rue (*Peganum harmala*: Kyrgyz ***ysyryk***, Persian and Tajik *isfand* or *sipand*) against the evil eye [154] [159]. Mahmoud Omidsalar notes that the plant was "well known among the ancient Indo-Iranians," remains "a common weed found in Persia, Central Asia, and the adjacent areas," and appears burning against the evil eye across early classical Persian literature [159]. Botanically, standard *Peganum harmala* belongs primarily to the arid Irano-Turanian steppe and semi-desert belt; while a variety (*P. harmala* var. *multisecta*) edges into arid pockets of the Chinese and Mongolian Altai and Dzungaria, and a sister species (*Peganum nigellastrum*) touches arid southern Siberian margins, wild rue is absent from the taiga and forest-steppe heartlands of Khakassia and the Russian Altai [160]. More decisively still, ritual fumigation with wild rue seeds against the evil eye belongs to the Iranian and arid Central Asian world—oasis dwellers and steppe nomads such as the Kazakhs alike—and is absent from Khakas and Southern Altaian shamanism, which relies on juniper instead [6] [123] [158] [159] [160]. Keeping both *archa* and *ysyryk* preserves both halves of Kyrgyz cultural geography inside the same household hearth.

The spring festival of ***Nooruz* (*Nawruz*)** reveals an identical layering. Marking the spring equinox itself was never borrowed from the south: the non-Muslim Khakas celebrate ***Chyl Pazy*** ("the head of the year") at dawn on the spring equinox—sweeping the home clean beforehand, putting on new clothes, climbing a rise to greet the sunrise while an elder prays, and feeding clan ancestor-figures [161]. What the Kyrgyz absorbed from Iranian Central Asia was the Persian dress wrapped around an older Turkic equinox day: the Persian name *Nooruz* (*Nawrūz*, "new day"), the solar calendar fixing it, and its signature sprouted-wheat dish ***sümölök*** [6] [154] [157].

Everyday subsistence offers an analogy for a puzzle that has hung over this narrative since Chapters 3.3 and 4.2. The Tang *Xin Tangshu* listed millet, wheat, barley, and highland barley among Yenisei Kyrgyz harvests [45], whereas the 982 Persian *Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam* insisted they possessed no villages or towns outside the khaqan's residence [64]. Historical nutrition studies of pre-Soviet Kyrgyz pastoralists suggest how both observers could be right at once [162]—though that evidence comes from the 19th- and 20th-century Tian Shan rather than the medieval Yenisei, and Rashid al-Din's report of cities and villages (Chapter 5.1) [87] shows that some Yenisei districts were settled outright. Alongside vertical transhumance—a highland way of life already deeply familiar across the Altai–Sayan mountains—herders practised **seasonal high-altitude grain farming**: cutting stone-lined foothill irrigation channels (***aryk***), families sowed frost-hardy barley (*arpa*), millet (*tary*), and wheat in spring, drove their herds up to the alpine *jailoo* for summer, and returned in autumn to reap the crop—harvesting cereal staples without maintaining permanent agricultural villages [162]. Tellingly, the core Kyrgyz lexicon for this grain-and-dairy diet is inherited Turkic, matching Khakas and Altaian parallels (*poza*, *talkan*, *kurut*) [6] [123]: fermented millet and barley beer (***bozo***), roasted grain meal mixed with milk or water (***talkan***), sour barley beverages (*maksym* and *jarma*), millet porridge (*botko*), fermented mare's milk (***kymyz***), clarified butter (*sary mai*), and dried sour curd balls (***kurut***, already recorded under that name in Mahmud al-Kashgari's 11th-century *Dīwān* [77]) [162].

Yet a pastoral kitchen built on boiled meat, dairy, and hardy mountain grains was never self-contained [126]. Unlike salt—abundant around southern Siberian salt lakes as well as local Tian Shan deposits—warm-climate agricultural staples and urban commodities had to be obtained through oasis markets along the Silk Road corridors skirting the range, from Sogdian Suyab in the Chüy valley (Chapter 2.2) [39] across the Bedel Pass toward Aksu and Kashgaria (Chapters 4.1 and 7.1) [69] [128]. Exchanging surplus livestock, wool, and hides for flour, rice, brick tea, vegetable oils, and spices [70] [134] [162], Kyrgyz herders held a strategic perch as far south as alpine grazing could reach—plugged directly into Silk Road commerce while keeping high, defensible summer pastures above lowland states [125] [126]. That multi-directional exchange left distinct culinary strata. Clay-oven flatbread (*nan*), orchard vocabulary (*darak*, "tree"), and celebratory rice dishes such as ***paloo*** (widely known via Russian as *plov*) entered through Iranian and Karluk–Uzbek neighbours across Fergana, whereas steamed dumplings (*manty*) reflect broader Central Asian–East Asian exchange and hand-pulled noodles (*lagman*) arrived even later via Tarim Basin Uyghur ties and the 1877–1884 Dungan (Hui Muslim) refugee migrations into Semirechye and Issyk-Kul [12] [162]. Together, those contacts drew a culinary border between the meat-, dairy-, and *bozo*-anchored northern highlands (Issyk-Kul and Naryn) and the oasis-inflected south (Osh and Fergana) that maps cleanly onto Chapter 8's dialect divide [12] [162].

Traditional clothing and domestic crafts reveal the exact same tension between pastoral self-reliance and Silk Road trade [6] [126] [163]. Local flocks and foothills supplied heavy winter survival gear: compressed sheep's wool felt (*kiyiz*) for outer cloaks (*kementai*), hats (*kalpak*), carpets (*shyrdak*), and yurt covers; leather and suede for riding trousers (*shalbar*); and tall wild steppe reed stalks (***chiy***) wrapped in dyed wool yarn to build breathable yurt insulation screens, kitchen partitions (*ashkana*), and felt-rolling mats [163]. What mountain pastures could not grow was soft, washable woven plant cloth. Whereas northern Khakas communities historically wove coarse summer garments from indigenous wild nettle and hemp fibers before imported textiles took over, Tian Shan Kyrgyz relied heavily on traded **cotton**—above all coarse unbleached ***böz*** and plain-weave ***mata*** from Fergana, Tashkent, and Kashgar—which Chokan Valikhanov even observed circulating virtually as currency in the 1858–59 Altyshar trade [134] [163]. Cotton supplied undergarments (*köynök*), coat linings, and the iconic ***elechek*** turban wound from up to thirty metres of white cloth [164] [163]; even tiny scraps of imported cotton and silk were hoarded and stitched into geometric ***kurak*** patchwork garments [163]. True **silk** (*shayı*) and **velvet** (*bakhmal*)—entering well before industrialization via Bukharan, Fergana, and Qing frontier trade—remained scarce luxury, diplomatic, and ceremonial fabrics reserved for aristocratic robes and trims rather than everyday pastoral wear [134] [163]. Married Kyrgyz women wore that entire north–south history on their bodies [6] [164] [163]: around the waist after a first child sat the ***beldemchi***—a warm, pleated wraparound riding skirt of velvet or wool stitched to a padded felt girdle, slit at the front for horseback riding in biting mountain air and traced by S. M. Abramzon and K. I. Antipina straight to southern Siberian and Altaian equestrian dress [6] [164] [163]—while atop the head rose the Islamic oasis cotton ***elechek*** [164]. The felt yurt (***boz üy***) displays the same composite joinery: its hemispherical frame, bent-wood roof wheel (*tündük*), and curved struts (*uuk*) share the Kipchak–Turkic architecture of the Kazakhs, while north-eastern dialects preserve Western Mongolic (Oirat) terms for tent and felt furnishings [95] [162].

Finally, **post-1400 funerary architecture** closes the regional mortuary sequence traced across this book—from the Tashtyk, Yenisei, and Chineta II cremations (Chapters 3.1 and 4.2) [44] [73], through the 12th–14th-century inhumations that followed them in the Altai (attributed to the local Altai population rather than to Kyrgyz; Chapter 5.2) [74], into Islamic Central Asia. Settling into the Tian Shan and gradually embracing Islam between the 16th and 19th centuries, Kyrgyz clans adopted baked- or mud-brick domed mausoleums (***kümböz***) modelled directly on local Karakhanid and Chagatayid shrines, alongside carved stone ram-horn gravestones (***kochkortash***) shared with the Kazakhs [165]. The country's most celebrated tomb—the **"Kümböz of Manas"** in the Talas valley, revered as the epic hero's burial place and pilgrimage *mazar*—is an authentic 14th-century Chagatayid monument. Epigraphic and architectural documentation by M. E. Masson, G. A. Pugachenkova, and Sh. E. Ratiia (1950), cited in *The Cambridge History of the Mongol Empire* and catalogued on Archnet, established that its carved Arabic portal inscription dates the tomb to **734 AH (1334–35 CE)** and dedicates it to **"Kenizek-Khatun, daughter of the amīr Abuka"** (remembered in popular accounts as "the most glorious of women, Kenizek-Khatun") [91] [166] [165]. Just as incoming clans moved onto Issyk-Kul's alpine meadows, they adopted the standing Chagatayid and Karakhanid mortuary landscape of the valleys and folded it directly into the *Manas* tradition.

**Table 9.1: Ritual and Material Culture Stratigraphy**

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Tenir Ata* and *Umai-Ene* [154] | Old Turkic steppe & Altai–Sayan [52] [156] | Yes [6] [123] | Among Turkic groups only | Shared Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan heritage [6] [156] |
| Spirit-masters (*ee*) of mountains, springs, and rivers [154] | Old Turkic (*yir sub*) & Siberian animism [45] [156] | Yes (*eezi*) [6] [123] | Merged into local *mazar* saint cults [154] [157] | Steppe/Siberian cosmology mapped onto Tian Shan geography [6] [154] |
| Cloth ribbons on sacred trees (*jalama* / *chüprök*) [154] | Altai–Sayan & pan-Eurasian votive custom [6] [154] | Yes (Altaian *jalam*, Khakas *chalama*) [6] [123] | Yes, at Islamic *mazars* [154] [157] | Convergence of an Altai–Sayan rite with Central Asian shrine pilgrimage [6] [154] |
| Equinox new year [154] | Shared across the Turkic world [161] | Yes: Khakas *Chyl Pazy* [161] | Yes: *Nawrūz* [157] | Common date; Persian name, solar calendar, and *sümölök* borrowed in place [6] [154] [157] |
| Fumigation: juniper (*archa*) [154] | Altai–Sayan and Inner Asian [158] | Yes (*archyn*, *artysh*) [6] [123] [158] | Juniper also used, less centrally [157] | Continuity with the Altai–Sayan world [6] [158] |
| Fumigation: wild rue (*ysyryk*, *Peganum harmala*) [154] | Iranian and Central Asian arid zone [159] [160] | Ritual seed-burning absent; core range is Irano-Turanian (reaches only arid Mongolian/Chinese Altai margin, not Khakassia/Russian Altai heartlands) [159] [160] | Yes (*isfand* / *sipand*) [159] | Absorbed in place from Tian Shan and Fergana populations [154] [159] |
