| Riding skirt (*beldemchi*) vs. wrapped white cotton turban (*elechek*) [164] [163] | Dual origin: Altai–Siberian riding skirt + Central Asian Muslim headdress [6] [164] [163] | *Beldemchi* parallels Altai–Siberian equestrian dress [6] [164] | *Elechek* (traded oasis *böz*/*mata* cotton) parallels Kipchak and oasis Muslim turbans [134] [164] [163] | Preserves northern equestrian dress at the waist and southern Silk Road cotton/Islamic dress on the head [6] [164] [163] |
| Domed brick mausoleums (*kümböz*) and ram-horn gravestones (*kochkortash*) [165] | Karakhanid, Chagatayid, and Kipchak Islamic funerary tradition [91] [166] [165] | No (northern sequence was cremation, followed in the Altai by a local inhumation rite) [44] [74] | Yes: modelled on Karakhanid/Chagatayid shrines (e.g., 734 AH / 1334–35 Talas tomb of Kenizek-Khatun) [91] [166] [165] | Marks post-15th-century adoption of Central Asian Islamic mortuary architecture [20] [165] |

#### 9.3 Late Islam, Buddhist Echoes, and the Three "Tengrisms"

Not every pre-Islamic or non-Islamic religious strand belonged either to Old Turkic cosmology or to Iranian oases. Alongside the Tibetan Buddhist vocabulary concentrated in north-eastern Kyrgyz dialects (Chapter 8.3), the **Tarbagatai Kyrgyz** of Xinjiang still practise Buddhism today [95]. Among the Sart-Kalmaks of eastern Issyk-Kul, the first migrant wave of 1863 arrived already Muslim, whereas the waves of 1884 and 1894 came as Buddhists who later converted—and **one of the oldest surviving mosques in the Issyk-Kul region**, at **Chelpek**, was built by Sart-Kalmaks in **1889** [11] [167].

Over the preceding centuries, Islam became the overarching framework holding these older layers together through five distinct historical stages traced by M. A. Zakirov and colleagues [20]:

1. **10th–12th centuries:** Islam arrived in the *region* before reaching the Kyrgyz—adopted as the Karakhanid state religion in 960 while the Kyrgyz remained largely on the Yenisei and in the Altai [20].
2. **14th–16th centuries:** The Moghul dynasty converted under Tughlugh Timur in 1354, yet "many Kyrgyz nonetheless stayed pagan," as Mirza Haidar attested in 1547 [20] [15].
3. **16th century:** Sufi networks began drawing Kyrgyz clans into the **Naqshbandi, Qadiri, Yasawi, and Kubrawi** orders, with Sayf al-Din Akhsikandi's Fergana chronicle depicting Kyrgyz youths studying in Islamic schools [16] [155] [119]—a southern tie deepened during the 17th-century White and Black Mountain Khoja conflicts in Kashgaria (Chapter 7.1) [128].
4. **17th–18th centuries:** Kyrgyz driven into the Fergana Valley by Dzungar pressure came under urban clerical influence, and warfare against the Buddhist Oirats was cast as holy war (*ghazāt*), binding Islam to Kyrgyz identity and the *Manas* cycle [20] [16].
5. **19th century:** Under the Khanate of Kokand, Kyrgyz *manaps* invited Uzbek mullahs into the mountain pastures; across the northern highlands, no permanent mosques had existed prior to Russian rule [20].

Zakirov and colleagues emphasize that mobile pastoralism was "one reason the Kyrgyz adopted Islam only superficially" [20], contrasting sharply with the southern oasis centres of Osh and Uzgen, which supported dozens of mosques and madrasas [20]. Even today, pilgrimage to *mazars* combining sacrifice, ritual bread, and cloth ribbons remains widespread across Kyrgyzstan, though stricter orthodox believers reject those customs as *shirk* (associating partners with God) [154].

Evaluating those pre-Islamic survivals cleanly requires separating **"Tengrism"** into three distinct phenomena that public debates routinely blur together:

- **The practices themselves are ancient.** *Tenir*, *Umai-Ene*, the spirit-masters of mountain and spring (*yir sub* / *ee*), and votive tree ribbons (*jalama*) stand in 8th-century runic inscriptions and reappear across Altaian and Khakas ethnography [6] [123] [52] [100] [156]. None of those elements was fabricated in modern times.
- **The label "Tengrism" is modern and external.** Surveying its long trajectory, F. Bayat and H. Kaplan note that the term was coined in hindsight by outside scholars ("generally external and constructed a posteriori") for an unnamed cosmological complex [168]. Yet they affirm real continuity: Tengri-centred motifs survived Islamisation across folklore, popular devotion, and Alevi–Bektashi traditions, treating shamanism as the ritual dimension of a broader worldview linking *kut*, *törü*, ancestor veneration, and Earth–Water powers [168]. Applying a late scholarly name to an ancient unnamed complex does not turn that complex into an invention.
- **The political movement is a post-Soviet project.** Marlène Laruelle analyzed post-Soviet Tengrism across Kyrgyzstan and Tatarstan as "a re-appropriation of a national past which is as much real as it is reconstituted," noting that President Akayev lauded it as the "natural" religion of the Turkic peoples while its chief theorist, Dastan Sarygulov, sat in parliament [169] [170]. That diagnosis holds squarely for 1990s–2000s nationalist ideology—which, as Bayat and Kaplan caution twenty years later, "should not be confused with the historically layered religious and cosmological structures" it draws upon [168].

Language and lived culture have now converged independently on the same layered portrait: an Altai–Sayan core reshaped by Kipchak waves and dressed in place with Iranian, Karluk, and Islamic Central Asian elements. Yet words, riding skirts, rituals, and brick tombs can all travel across communities without moving large numbers of people. One final forensic archive measures biological ancestry directly, dates when populations actually merged or split, and tests whether the northern core arrived as a conquering wave of men or a smaller stream woven into the Tian Shan through marriage: the genomes.

### Chapter 10: Forensic Test III — What the Genomes Show

> **Key Takeaways — Chapter 10**
>
> - **Eight genomic estimates from five studies converge on c. 1250–1600 CE:** Whole-genome admixture (~1150–1600, eastern pulse c. 1275), the Kyrgyz–Kazakh split (c. 1334), the Ulugqat–Kyrgyzstan split (c. 1578), and simultaneous 13th–14th-century founder expansions of Kyrgyz R1a-Z2125 and Mongol-linked C2-F3796 all anchor modern Kyrgyz formation in the Mongol and post-Mongol centuries.
> - **Broad Siberian ancestry without Cis-Baikal markers rules out a mass Minusinsk migration:** Although Ulugqat Kyrgyz carry 25.6% broad Siberian ancestry (and Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz more), their genomic models do not require the specific Cis-Baikal Yeniseian ancestry shared by Khakas and Southern Altaians, and they carry almost none of the northern male lineages Q (0–2%) and N (0–4.5%).
> - **Genetic compartments exclude a mass northern male migration but give a mixed sex-specific picture:** Northern Q and N male lines are nearly absent; the X chromosome (Siberian ancestry enriched, East Asian depleted) hints that Siberian ancestry entered partly through women; mtDNA (~69% East Eurasian) roughly matches the autosomal eastern share; and Y-DNA (39–63% R1a, mostly Z93/Z2125; 12–25.5% C2) points to male-biased steppe ancestry—leaving high R1a-Z2125 ambiguous between local Tian Shan and small Altai/Yenisei founders until medieval ancient DNA is available.
> - **The Golden Horde and "black hat" controls cleanly separate Kyrgyz from Kazakhs and Karakalpaks:** Kazakhs (51.9% C2, 6.5% R1a) and Karakalpaks (41% C2, 6% R1a, attested by 1597–98) carry a Jochid C2 majority and Kipchak–Nogai speech; Kyrgyz invert those paternal lines, retain Altai linguistic structure, and coalesced in Moghulistan rather than the Golden Horde.
> - **Neighbouring controls underscore a distinct trajectory—and an ecological split explains the oasis paradox:** Low Kyrgyz J2 (0–10%) and closer autosomal proximity to Kazakhs ($F_{ST} = 0.2\%$) than Uyghurs ($F_{ST} = 0.4\%$) reflect the Karakhanid divide between oasis farmers (Uzbek/Uyghur) and highland nomads (merged into Kyrgyz), while Sakha (>80% N), Lopnur (57.8% J2; 59.4% all-J), and Khotons (82.5% bottlenecked R1a1 from Turkestan) each follow different paternal histories.

Chronicles record political titles; languages and household customs can cross borders without moving large populations. Genomes measure something harder to fake: who actually left descendants, when ancestral pools merged or split, and whether men and women moved along the same routes. Submitted to that biological cross-examination, modern and ancient DNA deliver the sharpest verdict in the entire dossier—confirming a Mongol- and post-Mongol formative horizon in the Tian Shan while ruling out both a mass Minusinsk relocation and a Golden Horde origin.

#### 10.1 The Genetic Clock

In the highest-resolution whole-genome study of the Kyrgyz published to date, **Chen and colleagues (2025)** sequenced **36 Kyrgyz from Ulugqat County** in Xinjiang's Kizilsu prefecture alongside **16 Kyrgyz from Kyrgyzstan** and **44 Kazakhs from north-western China** [42]. Their primary ancestry model resolves the Ulugqat Kyrgyz into four major components: **41.7% East Asian, 25.6% Siberian, 25.2% West Eurasian, and 7.6% South Asian** [42]. One sampling caveat belongs directly beside those flagship numbers: they describe 36 residents of a single Xinjiang border county, whereas Chen and colleagues observe that the Kyrgyzstan sample carries **more Siberian and less South Asian ancestry** [42]. Still, the chronological framework cuts cleanly across both cohorts. Chen's two-wave admixture model places an initial East–West meeting roughly **2,225 years ago** (Chapter 2.2) followed by "secondary admixture events (∼875 to 425 years ago) linked to historical migrations under Mongol and post-Mongol rule"—roughly 1150 to 1600 CE—including an eastern ancestry pulse shared across Uyghurs, Kazakhs, and Xinjiang Kyrgyz about **30 generations ago** (c. 1275) [42]. A Eurasia-wide survey of Turkic speakers independently dates South Siberian and Mongolian admixture across Kazakhs, Kyrgyz, and neighbouring Kipchak and Karluk groups to the exact same **13th–14th centuries** [96].

Population-divergence clocks point into that same medieval crucible. In Chen's demographic tree, the two Kyrgyz cohorts "split from the common ancestry shared with" the Kazakhs roughly **691 years ago** (28 generations, c. **1334 CE** counted from 2025) and separated from one another around **447 years ago** (18 generations, c. **1578 CE**) [42]. Genetic distance confirms how tight the regional links remain: Xinjiang Kyrgyz sit at $F_{ST} = 0.13\%$ from Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz and $F_{ST} = 0.2\%$ from Kazakhs, versus $F_{ST} = 0.4\%$ from Uyghurs [42]. The authors enter an explicit technical caution: the Kazakh sample "did not completely support the same tree topology" as the Kyrgyz one, and their model could not disentangle recent gene flow, population bottlenecks, and selection [42]. Independently, a genomic study of **96 present-day Kazakhs** drawn from tribes across all three *zhuzes* dates the admixture that forged the modern Kazakh gene pool to **1341–1544**, spanning the birth of the Kazakh Khanate, and attributes its remarkable genetic uniformity across hordes to roughly **400 years** of strict seven-generation exogamy (*zheti ata*)—the exact marriage rule the Kyrgyz keep as *jeti ata* [32] [129].

Look closely at the timing of that ~1334 Kyrgyz–Kazakh divergence. It falls roughly 130 years before the founding of the Kazakh Khanate (1465–66), roughly a century before retrospective Qing and Persian notices place Kyrgyz in Moghulistan, and 170 years before the 1503–04 episode that Mirza Haidar recorded within living memory. Heavy 16th- and 17th-century intermarriage between Kazakhs and Kyrgyz would normally pull a modeled split date *younger*, not push it back into the 1330s; likewise, the ~1578 split between Ulugqat and Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz precedes modern imperial frontiers by three centuries. Rather than conflicting with the late textual horizon, those dates imply that the ancestral pools behind modern Kyrgyz and Kazakhs were already diverging across the Chagatayid/Altai–Moghulistan versus Jochid steppe divide during the 14th century—well before political labels froze along the borders.

Paternal Y-chromosome clocks converge on the exact same century. In **Zerjal et al. (2002)**, 63% of 41 men from central Kyrgyzstan carried haplogroup **R1a1** with notably low internal diversity—a textbook founder expansion whose most recent common ancestor (TMRCA) dates to roughly **620 years ago** (confidence interval 400–1,000; 670–820 years ago under alternative estimators), prompting the authors to float Mongol-era disruptions as a possible trigger without concluding [103]. Scaling up to **298 Xinjiang Kyrgyz men**, **Wen and colleagues (2022)** confirmed in their published abstract that **R1a1a1b2a2a-Z2125** and **C2b1a3a1-F3796** are the two leading paternal lineages, with both Kyrgyz-specific sub-branches expanding simultaneously in the **13th to 14th centuries**—tracing deeper roots to a Bronze Age Siberian/steppe pool and to medieval Niru'un Mongols respectively [104]. *(Secondary summaries of the paywalled full text report R1a-Z2125 at 39%, a deep Z2125 node at 2,585–3,107 years ago, and Kyrgyz sub-branch ages of 630–730 BP and 680 BP—finer figures not yet confirmed against the full text (Appendix E), though either BP baseline falls squarely inside the abstract's 13th–14th-century window [104].)* High modern R1a frequency therefore reflects a medieval bottleneck and boom, never raw, undisturbed continuity from Bronze Age Yenisei burials. (As a deep macro-haplogroup, R1a is vastly older—its primary split dates to roughly 5,800 years ago near present-day Iran, dividing European from Central–South Asian branches [171], and it was already present in Mesolithic Karelia by 6850–6000 BCE [22] [172].)

The Mongol-linked **C2-F3796** branch connects directly to the imperial elite of the 14th century. (Haplogroup labels in this chapter follow each study's own nomenclature, which varies between ISOGG versions; Wen's C2b1a3a1-F3796, Zhabagin's C2a1a3-M504, and Askapuli's C2a1a3-F1918 are all treated here as branches of the same Niru'un-linked star cluster.) Zhabagin and colleagues tie this "star cluster" to the early Niru'un Mongols, dating its Kazakh sub-branches to the 13th and 14th centuries [173]. In a 2026 *PNAS* paleogenomic study of four Ulytau mausoleum burials in central Kazakhstan, **Askapuli and colleagues** confirmed that all three paternally related males (buried in the Joshi Khan, Alasha Khan, and Ayakkamir/"Lower Tomb" mausoleums, two of the 14th century) carried a rare branch of this exact **C2a1a3-F1918 star cluster**, while their non-sex-chromosome (*autosomal*) profiles—alongside a fourth female burial, Bolghan Ana—matched Northeast Asian/Siberian populations rather than modern Kazakhs, verifying substantial post-14th-century steppe shift (though the authors stress that literal identification with Jochi cannot be proven without reference DNA) [174].

Converted to calendar years from each study's publication baseline, eight genomic estimates from five studies—four of them from Chen and colleagues' single dataset, and one concerning Kazakhs rather than Kyrgyz—cluster across the Mongol and post-Mongol centuries:

**Table 10.1: Genomic Estimates Converted to Calendar Years**

| Estimate | As published | Calendar years | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Secondary admixture | c. 875–425 years ago | c. 1150–1600 | Chen et al. [42] |
| Eastern pulse shared by Uyghurs, Kazakhs, and Xinjiang Kyrgyz | c. 30 generations | c. 1275 | Chen et al. [42] |
| South Siberian and Mongolian admixture in Kipchak and Karluk peoples | 13th–14th centuries | 13th–14th centuries | Yunusbayev et al. [96] |
| Kyrgyz-specific R1a-Z2125 and C2-F3796 branches | 13th–14th c. in abstract (c. 630–730 / 680 BP in secondary summary) | 13th–14th c. (c. 1290–1390 from 2021, or c. 1220–1320 from 1950) | Wen et al. [104] |
| Kyrgyz–Kazakh split | c. 691 years (28 generations) | c. 1334 | Chen et al. [42] |
| Kazakh gene-pool admixture | 1341–1544 | 1341–1544 | Gnecchi-Ruscone et al. [32] |
| Main Kyrgyz R1a1 founder | c. 620 years (400–1,000); 670–820 by other methods | c. 1380 (1000–1600); c. 1180–1330 | Zerjal et al. [103] |
| Xinjiang–Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz split | c. 447 years (18 generations) | c. 1578 | Chen et al. [42] |

Chen's estimates count back from 2025 using 25-year generations and Zerjal's from 2002; because the paywalled full text of Wen et al. leaves its exact baseline unverified here, both 2021 and the 1950 radiocarbon convention are displayed. Every central estimate sits between roughly 1250 and 1600, with only the earliest edges of secondary admixture and Zerjal's wider bounds reaching before 1207. Because admixture dates, population splits, and lineage ages measure different events, and because Yunusbayev and colleagues find the same Mongol-era admixture across Turkic speakers generally [96], the clustering marks a shared regional horizon rather than an independent fix on Kyrgyz formation.

Four methodological guardrails keep these dates honest [42] [129]. Admixture models assume fixed 25-year generations and discrete pulses, compressing centuries of ongoing intermarriage into a single date—and dating when two ancestries met, never when a group crossed a mountain pass [42]. Small samples (36, 16, and 44 individuals) can be nudged by recent neighbour gene flow (especially among Ulugqat Kyrgyz living beside Uyghurs and Kazakhs), bottlenecks, and selection [42]. Geographic coverage also skews toward northern/central Kyrgyzstan and Kizilsu, leaving the southern *ichkilik* clans of the Fergana and Pamir-Alay rim under-sampled [42] [103] [104]. Above all, existing genomic surveys are **not stratified by Kyrgyz *uruu***. In a society of patrilineal clans practising seven-generation exogamy, sampling forty men in one district can easily capture just a few closely linked lineages—producing exactly the kind of 63% single-haplogroup spike seen in small cohorts [103] [129].

How dramatically tribal stratification matters is proven by **Khussainova et al.'s (2022)** 24-tribe Kazakh survey covering **1,171 men (1,164 SNP-confirmed)** across all three *zhuzes* [175]. Although C2-M217 averages 51.9% nationwide, it surges past 70% in southern and western tribes (Alban, Kongyrat, Dulat, Baiuly, and Alimuly), whereas O2-M122 reaches 52.3% in the Naiman, G1-M285 hits 54% in the Argyn, Q reaches 66.7% in the Kangly, and R1b-M343 reaches 36.8% in the Kypshak [175]. Recent forensic work by **Isakova and colleagues (2025)** has expanded 23-Y-STR data across three geographic regions of Kyrgyzstan [176], yet a full *uruu*-stratified SNP phylogeny matching Khussainova's Kazakh benchmark remains unwritten. Sorting Kyrgyz Y-chromosomes by *uruu* would directly test whether Kipchak, Naiman, and Kongirat divisions of the *ichkilik* and left wing carry Kazakh-like C2 or O2 while right-wing Tagay and Adigine clans carry R1a.

#### 10.2 What the Northern Signature Rules Out—and Leaves Open

At first glance, two headline genomic findings look mutually contradictory: modern Kyrgyz carry roughly a quarter **Siberian** ancestry across the genome [42], yet their genomic models do not require the diagnostic **Yenisei basin** ancestry carried by the Khakas and Southern Altaians [28]. The contradiction disappears once analytical resolution is checked.

Chen and colleagues' "Siberian" component—labelled "Central Asia/Siberia" in the paper and modelled with broad geographic proxies (including a Chukchi proxy in one figure)—captures general northern and Inner Asian affinity rather than a single language family, noting only that "recent gene flow from northern populations that harbored more SIB ancestry" could account for the higher Siberian share in Kyrgyzstan than in Xinjiang [42]. **Zeng and colleagues' (2025)** **Cis-Baikal** component works at the opposite end of the microscope: a narrow genetic tracer-dye tied specifically to early Yeniseian speakers (Chapter 2.1) [28]. Testing Kyrgyz sampled across **Kyrgyzstan, China, and Tajikistan**, Zeng and colleagues found that "Yeniseians, South Siberian Turks and Samoyeds are the only populations with appreciable levels" of Cis-Baikal ancestry: every passing genomic model for the **Khakas, Shors, Tuvinians, and Southern Altaians** includes it, whereas models for the Kyrgyz do not require it [28]. Carrying broad northern ancestry without the specific Cis-Baikal Yeniseian signature fits gene flow from Inner Asia and the Altai borderlands without absorbing the Yeniseian-admixed core of the Minusinsk Basin. While Yeniseian absorption on the Yenisei continued into early modern times—after early departing clans had already moved south—the lack of any detectable Cis-Baikal requirement in Kyrgyz models weighs heavily against reading the modern Tian Shan Kyrgyz as a mass transplanted Minusinsk population—on the reasonable assumption that the medieval Minusinsk population, *kyshtyms* included, carried the ancestry its modern heirs still do.

The male lineages reinforce that boundary—while exposing an important nuance that earlier arguments have often overstated. Across published Y-chromosome surveys—Wells et al. (2001, $n=52$, 63.5% R1a1-M17 [177]), Zerjal et al. (2002, $n=41$, 63% R1a1 [103]), Di Cristofaro et al. (2013, $n=132$ Kyrgyzstan Kyrgyz, 54.5% R1a-M198/M17 alongside amplified C3b2b1-M401 and the lowest Y-DNA diversity in their panel alongside Pashtuns [178]), Zhabagin et al. (2022, $n=20$, 55% R1a [179]), Wen et al. (2022, $n=298$ NW Chinese Kyrgyz, 39% R1a-Z2125 [104]), and Chen et al. (2025, 13 of 16 men carrying haplogroup R as a whole, including any non-R1a branches [42])—Kyrgyz men consistently show **39–63% R1a** (predominantly Z93/Z2125; the 39% floor is Wen and colleagues' figure as given in secondary summaries), **12–25.5% C2** (largely C2-F3796), and **8–15% O** [103] [104] [177] [171] [178] [179]. By contrast, the hallmark northern Siberian male lineages **Q** and **N** nearly vanish: **Q** reaches **93.7% (45/48)** among Kets (Karafet et al. 2002 data as tabulated in Tambets et al. 2004, Table 3 [180], with Zeng et al. noting that branch Q-YP1691 occurs at high frequency among Yeniseian-speaking Kets and at lower levels in Samoyedic and Siberian Turkic neighbours such as Selkups and Tuvinians [28]) and stands at **16.7%** among Southern Altaian Altai-kizhi (Dulik et al. 2012 [102], amid pronounced Y-DNA differentiation even between northern and southern Altaians documented by Kharkov et al. 2007 [181]), while **N** accounts for roughly **50%** of Khakas men (Kharkov et al. 2011 [124]). Among Tian Shan and Pamir Kyrgyz, **Q sits at 0–2%** and **N at 0–4.5%** [104] [177] [178].

Here epistemic discipline matters. The near-absence of Q, N, and Cis-Baikal ancestry cleanly excludes a wholesale male migration from the mixed Yenisei/*kyshtym* population ancestral to the modern Khakas. Yet high **R1a-Z2125** by itself does **not** automatically rule out every northern male founder. True, ancient DNA confirms that R1a-Z2125 was already entrenched across the Tian Shan before Kyrgyz ever appear there—persisting from Iron Age Saka burials through 3rd–9th-century CE Issyk-Kul graves alongside rising East Eurasian ancestry [41] [104]. Yet as Chapter 2.1 documented, R1a-Z93/R1a1 *also* dominated Bronze and Iron Age southern Siberia (Andronovo, Krasnoyarsk, and Tashtyk Oglakhty [26] [27] [33]) and still makes up 50% of Altai-kizhi men [102] and 25–28% of Khakas men [124]. Because R1a-Z93/Z2125 sits natively at *both* ends of the 1,650-kilometre corridor—and because Chapter 6.2 argued that the Khakas descend disproportionately from N- and Cis-Baikal-carrying *kyshtyms* after the Yenisei elite was dismantled—Y-SNP frequencies alone cannot yet distinguish a local Tian Shan R1a-Z2125 lineage expanding during the 13th–14th centuries from a small R1a-Z2125 Altai/Yenisei elite undergoing a parallel founder boom. Only high-resolution sub-branch phylogenies compared directly against ancient Yenisei Khaganate and 10th–14th-century Issyk-Kul Chigil genomes (Chapter 11.2) can settle which end of the corridor supplied the Kyrgyz R1a-Z2125 founder line.

Whether northern ancestry entered through men or women is the next question, and the answer is more mixed than it first appears when maternal, paternal, and X-chromosomal compartments are examined side by side. First, across independent Xinjiang and Kizilsu samples, mitochondrial DNA (inherited exclusively through mothers) is predominantly East Eurasian—with the two largest cohorts converging within half a percentage point of one another, and close to the autosomal eastern share of about 67% (41.7% East Asian plus 25.6% Siberian) [42]:

**Table 10.2: Maternal (mtDNA) Lineages Across Xinjiang Kyrgyz Samples**

| Location | Sample ($n$) | East Eurasian | West Eurasian | Leading East Eurasian lineages | Leading West Eurasian lineages | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ulugqat County, Kizilsu (Xinjiang) | 36 | 20 of 36 | 16 of 36 | — | — | Chen et al. (2025) [42] |
| Northwest China (Xinjiang) | 91 | 69.23% | 30.77% | D4 10.99%; C4 9.89%; A 6.59%; F1 & B4 5.49% each; G 4.40%; C5 3.30%; Z1 2.20% | H 7.69%; T 6.59%; U 4.40% | Wang et al. (2022) [182]\* |
| Kizilsu Prefecture (Xinjiang) | 138 | 69.57% | 30.43% | C 14.49%; D4 14.49% | H 13.77% | Guo et al. (2020) [183] |

*\*Wang et al.'s 48-population reference panel omitted Siberians, so its apparent maternal affinity with Han Chinese reflects available reference groups rather than a test of Siberia [182].*

Setting all four biological compartments of the Kyrgyz genome side by side shows two different signals—while keeping transparent that these rows draw on overlapping Kyrgyz cohorts rather than one single unified sample. The maternal pool roughly mirrors the autosomes (in Chen's own 36 Ulugqat individuals, at 20 of 36 or about 56% East Eurasian, it is if anything less eastern than they are), so mtDNA adds little evidence of female-biased eastern input. The paternal pool, dominated by steppe-derived R1a, is far more western than the autosomes, pointing to male-biased steppe ancestry. The only Siberian-specific sign of female bias comes from the X chromosome. Because women carry two X chromosomes and men only one, two-thirds (67%) of the X chromosomes in any population pass through mothers while autosomes split 50/50; any ancestry enriched on the X chromosome therefore entered disproportionately through women:

**Table 10.3: Ancestry Breakdown Across Genetic Compartments (Tian Shan, Pamir, and Xinjiang Kyrgyz)**

| Compartment | Inheritance | East Asian / East Eurasian | Siberian | West Eurasian / Steppe | Northern Q + N | Cohort | Implication | Sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Autosomes** | 50% pat. / 50% mat. | 41.7% East Asian | 25.6% | 25.2% *(+7.6% S. Asian)* | — | Ulugqat ($n = 36$) | Whole-genome baseline (Kyrgyzstan has more Siberian, less S. Asian) | [42] |
| **X chromosome** | 33% pat. / 67% mat. | **↓ Depleted** vs. autosomes | **↑ Enriched** vs. autosomes | — | — | Ulugqat ($n = 36$) | Siberian ancestry entered partly via women (directional signal from $n = 36$ [184] [185]) | [42] |
| **mtDNA** | 100% maternal | 69.2–69.6% East Eurasian | *(within East Eurasian)* | 30.4–30.8% | — | NW China / Kizilsu ($n = 91, 138$) | Roughly matches the autosomal eastern share (~67%), so adds little evidence of sex bias | [182] [183] |
| **Y chromosome** | 100% paternal | C2 12–25.5%; O 8–15% | *(see Q + N)* | R1a 39–63% *(Z93/Z2125)* | Q 0–2%; N 0–4.5% | Tian Shan, Pamir & Xinjiang ($n = 20$–$298$) | Excludes mass Minusinsk/*kyshtym* male migration (Khakas ~50% N; Kets 93.7% Q); R1a dominance points to male-biased steppe ancestry | [42] [103] [104] [177] [178] [179] [124] [180] |

Twenty years before Chen and colleagues, **Comas et al. (2004)** documented the background against which any such signal has to be read: neighbouring Central Asian ethnic groups share closely overlapping maternal (mtDNA) pools, whereas their paternal (Y-DNA) pools differ sharply from group to group—the classic signature of "higher female than male migration" across ethnic and clan boundaries, with their Kyrgyz sample carrying 7.0% northeast Asian mtDNA G2a [186]. That generic female mobility under patrilocal marriage is not itself directional; it is the baseline the X-chromosome result has to rise above.

A sensible sociological objection deserves a direct answer: across Central Asia, married couples traditionally lived with the husband's clan (*patrilocal* residence) while taking wives from outside the clan (*exogamy* under *jeti ata*). Wouldn't routine local wife-exchange naturally diffuse maternal lineages faster than paternal ones without any northern migration? Ordinary neighbouring exogamy pushes outside ancestries toward the maternal line without regard to their source; on its own it would not be expected to **enrich Siberian** ancestry while **depleting East Asian** ancestry on the X chromosome of the same people [42]. If that directional split holds in larger samples, it points to sex-biased intermarriage with specific source populations—Siberian-rich families supplying wives, while East Asian ancestry, depleted on the X, arrived disproportionately through men, as the Mongol-era C2 lineages would lead one to expect. That fits prolonged Altai–Irtysh cohabitation [4] [5], strict *jeti ata* exogamy [129], and Washington's model of Mongolic speakers marrying into Turkic households [150], all operating inside an open *uruu*/*sanjyra* clan system built to adopt allies [46] [129].

Statistical restraint still applies. When Goldberg and colleagues used X-versus-autosome comparisons to argue for heavily male-biased Bronze Age steppe migration into Europe, **Lazaridis and Reich (2017)** failed to replicate the result and exposed mathematical skew in X-ancestry estimators [184]. **Goldberg et al. (2017)** countered that bias flips sign at extreme proportions—overestimating high shares and underestimating low ones in low-coverage ancient DNA—while intermediate shares (~25%, matching Kyrgyz Siberian ancestry) remain stable [185]. Although Chen et al. worked from high-coverage modern whole genomes rather than degraded ancient DNA, in a sample of 36 Ulugqat residents the X-chromosome asymmetry is best read as a **directional signal awaiting replication rather than a precise percentage** [42].

One final genomic note closes the high-altitude puzzle raised in Chapters 7.1 and 9.2: Chen and colleagues detect ancestry-biased natural selection at four genes—including ***RYR2*** (cardiac function) and ***C10orf67*** (linked to hypoxia response)—yet find "no classical high-altitude adaptation genes (e.g. *EPAS1*)" [42], fitting seasonal alpine *jailoo* grazing at 2,500–3,500 metres rather than permanent Tibetan-scale hypoxia. Crucially, no published study yet includes ancient genomes from **Yenisei Kyrgyz Khaganate graves**—a gap with a structural cause, since the Yenisei Kyrgyz cremated their dead and cremated remains are generally unsuitable for ancient DNA recovery [58]—or from **15th–17th-century Tian Shan Kyrgyz burials**.

#### 10.3 The Steppe Controls: Kazakhs, Karakalpaks, and Why the Horde Is Wrong

Because the Kyrgyz speak a Kipchak language, share clan names with Kazakhs, and carry Mongol-era admixture, two recurring shortcuts tempt readers: first, that the Kyrgyz originated inside the **Golden Horde** as Turkicised Mongols who adopted Kipchak speech; and second, that **Karakalpaks, Kazakhs, and Kyrgyz** are essentially one steppe people divided only by modern hats and Soviet borders. Testing both proposals against historical archives, Chapter 8's linguistic findings, and the comparative Y-chromosome table separates genuine kinship from false equivalence.

##### 1. Golden Horde Kipchaks and Turkicised Mongols: Right Process, Wrong Horde

In the 14th century, the Arab encyclopedist **Shihab al-Umari** memorably described how Mongol conquerors in the Golden Horde (*Desht-i-Qipchaq*) assimilated into their Kipchak subjects: settling on Kipchak pastures and intermarrying, "they became just like Kipchaks, just by settling on their land, as if they were one ethnic group," dissolving "like salt in water" [187]. A remarkably similar process unfolded among the Kyrgyz—making four genuine parallels easy to spot: Bayesian basic vocabulary groups Kyrgyz with Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Nogai inside South Kipchak [13]; steppe clans (Kipchak, Naiman, Kereit, Kongirat, plus sub-clans Jetti-kashka and Kytay-kipchak) appear across both peoples [5]; the main Kyrgyz C2 lineage (C2-F3796 / C2a1a3-M504) belongs to the exact star cluster carried by Askapuli's three Ulytau Golden Horde mausoleum men [173] [174] [179]; and autosomally Kyrgyz stand very close to Kazakhs ($F_{ST} = 0.2\%$), with Wang and colleagues tracing westward-moving Mongols absorbed into Turkic speakers including Xinjiang Kyrgyz under the Chagatai Khanate [42] [188].

Yet the horde is wrong. For the Kyrgyz, that synthesis happened in **Moghulistan** (the eastern Chagatai khanate) and the **Altai**, not inside the Golden Horde. Five lines of evidence split the Kyrgyz from a Golden Horde origin:

- **Geography and eyewitnesses:** Issyk-Kul and the Chüy valley were Chagatayid territory. In 1547, Mirza Haidar counts the Kyrgyz inside "the tribe of Moghul" and sets them apart from the "Uzbeg"—the Jochid steppe triad divided by early-16th-century chronicler **Fazlullah ibn Ruzbihan** into Shaybanids, Kazakhs, and Manghuds (Nogais) [15] [127] [187]. Popular summaries listing "Kyrghyz" inside the Golden Horde recycle the later Russian blunder of calling Kazakhs "Kirghiz." Golden Horde influence reached the Kyrgyz at second hand—when Jochid sultans Janibek and Kerey settled on the Chüy in 1459, launching two centuries of Kazakh–Kyrgyz partnership and Kipchak linguistic contact [14] [127].
- **Inverted paternal lineages:** Literally, "Mongol fathers, Kipchak speech" describes the **Kazakhs**, whose 1,164-man survey yields **51.9% C2-M217** and just **6.5% R1a1a-M17** [175]. Among the Kyrgyz, the proportions flip: **39–63% R1a** against **12–25.5% C2** [42] [103] [104] [178] [179].
- **Altai linguistic substrate:** As Chapter 8 established, Kyrgyz retains positive Southern Altai sound and grammar features—full labial harmony, secondary long vowels (*too* vs. Kazakh *tau*), and early Siberian-shared Mongolic loans [12] [95] [139]—and never participated in the western Kipchak–Nogai `*š > s` and `*č > š` shifts (which, as Normanskaja showed in Chapter 8.1, completed late on the western steppe [141]).
- **Religious chronology:** Both Golden Horde and Moghulistan courts had embraced Islam by the mid-14th century, whereas the Kyrgyz were "still infidels" in 1547 [15]—matching incomers stepping out of the Oirat–Altai frontier outside both Islamic realms.
- **Pan-steppe material culture:** Felt yurts, horse harness, and transhumance predate both hordes and belong to Altaians alike [36] [71], incapable of distinguishing one horde from another.

##### 2. The "Black Hat" Control: Karakalpaks, Kazakhs, and Soviet Borders

Emerging directly out of the 15th-century Jochid–Uzbek and Nogai steppe confederations [21] [187] [140], the **Karakalpaks** offer a clean living control showing what a genuine Golden Horde–Nogai successor looks like. Surface culture blurs all three peoples together: men across all three wear the felt *kalpak* [189], and Chokan Valikhanov noted in the mid-19th century that the 14th–16th-century "Nogai era" epic cycle (such as *Edige*, founder of the Nogai Horde) formed shared heritage across Nogais, Kazakhs, and Karakalpaks while situating *Manas* in the same post-Mongol epic horizon [134] [190].

Neither the Karakalpak identity nor the ethnic split behind it was manufactured by 20th-century Soviet mapmakers. As Russian orientalist **Pavel Ivanov** demonstrated from Bukharan archives in 1935, a charter issued by Shaybanid ruler **Abdullah Khan II in 1006 AH (1597–98 CE)** already records the *Qaraqalpaqs* ("black kalpaks") living near Sighnaq on the lower Syr Darya [191] [140]. (Older speculation linking them to the 12th-century *Chernye Klobuki* or "Black Caps"—Turkic auxiliaries of Kievan Rus'—rests purely on matching hat names with zero historical or archaeological backing [140].) Likewise, though felt *kalpaks* were worn across all three groups (photographed on Kazakhs around 1870), the white *ak-kalpak* became an official exclusively Kyrgyz national emblem only recently, inscribed by UNESCO in 2019 [192] [189]. Early Soviet border shuffling—placing the **Karakalpak Autonomous Oblast** inside the "Kirghiz [Kazakh] ASSR" in **1925**, transferring it directly to the **Russian SFSR in 1930**, raising it to an **autonomous republic in 1932**, and reassigning it to the **Uzbek SSR in 1936** [21]—shifted administrative lines atop pre-existing peoples rather than inventing them.

Beneath those shared hats and administrative transfers, three independent tests group Karakalpaks straight with Kazakhs and cleanly apart from the Kyrgyz:

- **Language:** Karakalpak belongs to Kipchak–Nogai and is mutually intelligible with Kazakh and Nogai, whereas Kyrgyz pairs structurally with Southern Altai (Chapter 8.1) [8] [13] [136].
- **Paternal lineages:** In **Zhabagin et al.'s (2022)** full-sequence Y-chromosome panel across ten Central Asian groups [179], Karakalpaks ($n=17$) carry **41% C2** (29% C2a1a3-M504 star cluster plus 12% C2a1a2a-M48, characteristic of Junior *zhuz* Kazakhs and Oirats), **18% O-M175**, **12% J2a**, and only **6% R1a1a-M17**—matching Kazakhs (54% C2 in Zhabagin; 51.9% C2 vs. 6.5% R1a in Khussainova [175]) and inverting the Kyrgyz (55% R1a1a-M17 in Zhabagin; 39–63% across surveys [103] [104] [177] [178] [179]).
- **Geography and livelihood:** From the Syr Darya, Karakalpaks moved during the 18th century into the **Amu Darya delta** south of the Aral Sea, combining herding with delta farming and fishing under the Khanate of Khiva [21] [140].

##### 3. The Comparative Y-Chromosome Matrix and the Oasis Paradox

Set side by side across thirteen populations, the paternal landscape puts Kyrgyz uniqueness into sharp relief:

**Table 10.4: Comparative Y-Chromosome Haplogroup Frequencies Across Steppe and Siberian Controls**

| Population | R1a | C2 | Q | N | Other main lineages | Sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kyrgyz (Tian Shan and Pamirs) | 39–63% | 12–25.5% | 0–2% | 0–4.5% | O 8–15% | [103] [104] [177] [171] [178] [179] |
| Southern Altaians (Altai-kizhi) | 50% | 20% | 16.7% | 4.2% | — | [102] |
| Khakas | 25–28% | 5–10% | about 5% | about 50% | — | [124] |
| *Mongol, Oirat, and Kipchak steppe controls* | | | | | | |
| Kazakhs across all three *zhuzes* (1,164 SNP-confirmed; 22 sequenced) | 6.5% (0% in small panel) | 51.9% (54% in small panel) | 3.1% | 6.9% | O2 8.0%; G1 7.1%; J 6.2%; R1b 5.6% | [175] [179] |
| Karakalpaks (17 men sequenced in full) | 6% | 41% | none recorded | 6% | O 18%; J2a 12%; J1, G, E 6% each | [179] |
| Mongols of Mongolia (852 men for C2 and O) | 6% | 58% | — | about 10%, with Q | O 17% | [193] [194] |
| Golden Horde elite, central Kazakhstan (3 men; two 14th c.) | none | 3 of 3, star cluster (C2a1a3-F1918) | none | none | — | [174] |
| Kalmyks, Volga Oirats (426 men) | 3% | 56% | — | 10% | O 12%; R2a 8% | [195] [196] |
| Oirats of western Mongolia: Dörvöd, Torguud, Khoshuud (105 men) | — | 66–78% | — | — | Characteristic subclade C3-F6379 | [194] [196] |
| Xinjiang Mongols (165 men) | 10% | 18% or more | — | 5% | O 25% or more | [188] |
| *Other peoples linked with the Kyrgyz* | | | | | | |
| Khotons of Uvs (40 men) | 82.5% (33/40) | 10.0% (C2-M48/M86, reported as C3c) | 0% (P[xR1a] 0%) | — | O 2.5% (1/40); other 5.0%; bottlenecked diversity 0.314 | [111] |
| Lopnur people (64 men) | none recorded | 2–3% | about 2% | about 2% | J 59.4% (J2a1 43.8% + J2a2 14.1% = 57.8% J2); R2 9.4%; L1c 7.8%; O about 8% | [197] |
| Sakha (Yakuts) | rare | up to 13% (northern Sakha) | none | over 80% | — | [198] [199] |

One apparent paradox jumps right off this matrix—and Chapter 4.3's Karakhanid ecological split resolves it cleanly. If resident Karluks and Chigils contributed heavily to the Tian Shan Kyrgyz, why are modern Kyrgyz autosomally twice as close to Kipchak-speaking Kazakhs ($F_{ST} = 0.2\%$) as to Karluk-speaking Uyghurs ($F_{ST} = 0.4\%$ [42]), and why do Kyrgyz men carry relatively little of the oasis haplogroup **J2** (0–10%, against 18–25% in Uyghurs and Uzbeks and 57.8% J2—59.4% all-J—in the Lopnur people [197] [179])? Because the medieval Karakhanid population divided along an ecological fault line. Lowland oasis Karluks and Chigils across Kashgaria, Fergana, and Transoxiana merged with dense Iranian agricultural populations rich in J2 and southern oasis ancestry, forging modern Uzbek and Uyghur gene pools [32] [38] [179]. Up on the alpine pastures of Issyk-Kul and Semirechye, pastoral Chigil, Karluk, and Türgesh clans preserved the Saka-steppe nomadic profile—dominated by R1a-Z2125—and subsequently mixed with the same 13th–14th-century Kipchak–Mongol pool that shaped the Kazakhs [32] [41] [104]. Two empirical gaps keep this reconstruction indirect: 10th–14th-century Chigil/Karakhanid burials around Issyk-Kul remain unsequenced (the only published 14th-century human genomes from the region, from the urban Christian cemeteries of Kara-Djigach and Burana in the Chüy valley, were sequenced to trace the Black Death and represent an urban Christian community rather than mountain pastoralists [203]), and southern *ichkilik* Kyrgyz near the Fergana oases remain under-sampled [42] [103] [104].

Every other control row underscores how distinct the Kyrgyz trajectory was. **Mongols and Oirats** carry 56–78% C2 [193] [194] [195] [196], proving Mongol paternal ancestry inside the Kyrgyz (12–25.5% C2) was a real minority—while 62 ancient Mongol-period steppe individuals (local elites rather than khans) show male-biased East Asian inflow and rising O2a [200], and Xinjiang Mongols carry only 10% R1a, confirming that dominant Kyrgyz R1a did not arrive with Mongol conquerors [188]. The **Sakha (Yakuts)** provide the sharpest northern foil: traced to the Altai–Sayan via Baikal like the Kyrgyz ethnonym, over 80% of Sakha men descend from a single branch of **haplogroup N** (expanding ~1,300–1,400 years ago, with a second boom ~900 years ago matching the 11th–13th-century push to the middle Lena) [198] [199]—parallel medieval upheavals powered by entirely different male founders. The **Lopnur people** of the Tarim carry 57.8% J2 (59.4% all-J) and no recorded R1a [197], with ~41% East Asian, ~26% West Eurasian, and ~24% Siberian ancestry showing *male-biased* western gene flow [201]—the same direction as the Kyrgyz R1a signal, but carried by oasis J2 lineages rather than steppe R1a.

Finally, the **Khotons of Uvs** show what a true Dzungar-era deportation from Turkestan looks like in Y-DNA. **Katoh et al. (2005)** ($n=40$)—still the sole published Y-chromosome survey of the Khotons—revealed **82.5% R1a1 (33/40)** with severe bottlenecked diversity (**0.3141**) and just **10.0% C2-M48/M86** (reported as C3c) [111], completely unlike surrounding western Mongolian Oirats (66–78% C2 [194] [196]). Because Katoh's markers resolved R1a only to M17 rather than Kyrgyz-specific Z2125 subclades (and subsequent Oirat surveys such as Balinova et al. 2019 [196] did not sample Khotons), that bottlenecked Central Asian profile fits a small deported Turkestani or Tian Shan Kyrgyz captive group settled in Uvs under the Dzungars without yet measuring how large its specifically Kyrgyz share was [111]. Meanwhile, Balinova et al. (2019) *did* sample **61 Sart-Kalmaks in Kyrgyzstan**, finding **>30% R1a-Z2125** and inferring substantial male-mediated gene flow from neighbouring Kyrgyz [196]—confirming genetically what Chapter 1.2 tracked ethnographically: Oirat-speaking migrants living beside the Kyrgyz absorb Kyrgyz paternal lineages and identity within generations.

All five archives—written chronicles, archaeology, language, material culture and belief, and whole genomes—have now testified. None tells a simple story alone; set beside one another, they dismantle two centuries of either/or debate and assemble a single, coherent explanation of how a northern name came to anchor a Tian Shan people.

### Chapter 11: Synthesis — How a Name Outlives a Migration

#### 11.1 Putting the Pieces Together

When written chronicles, excavated cemeteries, spoken grammar, household rituals, and whole genomes are laid on the same table, one coherent narrative emerges across the 1,650 kilometres separating Kyzyl from Barskoon. The record supports continuity of a **political name and institutional lineage** travelling in stages from the upper Yenisei through the Altai and western Mongolia's Oirat sphere into the Tian Shan—rather than a mass demographic exodus straight from Minusinsk to Issyk-Kul.

On the Yenisei, a Turkic-ruled society of mixed ancestry bore the Kyrgyz name securely from the 6th century onward. Even after smashing the Uyghur khaganate in 840, its core population stayed anchored in the Sayan and Minusinsk basins—where a Yenisei "Kyrgyz Land" endured until the Dzungar deportation of 1703—while Chigils and Karluks grazed the shores of Issyk-Kul. Across those intervening centuries, independent Muslim geographers placed the Kyrgyz firmly in the north, apart from al-Marvazi's ~1120 borderland group living beside Karakhanid Muslims and Yu. N. Esin's single, unexcavated reading of an Aksu/Barčūk holding in al-Idrisi's atlas [20] [50] [63] [64] [76] [80].

Archaeology and ethnography mark the exact reach—and limits—of the northern trail. Cremation, the defining mortuary signature of the medieval Yenisei Kyrgyz, tracks continuously from Tashtyk crypts through Tang and Persian accounts into 9th–11th-century kurgans in the Altai, only to stop there—followed in the Altai by a standardized 12th–14th-century inhumation rite of the local population [74] and, along the southern border, by one Kyrgyz group's switch to burial beside Muslim neighbours [20]. Juniper fumigation (*archa*), by contrast, reaches all the way into modern Kyrgyzstan alongside Khakas and Altaian parallels [123] [158], yet purifying dwellings with aromatic smoke spans the entire world from the Altai to the Iranian plateau [154] [155]. Set side by side, those two customs expose the boundary of ritual evidence: the burial practice most characteristic of the northern Kyrgyz never reached the Tian Shan, while the purification rite that did reach the Tian Shan is shared across too many neighbours to prove ancestry on its own.

Between the 15th and 18th centuries, the modern Kyrgyz took shape in the Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay out of incoming northern and Altai groups, older resident mountain Turkic clans, and Moghul, Kipchak, and Oirat neighbours—often sharing khans and winter frontiers with the forming Kazakhs. Both push and pull drove that convergence. Mongol campaigns, Yuan frontier clearances, and Oirat expansion provided the political **push** that shook mobile groups loose from the Altai–Irtysh corridor; pastoral **non-autarky** [126] and Silk Road geography supplied the directional **pull**. Because herding economies cannot survive on meat, milk, wool, and hides alone—relying on oasis bazaars for cotton cloth (*böz* and *mata*), grain, brick tea, oils, and spices [134] [162] [163]—the Tian Shan stood as the prime strategic perch in Inner Asia: as far south as alpine vertical grazing could reach, combining snow-fed summer pastures and defensibility ("low outside interference" [125]) right above the commercial arteries of Chüy, Fergana, and Kashgaria.

The genomes lock that timeline and demographic scale into place. Modern Kyrgyz carry roughly a quarter broad northern/Siberian ancestry alongside 13th–14th-century Mongol-era admixture [42] [96], yet their models do not require the Cis-Baikal Yeniseian signature shared by the Khakas and Southern Altaians [28], and Kyrgyz men carry almost none of the northern male lineages Q and N [104] [177] [178]. The sex-specific picture is mixed: X-chromosome enrichment of Siberian ancestry hints that some northern ancestry entered through women [42], the steppe-derived R1a majority on the Y chromosome points to male-biased western ancestry [103] [104], and mtDNA roughly mirrors the autosomes [182] [183]. Compared directly against Kazakhs, Karakalpaks, Mongols, Oirats, Sakha, Lopnur, and Khotons (Table 10.4), the Kyrgyz paternal combination—steppe-rooted R1a-Z2125 dominating above a Mongol-era C2 minority—matches none of them. Because R1a-Z93/Z2125 sat natively at *both* ends of the corridor (Tagar/Tashtyk/Altai in the north and Saka/early medieval Issyk-Kul in the south), Y-SNP frequencies alone cannot yet rule out a modest R1a-rich Altai/Yenisei founder beside resident Tian Shan clans. What the genomes exclude cleanly is any wholesale transplantation of the mixed Minusinsk/*kyshtym* population—whose true demographic heirs stayed north and became the Khakas.

Name continuity and ancestral discontinuity fit cleanly inside one social mechanism. Repeated small groups moving out of the Altai and Oirat sphere and marrying into resident Tian Shan and Kipchak populations under strict seven-generation exogamy (*jeti ata*) and open clan adoption (*uruu*/*sanjyra* [46] [129]) produce what the DNA records: medieval paternal founder expansions and a modest, possibly female-biased northern share, rather than a broad Yenisei genomic footprint. A prestigious political name and title hierarchy (*inal*, *idi*) travel at zero demographic cost—and unlike tiny conquering elites whose speech vanishes into a settled majority, Altai-shaped Kipchak prevailed across the Tian Shan because post-1218 Mongol and Moghulistan upheavals had already split settled Karluk–Chigil oases into Chagatai towns while reorganizing the high pastures, and the second Eastern Kipchak wave and Kazakh partnership reinforced Kipchak from the west. Which kin channel carried the speech itself remains open: language usually follows fathers in patrilocal clans [202], which favours a small but expanding northern patriline over the female-mediated Siberian ancestry the X chromosome hints at.

Measured against the three classic schools introduced in Chapter 1, this synthesis lands nearest to **name transfer**, while modifying the starker versions advanced by S. M. Abramzon and L. R. Kyzlasov: the ethnonym was never borrowed purely from afar, but carried southward by real, repeated small movements out of the Altai and Oirat world. The **staged migration** school (Valikhanov, Radlov, Karaev) recognized genuine northern ties, yet magnified their demographic weight and pushed them centuries ahead of the Tian Shan record, which opens in the 15th and 16th centuries rather than the 9th. The **Tian Shan autochthony** school finds no support: the Han bearings place Jiankun far to the north, and apart from two contested clues, no source puts Kyrgyz in or beside the Tian Shan before the 15th century. V. Ya. Butanaev's ethnic-continuity reading, which concerns the Yenisei rather than the bridge, holds firmly for the Khakas without stretching 1,650 kilometres south to Issyk-Kul. Language and lived culture confirm the same composite design: an Old Turkic and Altai–Sayan core (*Tenir*, *Umai-Ene*, *jalama*, *archa*, *beldemchi*, full labial harmony, secondary long vowels, and early Siberian-shared Mongolic loans) reshaped by two waves of Kipchak contact and overlaid in place by Iranian, Karluk–Chagatai, and Silk Road oasis culture (*Nooruz*, *ysyryk*, *elechek*, *paloo*, traded *böz*/*mata* cotton, *kümböz* shrines, and late Perso-Arabic vocabulary filtered through Fergana and Sufi channels).

For nearly two centuries since Valikhanov, the debate has offered too narrow a choice. *Migration* and *name transfer* were posed as opposites—either a people marched south or a word drifted south—because both camps quietly assumed that planting a political name in a populous mountain range requires a conquering wave of men large enough to stamp their paternal lineages across the country. That is the exact scenario the genomes refute. Instead, the five archives converge on a name travelling with smaller northern bands into a society whose clans were explicitly built to graft outsiders onto shared genealogies [46] [129] and whose marriage law compelled every patrilineage to bring in wives from beyond seven generations [129]. The name is therefore older than the people who carry it, and that is not a paradox. In a tribal world where belonging ran through political adoption and exogamous alliance as much as through blood, a lineage of names and a lineage of genes could diverge over centuries while both remained entirely real—which is how chronicles follow *Kyrgyz* from Han scouts on the northern steppe to Lake Issyk-Kul even as the genomes root most of its modern bearers in the mountains and steppes of Central Asia.

#### 11.2 What Would Settle the Remaining Mysteries

Closing the remaining evidentiary gaps depends on targeted work across archaeogenetics, tribal population genetics, linguistics, and archival ethnography:

- **Ancient genomes across four missing horizons:** As of 2026, direct paleogenomic comparisons still await sequenced remains from (1) **rare uncremated Yenisei Kyrgyz or closely adjacent 9th–11th-century Altai–Sayan inhumations** (since standard Yenisei Kyrgyz Khaganate burials are cremations unsuitable for DNA recovery [58]); (2) **15th–17th-century Tian Shan Kyrgyz graves** to pair against them; (3) **10th–14th-century nomadic Chigil and Karakhanid cemeteries around Issyk-Kul** (beyond the 14th-century urban Christian cemeteries of Kara-Djigach and Burana in the Chüy valley, sampled in tracing the Black Death [203]), needed to test whether resident mountain pastoralists supplied the dominant Kyrgyz R1a-Z2125 branch [104] and how much biological continuity links Chigils to clans such as the Bugu; and (4) **13th–15th-century burials from the Altai and the Uvs lake basin of western Mongolia**, needed to test whether southbound groups carried the Cis-Baikal ancestry seen in Southern Altaians and not required by models of modern Kyrgyz [28] and whether Kyrgyz-named communities actually settled beside Khyargas Nuur.
- **Tribe-stratified (*uruu*-level) modern phylogenies:** While recent forensic datasets such as Isakova et al. (2025) have expanded geographic 23-Y-STR coverage across Kyrgyzstan [176], the decisive test requires dated whole-Y-chromosome SNP trees and autosomal genomes sorted systematically across the right wing (*ong*: Tagay and Adigine), left wing (*sol*), and *ichkilik*—matching Khussainova et al.'s 24-tribe Kazakh benchmark [175]. Comparing individual Kyrgyz *uruu* directly against Altaian, Khakas, Kazakh, Karakalpak, and Oirat panels will test the 13th–14th-century founder expansions [103] [129], check whether Kipchak, Naiman, and Kongirat divisions carry Kazakh-like C2 or O2 while right-wing clans carry R1a, and screen at scale for Yeniseian-linked Q-YP1691 [28].
- **High-resolution Khoton subclade matching:** Now that Katoh et al.'s (2005) 82.5% R1a1 founder bottleneck among the Khotons of Uvs is established [111]—resolving R1a only to M17, since later Oirat surveys such as Balinova et al. (2019) [196] did not sample Khotons—new high-resolution sampling with R1a-Z2125 and Y-STR matching against Tian Shan Kyrgyz clades is needed to separate specifically Kyrgyz captives from broader Turkestani deportees.
- **Consolidated grammatical substrate results from the Mainz *TMCKyrgyz* project:** Although Rysbek Alimov's lexical studies from Grant 101024434 are now in print [95] [148], consolidated findings testing whether Mongolic reshaped Kyrgyz phonology, morphology, and syntax as a structural substrate remain awaited [147]—and will help bridge the gap between Alimov's >3,000-entry catalogue and Jonathan Washington's ~200-word phonological filter [150].
- **Dated Perso-Arabic loanword stratigraphy and spoken dialect corpora:** Sorting Perso-Arabic borrowings by phonological adaptation and by Uzbek, Tajik, Uyghur, or Kazakh mediation will test Chokan Valikhanov's 1850s report that northern tribes had scarcely acquired them [20] [81] [153] [138]. In parallel, usage frequencies from a dialect-stratified spoken Kyrgyz corpus set beside Southern Altai, Khakas, and Kazakh will show which inherited everyday roots (such as *but* versus *ayak* for "foot/leg") dominate live regional speech where dictionaries stay silent [145] [146].
- **Systematic cross-indexing of *sanjyra* genealogies:** Matching oral *sanjyra* pedigrees systematically against dated tribal names in Persian, Chinese, and Russian archives will sharpen when each *uruu* entered the Tian Shan confederation.

*(For quick reference across all regions, places, scholarly debates, evidentiary confidence levels, and remaining open source audits, see Appendices A through E below.)*

## Appendices

### Appendix A: Three Regions Side by Side

Because Kyrgyz history unfolds across three distant zones at once, this chronological grid aligns the Yenisei–Sayan core, the Altai–Mongolian bridge, and the Tian Shan–Fergana basins from the Bronze Age to Russian annexation. Primary and secondary citations appear in Chapters 2–7; genomic divergence and lineage-expansion dates are tabulated separately in Chapter 10.1 (Table 10.1), since molecular clocks date lineages rather than map coordinates.

**Table A.1: Three Regions Side by Side (c. 3000 BCE–1876 CE)**

| Period | Yenisei and Sayan | Altai, Mongolia, and the Oirat World | Tian Shan and Fergana |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| c. 3000–200 BCE | Afanasievo, Okunevo, Andronovo-related, and Karasuk herders; Yeniseian speakers; Tagar culture | Afanasievo; eastern Scythian gene pool forms; Pazyryk culture | Saka, c. 600–200 BCE |
| c. 200 BCE–550 CE | Gekun and Jiankun in Han records, place uncertain; Tashtyk culture; few later notices | Possible early home of the Gyangun; Jiankun northwest of Kangju in the 3rd-century *Weilüe* | Wusun from c. 160 BCE (final diplomatic missions to Northern Wei in 436–437 CE under Rouran pressure); Sogdian trading post at Suyab from the 5th or 6th century |
| 550s–744 | Türk conquest in the 550s; Xueyantuo overlordship to the mid-640s; Tang prefecture, 648; war with the Second Türks, 710 or 711 | Türk khaganates on the Orkhon | Western Türks; Suyab; Chigils |
| 744–840 | Uyghur defeat of the Kyrgyz, 758–760; self-declared qaghan and some twenty years of war from about 820 | Uyghur khaganate, 744–840 | Karluks take Suyab, c. 766; Chigils on Issyk-Kul |
| 840–c. 900 | Kyrgyz destroy the Uyghur khaganate; court moves south of Mount Lao (generally placed in the Tannu-Ola range); population stays in the Sayan and Minusinsk basins, with most Kyrgyz kurgans in Tuva | Pursuit of the Uyghurs; campaign against the Shiwei, c. 847–848; Kyrgyz cremations in the Altai begin; some Kyrgyz burials in Mongolia | Perhaps a Kyrgyz holding near Aksu (on one reading of al-Idrisi) |
| c. 900–1207 | The Khirkhiz country of the *Ḥudūd al-ʿĀlam* (982) and Gardizi (c. 1050); Qara Khitai frontier, c. 1130 | Altai cremations end in the 11th or 12th century; a Kyrgyz group living near Muslims, c. 1120 (al-Marvazi) | Karakhanids from the mid-10th century, Muslim from 960; Chigils at Barskan; Qara Khitai at Balasagun from the 1130s |
| 1207–1368 | Submission to the Mongols, 1207; revolt, 1218 or 1219; Yuan province; 1292–1293 campaign of Qubilai's general Tuq Tuqa and clearance/resettlement of households toward Manchuria (Zhaozhou) and Mongolia; *kyrgys* groups said to reach the Lena | Ariġ Böke's appanage; Qaidu's wars; Oirats as neighbours | Mongol conquest, 1217 or 1218; Chagatai ulus; 734 AH (1334–35) Talas mausoleum of Kenizek-Khatun; Moghulistan from the 1340s; Islam at the Moghul court, 1354 |
| 1368–c. 1500 | Principalities under Oirat overlordship; thin record | Kyrgyz dynasty (Ugechi-Kashka) over the Oirats of north-western Mongolia, from 1399 (end c. 1440 estimated); Oirat inroads into Moghulistan (Vais Khan, 1420s) | Kyrgyz reported retrospectively in Moghulistan from the first half to mid-15th century (~1433–1440 in *Xiyu zhi*; mid-15th c. in *Bahr al-asrar*); Kazakh Khanate, 1465–1466 |
| 1500–1700 | Russian contact, 1606; the "Kyrgyz Land" of principalities; Dzungar overlordship from 1666 | Altan Khans, overlords of the Yenisei Kyrgyz; Dzungar Khanate; Turkestani captives settled in Uvs, later the Khotons | Kyrgyz first recorded within living memory, 1503–04 (in *Tarikh-i Rashidi*, 1547); shared khans with the Kazakhs; Kyrgyz interventions in 17th-century Kashgaria and the Yarkand Khoja wars (to 1705); Dzungar pressure toward Fergana |
| 1700–1876 | Deportation to Dzungaria, 1703; Khakas take shape | Fuyu Kyrgyz moved to Manchuria, 1733 and 1755–1757 | Nominal Qing *fanshu* status and Kashgar/Ili frontier markets from 1758–59; Atake Biy's 1785–87 embassy to Russia; Kokand rule, 1825–1830; Russian annexation, 1855–1876 |

### Appendix B: Gazetteer of Key Places and Corridors

The argument moves across more than twenty basins, mountain passes, and oasis towns between southern Siberia, Manchuria, and the Pamirs. The table below orders the key places geographically from north-east to south-west.

**Table B.1: Key Basins, Mountain Passes, and Corridors (North-East to South-West)**

| Place or corridor | Modern location | Ecological setting | Role in the Kyrgyz story | Sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Minusinsk Basin (Abakan) & Kyzyl | Khakassia & Tuva, Russia | Steppe basins along the upper and middle Yenisei | Core of the medieval Yenisei Kyrgyz state to 1703; thickest northern runic inscriptions; homeland of the modern Khakas | [1] [2] [122] |
| Usinsk Basin & Kögmen (Sayan) Range | Krasnoyarsk Krai & Tuva, Russia | Mountain-basin corridor between Minusinsk and Tuva | Crossed by the Türks in 710–711; about 16 excavated post-840 Kyrgyz sites (campsites, cremation cemeteries, memorials) in use until the 1293 Yuan transfer toward Manchuria | [52] [61] [91] |
| Lake Khövsgöl & Shishkhid River | Northern Mongolia | Forest-steppe headwaters of the Yenisei | Early 13th-century homeland of the Oirats, neighbours of the Yenisei Kyrgyz from 1207 | [86] |
| Orkhon Valley (Ötüken) | Central Mongolia | Imperial steppe river valley | Seat of the Türk and Uyghur khaganates and later Mongol Karakorum; *not* settled by the Kyrgyz after 840 | [55] [66] [60] |
| Khyargas Nuur (Uvs Basin) | Uvs Province, north-western Mongolia | Closed salt-lake basin between the Altai and Hangai | Bears a Mongolian form of the Kyrgyz name; seat of the 17th-century Khotogoid Altan Khans and home of the Turkic-origin Khotons | [78] [105] [110] [111] |
| Chineta II & the Russian/Mongolian Altai | Altai Krai & Altai Republic, Russia | Alpine valleys and steppe foothills | 4 late-9th-c. Kyrgyz cremation kurgans and an 11th-c. intrusive burial in a Srostki mound; 12th–14th-c. inhumations; homeland of the Southern Altaians | [73] [74] [101] |
| Upper Irtysh & Dzungaria | East Kazakhstan & northern Xinjiang, China | Steppe corridor between the Altai and the Tian Shan | Medieval Kimak–Kipchak territory (first Kipchak linguistic stage); core of the 17th–18th-century Dzungar Khanate | [5] [122] |
| Beshbalyk & Turfan (Jushi) | Eastern Xinjiang, China | Oasis basins north and south of the eastern Tian Shan | Han and medieval geographic benchmark 5,000 *li* south of Jiankun; Uyghur kingdom after 840 | [36] [50] [98] |
| Almaliq (Ili Valley) | Ili Kazakh Autonomous Prefecture, Xinjiang | Broad river valley north of the central Tian Shan | Seasonal camp of Chagatai Khan and early capital of Moghulistan | [112] |
| Chüy Valley (Suyab / Ak-Beshim & Balasagun) | Northern Kyrgyzstan | Lowland river valley north of the Kyrgyz Ala-Too | Western Türk, Sogdian, Karluk, Karakhanid, and Qara Khitai capital; where Janibek and Kerey settled in 1459 | [39] [75] [127] |
| Talas Valley & Karkara | North-western & eastern Kyrgyzstan | Mountain-steppe valleys bordering Kazakh pastures | 8th–10th-century Western Türk/Karluk runic stones; 734 AH (1334–35) Chagatayid *kümböz* of Kenizek-Khatun later linked to Manas; zone of strongest Kazakh dialect contact | [12] [83] [165] [91] [166] |
| Issyk-Kul Basin & Barskoon (Barskan) | North-eastern Kyrgyzstan | High alpine lake basin (1,600 m) surrounded by 4,000 m peaks | Chigil stronghold (7th–11th c.) and birthplace of Mahmud al-Kashgari; battle site of 1514–33; held by the Kyrgyz Bugu tribe | [6] [15] [77] |
| Bedel Pass & Aksu (Barčūk) | Kyrgyzstan–Xinjiang border | High mountain pass linking Issyk-Kul to the northern Tarim rim | Ancient trade and vertical-transhumance corridor; site of a possible post-840 Kyrgyz holding in al-Idrisi's atlas and 17th-century Kyrgyz interventions in Kashgaria | [63] [69] [128] |
| Fergana Valley (Osh & Uzgen) & Pamir-Alay | Southern Kyrgyzstan, Uzbekistan, Tajikistan & Afghanistan | Irrigated oasis valley rimmed by high alpine pastures | Karakhanid and Chagatai urban centre; destination of 16th–18th-century Kyrgyz movements; core of the southern (*ichkilik*) dialect and Sufi conversion | [2] [20] [16] [119] |
| Fuyu County | Heilongjiang Province, north-east China | Manchurian plain | Home of the Fuyu Kyrgyz, deported from the Yenisei via Dzungaria in 1703–1757 and speaking a dialect of Khakas | [8] [9] |

### Appendix C: Reference Tables — Scholarly Reconstructions, Modern Groups, and Historical Exonyms

**Table C.1: Major Scholarly Reconstructions of Kyrgyz Ethnogenesis**

| Scholar | Where the Tian Shan Kyrgyz formed | When | Role of the Yenisei | Sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Valikhanov, Radlov, Karaev | Yenisei and Altai, then the Tian Shan | 9th–13th centuries | Medieval migrations | [5] |
| Bichurin, Aristov, Soltonoev | Tian Shan | From antiquity | Early Kyrgyz presence in the Tian Shan | [5] |
| V. V. Bartold | Tian Shan, on a local Turkic layer that included the Chigils and Karluks | Kyrgyz first named there in the 16th century | Brought the name, with Kipchak and South Siberian elements | [5] [78] |
| S. M. Abramzon | Eastern Tian Shan, from local Turkic tribes (Chigils, Karluks) and from Mongolic and Eastern Kipchak groups | 14th–17th centuries | No mass migration; the name more political than ethnic | [4] [6] |
| K. I. Petrov | Upper Yenisei and southern Altai, with Kimak–Kipchak and Mongol tribes | Chinggisid era onward | Real but mixed | [4] [5] |
| L. R. Kyzlasov | "Inner Asian Kyrgyz" between the Altai and the Khingan | Early Chinggisid era, then south | The name a political designation; denies Yenisei ancestry | [4] [5] |
| S. Soucek | Nomads arriving under Chinggisid rule, pushed by the Oirats | 13th–16th centuries | Yenisei Kyrgyz as a Turkic elite over Samoyed and Ostyak subjects | [4] |
| K. Menges | Tian Shan | After settlement | Kipchak character from close contact with the Kazakhs | [4] |
| A. Mokeev | Tian Shan | 15th–18th centuries | Kyrgyz moved into Moghulistan under Oirat (Kalmak) pressure | [5] |
| R. Abdumanapov | Western and southern Altai, then the Tian Shan | After 840; Tian Shan from the 15th century | Yenisei Kyrgyz over a Kimak–Kipchak majority | [5] [14] |

**Table C.2: Modern Kyrgyz-Named and Historically Related Groups**

| Group | Where | Self-name | Language | Relation to the Kyrgyz | Sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kyrgyz of Kyrgyzstan | Tian Shan and Pamir-Alay; about 5.2 million in 2023 (73.4% of 7.04 million) | Kyrgyz | Kyrgyz, a Kipchak language | The main population | [3] [8] [205] |
| Kyrgyz of Xinjiang | Kizilsu Kyrgyz Autonomous Prefecture, including Ulugqat County on the Kyrgyzstan border | Kyrgyz | Kyrgyz | Same people; nearly identical genetically ($F_{ST}$ genetic distance of just 0.13%) | [42] |
| Pamir Kyrgyz | Eastern Pamir (Murghab) in Tajikistan; Little and Great Pamir in Afghanistan; Ulupamir village, Van province, Turkey | Kyrgyz | Kyrgyz | Same people; in the Afghan Pamirs from the 16th century, with a wave from Osh in 1916 | [70] [125] [133] |
| Fuyu Kyrgyz | Fuyu County, Heilongjiang, China | Kyrgyz | Fuyu Kirghiz, a dialect of Khakas (South Siberian Turkic) | Descendants of Yenisei Kyrgyz moved east in the 18th century | [8] [9] |
| Khakas | Republic of Khakassia, Russia | *Tadar*, from Russian "Tatar" | Khakas, a South Siberian Turkic language | Population of the 17th-century Yenisei "Kyrgyz Land"; folklore keeps the name Kyrgyz | [1] [7] [8] |
| Southern Altaians | Altai Republic, Russia | Altai-kizhi (with Telengits and Teleuts) | Southern Altai | Closest linguistic relatives of the Kyrgyz | [8] [12] [13] |
| Sart-Kalmaks | Eastern Issyk-Kul, Kyrgyzstan | Kalmak | Kyrgyz; formerly an Oirat dialect | Oirat migrants of 1863–1894; many now register as Kyrgyz | [10] [11] |

### Appendix D: Evidence Scorecard — What Is Settled, Strongly Probable, and Open

This scorecard distils the ten central questions of the book into three epistemic tiers, separating claims that the combined historical, linguistic, archaeological, and genetic record settles from those that remain strongly probable or genuinely open.

**Table D.1: Evidence Scorecard Across Core Questions**

| Status | Core question | Verdict | Key sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Settled** | Did Russians invent or misapply the name "Kyrgyz" to the Tian Shan people? | **No.** Persian chronicles attest Kyrgyz in the Tian Shan by 1503–04 (written 1547), six decades before Russian contact on the Yenisei (1606). Russian clerks misapplied *Kirghiz* to the Kazakhs and coined *Kara-Kirghiz* for the real Kyrgyz. | [2] [14] [15] [17] [18] |
| **Settled** | Are the Xinjiang and Pamir Kyrgyz separate peoples from Kyrgyzstan's Kyrgyz? | **No.** One people sharing language, identity, and recent ancestry (Kyrgyzstan–Ulugqat $F_{ST} = 0.13\%$), divided by 19th–20th-century imperial borders. | [42] [70] [133] |
| **Settled** | Did the Yenisei Kyrgyz occupy Mongolia's Orkhon heartland after 840? | **No.** Their court moved south of Mount Lao (generally Tannu-Ola), fifteen days' ride short of the Orkhon, while their population and cemeteries stayed in the Minusinsk and Tuva basins. | [55] [61] [66] [60] |
| **Settled** | Did the Kyrgyz emerge from the Golden Horde alongside Kazakhs and Karakalpaks? | **No.** Parallel Mongol–Kipchak synthesis unfolded in Moghulistan and the Altai rather than the Jochid steppe. Kazakhs (51.9% C2, 6.5% R1a) and Karakalpaks (41% C2, 6% R1a) carry a Jochid C2 majority; Kyrgyz carry an R1a-Z2125 majority (39–63%) and retain Altai-shared linguistic traits. | [15] [12] [187] [175] [179] |
| **Settled** | Were western Mongolia's Khotons—or the Sakha or Lopnur—founders of the Kyrgyz? | **No.** Khotons are Dzungar-era Turkestani captives (82.5% bottlenecked R1a1); Sakha men are >80% N and Lopnur men 57.8% J2 (59.4% all-J). None forms a Yenisei–Tian Shan paternal bridge. | [110] [111] [197] [198] [199] |
| **Strongly Probable** | Was there a mass migration or conquering male elite straight from Minusinsk to Issyk-Kul? | **No.** The Yenisei polity lasted until 1703; genomic models for modern Kyrgyz do not require the Cis-Baikal Yeniseian ancestry shared by Khakas and Southern Altaians, and Kyrgyz carry almost no northern Q or N male lines and at most a modest, possibly female-biased Siberian input—fitting small staged movements absorbed via open clans (*uruu*) and exogamy (*jeti ata*). | [4] [42] [28] [104] [122] [129] |
| **Strongly Probable** | How did the Kyrgyz language take its present form? | **Staged contact shift.** An Altai–Sayan Turkic base (closest to Southern Altai and carrying 13th–14th-century Siberian-zone Mongolic loans) was reshaped by two waves of Kipchak contact (Irtysh–Altai, then Tian Shan–Fergana), with later oasis Perso-Arabic and Chagatai loans staying lexical rather than structural. | [5] [12] [13] [81] [95] [136] [138] |
| **Strongly Probable** | What became of the medieval Chigils and Karluks of the Tian Shan? | **Ecological split.** Sedentary oasis dwellers folded into Chagatai-speaking Uzbeks and Uyghurs, while high-pasture herders merged into incoming Kyrgyz clans—preserving older tribal names (*uruu*), likely supplying much of the local R1a-Z2125 base, and binding highland herders to oasis trade networks. | [6] [78] [38] [77] [104] [126] |
| **Open / Contested** | How large and how early was the Mongolic linguistic imprint? | **Scale and dominant era disputed.** Alimov counts over 3,000 Mongolic loanwords (with 16th–18th-century Oirat largest); Washington counts ~200 passing strict phonological tests (mostly Middle Mongol). Potential grammatical substrate effects await consolidated publication. | [95] [150] [147] |
| **Open / Contested** | What share of Tian Shan Kyrgyz ancestry came from pre-15th-century locals versus Altai/Yenisei newcomers? | **Unquantified without medieval ancient DNA.** Ancient genomes from 9th–11th-century Yenisei burials, 10th–14th-century Issyk-Kul Chigil/Karakhanid sites, and 13th–15th-century Altai/western Mongolia cemeteries—paired with *uruu*-stratified modern Y-DNA—are needed to partition local versus incoming R1a-Z2125 directly. | [28] [104] [175] [176] |

### Appendix E: Source Audit and Remaining Open Questions

Published scholarship and full-text primary verification have resolved almost all of the archival, linguistic, and genetic uncertainties flagged in earlier drafts—including the final Wusun missions (436–437 CE), the 1293 Yuan relocation of Yenisei Kyrgyz households toward Manchuria, Dashkovskiy's (2014) Russian full-text Chineta II kurgan dates, Abdumanapov's (2025) 15th-century Persian vs. Qing *Xiyu zhi* sourcing, Kyrgyz plural vs. accusative/genitive consonant assimilation after `-r` and `-y`, Alimov's (2024) complete *HUTAD* companion citation, primary Ket Y-DNA sourcing (Karafet et al. 2002 via Tambets et al. 2004, Table 3), Khoton vs. Sart-Kalmak Y-DNA (Katoh et al. 2005 vs. Balinova et al. 2019), the peer-reviewed PNAS publication of Askapuli et al. (2026), and primary epigraphic documentation of the 734 AH Talas mausoleum. Several genuine empirical, archival, or paywall limits remain open in the available published record (Table E.1).

**Table E.1: Remaining Open Archival, Archaeological, and Paywalled Questions**

| Open item | Current status in the published record | Where it arises | Relevant sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Exact archaeological site of **Mount Lao (Duman)** | Standard scholarship identifies Mount Lao with the Tannu-Ola range south of the upper Yenisei; through 2026, recent fieldwork in central Tuva and the Usinsk Basin remains funerary rather than palatial, and no excavated post-840 royal court site has been identified. | Ch. 3.4, Ch. 4.1 | [45] [60] |
| Earliest dated attestation of **Khyargas Nuur** | Absent from surviving medieval chronicles. Earliest dated map attestation identified so far is *Kirgis noor* on the **1766** Manchu Qianlong imperial atlas (British Library IOR/X/3265); earlier 18th-century Russian/European maps (e.g., Renat 1716–33; Unkovsky 1722–24) remain to be checked. | Ch. 5.2 | [78] [105] [107] |
| Radiocarbon inventory of **western Mongolia burials** and missing **ancient DNA horizons** | No site-by-site radiocarbon inventory of medieval Kyrgyz burials in western Mongolia is published. Standard 9th–11th-century Yenisei Kyrgyz graves are cremations unsuitable for DNA recovery; 10th–14th-century Issyk-Kul nomadic Chigil/Karakhanid and 13th–15th-century Altai/western Mongolia ancient genomes remain unsequenced. | Ch. 5.2, Ch. 10.2, Ch. 11.2 | [28] [44] [58] [104] [109] |
| Paywalled full text of **Wen et al. (2022)** | The published abstract directly confirms $n = 298$ Xinjiang Kyrgyz and 13th–14th-century TMRCAs for leading lineages R1a-Z2125 and C2-F3796, while finer secondary-summary figures (39%, 630–730 BP / 680 BP, exact BP baseline 1950 vs. ~2021) and full C2/O/Q/N counts remain behind Springer's paywall. | Ch. 10.1, Ch. 10.2 | [104] |
| **Six print-only, paywalled, or abstract-only source checks** | Direct physical or full-text checks remain open for: (1) *MIKK* Vol. 2 (2003), p. 200 (*Xiyu zhi* 330- vs. 220-year pages); (2) Minorsky (1942), p. 30 and commentary (for the Muslim-neighbour burial clause absent from ʿAwfi's 13th-century abridgement); (3) Malyarchuk et al. (2013), Table 1 (paywalled; note 2013 "C3" = modern C2); (4) Liu et al. (2018), visual Table 1 for non-J Lopnur clades (all-J 59.4% and J2 57.8% confirmed from English abstract); (5) Sapalova (2010), paid Dissercat full text; and (6) Todoriki (2025), published as a PIAC conference abstract only. | Ch. 4.2, Ch. 5.1, Ch. 7.1, Ch. 10.3 | [14] [76] [93] [94] [195] [197] |
| Calibration of the **Chineta II** radiocarbon dates | Dashkovskiy (2014) reports 1300 ± 45 BP (kurgan 10) and 1100 ± 70 BP (kurgan 12). If these are uncalibrated ages, the first falls largely before 840 on calibration and sits awkwardly with the typological Yakonur dating (c. 840–950); published calibrated ranges or a re-dating are needed. | Ch. 4.2 | [73] |
| Cohort of the **Chen et al. (2025)** Y-chromosome count | The "13 of 16 men" carrying haplogroup R should be checked against the paper to confirm whether they belong to the Ulugqat cohort or the 16-person Kyrgyzstan sample, and which R branches they carry. | Ch. 10.2 | [42] |
| **Wells et al. (2001)** Kyrgyz frequency | The article is open access (PMC56946); the 63.5% R1a1-M17 figure for the Kyrgyz sample ($n = 52$) has not yet been re-read against its table. | Ch. 10.2 | [177] |
| Human ancestry results of **Spyrou et al. (2022)** | Genome-wide data from individuals buried in 1338–1339 at Kara-Djigach and Burana were generated alongside the plague genomes; their ancestry results have not yet been integrated into Chapter 10. | Ch. 10.3, Ch. 11.2 | [203] |
| Standard-edition checks for translations read via the **Kyrgyz American Foundation** | Gardizi, Rashid al-Din, Savinov, Bartold (on Kyrgyz-Nor), Ushnitsky, Ugdyzhekov, and Khudyakov are read in translations posted by the foundation. Key quotations should be checked against standard scholarly editions, such as A. P. Martinez's (1982) translation of Gardizi's chapters on the Turks and W. M. Thackston's (1998–99) translation of Rashid al-Din. | Ch. 3–6 | [44] [50] [65] [78] [87] [106] [121] |
| Scholarly source for the **kümböz** typology | The statement that Kyrgyz domed mausoleums were modelled on Karakhanid and Chagatayid shrines currently rests on Open.kg's overview; a scholarly architectural source should support or replace it. | Ch. 9.2 | [165] |
